A groupoid approach to regular ∗ * -semigroups
James East, P.A. Azeef Muhammed

Abstract
In this paper we develop a new groupoid-based structure theory for the class of regular ∗ * -semigroups. This class occupies something of a ‘sweet spot’ between the important classes of inverse and regular semigroups, and contains many natural examples. Some of the most significant families include the partition, Brauer and Temperley-Lieb monoids, among other diagram monoids. Our main result is that the category of regular ∗ * -semigroups is isomorphic to the category of so-called ‘chained projection groupoids’. Such a groupoid is in fact a triple ( P , 𝒢 , ε ) (P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon) , where: • P P is a projection algebra (in the sense of Imaoka and Jones), • 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is an ordered groupoid with object set P P , and • ε : 𝒞 → 𝒢 \varepsilon:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G} is a special functor, where 𝒞 \mathscr{C} is a certain natural ‘chain groupoid’ constructed from P P . Roughly speaking: the groupoid 𝒢 = 𝒢 ⁡ ( S ) \mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S) remembers only the ‘easy’ products in a regular ∗ * -semigroup S S ; the projection algebra P = P ⁡ ( S ) P=P(S) remembers only the ‘conjugation action’ of the projections of S S ; and the functor ε = ε ⁡ ( S ) \varepsilon=\varepsilon(S) tells us how 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and P P ‘fit together’ in order to recover the entire structure of S S . In this way, we obtain the first completely general structure theorem for regular ∗ * -semigroups. As a consequence of our main result, we give a new proof of the celebrated Ehresmann–Schein–Nambooripad Theorem, which establishes an isomorphism between the categories of inverse semigroups and inductive groupoids. Other applications will be given in future works. We consider several examples along the way, and pose a number of problems that we believe are worthy of further attention. Keywords : Regular ∗ * -semigroups; inverse semigroups; groupoids; projection algebras; partition monoids. MSC: 20M10, 20M50, 18B40, 20M17, 20M20, 20M05.



1
Introduction

The
ESN Theorem
is arguably the most important result in inverse semigroup theory:

Ehresmann–Schein–Nambooripad Theorem
.

The category
{\bf IS}
of inverse semigroups (with semigroup homomorphisms) is isomorphic to the category
{\bf IG}
of inductive groupoids (with inductive functors).

This result was first formulated as above by Lawson in
[
88
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, who named it after the three mathematicians who had contributed most to its development. The key motivation for the theorem is that it ties together the two main approaches to partial symmetries
that were not captured by groups: the
inductive groupoids
of Charles Ehresmann
[
43
,
44
]
, and the
inverse semigroups
of Viktor Wagner
[
147
,
148
]
and Gordon Preston
[
126
,
127
]
. A fuller account of the historical background can be found in
[
88
,
68
,
67
]
; see also
[
108
]
.

The exact nature of the isomorphism in the ESN Theorem reveals a lot more than is evident in the above statement. Not only are the
categories

{\bf IS}
and
{\bf IG}
‘the same’, but in fact the
objects
of these categories are, too: inverse semigroups
are
inductive groupoids, and conversely. More formally, an inverse semigroup
S
can be identified with the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
with:

•

object/identity set
E(S)
, the semilattice of idempotents of
S
, and

•

morphism set
S
, where a semigroup element
a
is thought of as a morphism
aa^{-1}\to a^{-1}a
.

Together with a natural order, this is enough to recover the entire structure of
S
. At the morphism level, inverse semigroup homomorphisms
S\to S^{\prime}
and inductive functors
\mathcal{G}(S)\to\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
are precisely the same maps.
We will give more detailed information (including a new proof of the theorem) in Section
9
, but for now it is worth noting that the (partial) composition in
\mathcal{G}(S)
is a restriction of the (total) product in
S
. Roughly speaking, the groupoid ‘remembers’ only the ‘easy’ products in the semigroup. This is particularly useful in defining inverse semigroups (from inductive groupoids) or inverse semigroup homomorphisms (from inductive functors).

The ESN Theorem has had a major impact outside of (inverse) semigroup theory, with one particularly fruitful direction being the application of inverse semigroups to C
∗
-algebras. For example, the articles
[
121
,
95
,
96
,
84
,
15
,
109
,
81
,
45
,
90
,
17
]
display a mixture of semigroup-theoretic and groupoid-based approaches. For more extensive discussions, and many more references, see Lawson’s survey
[
91
]
and Paterson’s monograph
[
120
]
. See also

[
139
,
140
,
138
]

for applications to representation theory.

Another way to measure the influence of the ESN Theorem is to consider its extensions and generalisations. When faced with an important class of semigroups, one naturally looks for an ‘ESN-type link’ with an equally-natural class of categories, and vice versa. Thus, we have ESN-type theorems for regular semigroups, restriction semigroups, Ehresmann semigroups, concordant semigroups, DRC semigroups, and others; see for example
[
152
,
89
,
20
,
48
,
54
,
47
,
2
,
150
,
149
,
114
,
87
,
92
,
67
,
53
,
52
,
66
,
151
,
49
,
141
,
143
,
142
]
. As with several ‘dualities’ in mathematics
[
18
,
144
,
11
,
125
,
124
,
128
,
129
]
, such correspondences allow problems in one field to be translated into another, where they can hopefully be solved, and the solution then reinterpreted in the original context.
1
1

1

We note, however, that in these dualities morphisms are reversed when moving from one category to the other, whereas the directions are consistent in the ESN Theorem and its variations just cited.

Arguably the most significant of the papers just cited was Nambooripad’s 1979 memoir
[
114
]
. As well as pioneering the categorical approach (which led to the ‘N’ in ‘ESN’), this paper majorly extended the ESN Theorem to the class of
regular
semigroups. These are semigroups in which every element has
at least one
inverse, rather than a unique one as for inverse semigroups. Dropping the uniqueness restriction on inverses leads to a far more general class of semigroups, which contains many additional natural examples, including semigroups of mappings, linear transformations, and more. The increase in generality of the semigroups led inevitably to an increase in the complexity of the categorical structures modelling them. Although Nambooripad still represents a regular semigroup by an ordered groupoid, the groupoid is not enough to completely recover the semigroup; an additional layer of structure is required. The need for this extra data is due to the fact that the object set of such a groupoid is not a semilattice (as for inverse semigroups), but instead a
(regular) biordered set
: a partial algebra with a pair of intertwined pre-orders satisfying a fairly complex set of axioms. The increase in generality also led to the sacrifice of the category
isomorphism
. Nambooripad’s main result,
[
114
, Theorem 4.14]
, is that the category of regular semigroups is
equivalent
to a certain category of groupoids (which Nambooripad also called
inductive
).

One way to understand this sacrifice is via the
loss of symmetry
when moving from inverse to regular semigroups. As noted above, inverse semigroups were devised to model partial symmetries that were unrecognisable by groups. This is formalised in the Wagner–Preston Theorem, which states that any inverse semigroup is (isomorphic to) a semigroup of partial symmetries of some mathematical structure; see
[
88
, Theorem 1.5.1]
. But an inverse semigroup is itself an extremely symmetrical mathematical structure in its own right, so much so that the canonical proof of the Wagner–Preston Theorem involves showing that an inverse semigroup is (isomorphic to) a semigroup of partial symmetries of
itself
.

The above is not true of arbitrary regular semigroups, however, as any regular semigroup representable by partial symmetries is necessarily inverse. In a certain sense, an over-abundance of inverses leads to an over-abundance of idempotents, and then to ambiguity in identifying the domain and range of a semigroup element/morphism, hence the necessity of Nambooripad’s more elaborate groupoids.

The current article concerns a class of semigroups occupying something of a ‘sweet spot’ between inverse and regular semigroups, the so-called
regular
*
-semigroups
of Nordahl and Scheiblich
[
119
]
. These are semigroups with an additional unary operation
a\mapsto a^{*}
satisfying the laws:

(a^{*})^{*}=a=aa^{*}a\and(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}.

The class of regular
*
-semigroups has received a great deal of attention in recent years, partly because of the prototypical examples of the so-called
diagram semigroups
. These semigroups include families such as the Brauer, Temperley-Lieb, Kauffman and partition monoids, and have their origins and applications in a wide range of mathematical and scientific disciplines, from representation theory, low-dimensional topology, statistical mechanics, and many more; see for example
[
40
,
86
,
50
,
82
,
5
,
4
,
3
,
83
,
12
,
77
,
76
,
78
,
102
,
101
,
104
,
38
,
42
,
39
,
41
,
99
,
106
,
153
,
94
,
62
,
154
,
9
,
10
,
93
,
23
,
22
,
105
,
103
,
13
,
145
,
79
,
80
,
75
,
35
]
. These monoids have provided a strong bridge between semigroup theory and these other disciplines; for a fuller discussion of this fruitful dialogue, and for many more references, see the introduction to
[
35
]
.

While an element
a
of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
might not have a
unique
inverse, it certainly has a
distinguished
inverse, namely
a^{*}
. This resolves the above ‘ambiguity’, and allows one to define a groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
in entirely the same way as for inverse semigroups, where now we think of
a
as a morphism
aa^{*}\to a^{*}a
. While these domain/range idempotents do not generally account for
all
the idempotents of
S
, they are precisely the so-called
projections
, i.e. the elements
p\in S
satisfying
p^{2}=p=p^{*}
. The set
P=P(S)
of all such projections forms a (unary)
projection algebra
, as originally formulated by Imaoka
[
72
]
, albeit under a different name. As we will see, there are important algebraic and order-theoretic ties between the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and the algebra
P
, resulting in what we will call an
(abstract) projection groupoid

(P,\mathcal{G})
. An additional link comes in the form of a certain functor
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S):\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
is the so-called
chain groupoid
of
P
; this groupoid, which will be defined later, bears some resemblance to Nambooripad’s groupoid of
E
-chains

[
114
]
. One consequence of the current work is that the triple
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, which we will call a
chained projection groupoid
, contains enough information to completely recover the structure of
S
.
We go much further than this, however, and prove the following:

Main Theorem

(see Theorem
8.1
)
.

The category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups (with
*
-semigroup homomorphisms) is isomorphic to the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids (with chained projection functors).

The definition of some of the terms in the above theorem will be deferred until later sections. For now we will give a brief outline of the structure of the paper.
Section
2
contains preliminary material and results on semigroups and (small) categories.
Regular
*
-semigroups are introduced in Section
3
, where among other things we construct a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf OG}
into the category of ordered groupoids (see Proposition
3.27
).
Section
4
concerns the category
{\bf PA}
of projection algebras; we again construct a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
(Proposition
4.25
), and give a new proof of the known fact
[
72
]
that every (abstract) projection algebra is the algebra of projections of some regular
*
-semigroup (Proposition
4.26
).
Section
5
builds on Section
4
, and introduces the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}(P)
associated to a projection algebra
P
; this again leads to a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf OG}
(Remark
5.19
).
The above ideas are all brought together in Section
6
, where we introduce the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids, and construct a functor
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
(Theorem
6.42
).
In Section
7
we construct a functor
{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
in the reverse direction (Theorem
7.14
).
We then prove our main result (Theorem
8.1
) in Section
8
, by showing that the functors
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
are mutual inverses.
We return to the ESN Theorem in Section
9
, where we give a new proof as an application of our main result.
We conclude by discussing future directions and open problems in Section
10
.
Several examples are considered throughout the text, in order to provide concrete cases of our abstract constructions, and to highlight some of the subtleties that arise.

Acknowledgement.

We are very grateful to an anonymous referee for a number of insightful questions and comments that led to substantial improvements.

2
Preliminaries

We now gather the preliminary definitions and basic results we require in the rest of the paper. We cover semigroups in Section
2.1
, and (ordered) categories and groupoids in Section
2.2
.
Much of what we present here can be found in standard texts, but we include it to establish notation and conventions, and to keep the paper as self-contained as possible.
For further general background, we refer the reader to texts such as
[
69
,
19
]
for semigroups,
[
88
]
for inverse semigroups,
[
97
]
for categories, and
[
16
]
for universal algebra, though the latter is only used tangentially.

2.1
Semigroups

A
semigroup
is a set with an associative binary operation, which will typically be denoted by juxtaposition. A
monoid
is a semigroup with an identity element. As usual we write
S^{1}
for the
monoid completion
of the semigroup
S
. So
S^{1}=S
if
S
happens to be a monoid; otherwise,
S^{1}=S\cup\{1\}
, where
1
is a symbol not belonging to
S
, acting as an adjoined identity element.

Green’s relations
are five equivalence relations, defined on an arbitrary semigroup
S
as follows
[
57
]
. First, for
a,b\in S
we have

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ aS^{1}=bS^{1},\hskip 20.00003pta\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}a=S^{1}b,\hskip 20.00003pta\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}aS^{1}=S^{1}bS^{1}.

Note that
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b
precisely when either
a=b
or else
a=bx
and
b=ay
for some
x,y\in S
; similar comments apply to
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}
. The remaining two of Green’s relations are defined by

{\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\cap{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}\and{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\vee{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}},

where the latter is the join in the lattice of equivalences on
S
, i.e. the least equivalence containing
{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\cup{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}
. It is well known that
{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\circ{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}\circ{\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}
, and that
{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}
if
S
is finite. We denote the
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-class of
a\in S
by

R_{a}=\{b\in S:a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\},

and similarly for
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-classes
L_{a}
, and so on.

An element
a
of a semigroup
S
is
regular
if
a=axa
for some
x\in S
. The element
y=xax
then has the property that
a=aya
and
y=yay
; such an element
y
is called an
inverse
of
x
. We say
S
is
regular
if every element of
S
is regular. The set of
idempotents
of
S
is denoted

E(S)=\{e\in S:e=e^{2}\}.

Idempotents are of course regular. An
inverse semigroup
is a semigroup in which every element has a unique inverse. It is well known that
S
is inverse if and only if it is regular and its idempotents commute; in this case,
E(S)
is a
semilattice
, i.e. a semigroup of commuting idempotents.

In this paper we are concerned with a class of semigroups contained strictly between regular and inverse semigroups, the so-called
regular
*
-semigroups
of Nordahl and Scheiblich
[
119
]
. We saw their definition in Section
1
, and we will return to them in Section
3
.

2.2
Categories

Broadly speaking, we consider two types of categories in the current paper:

•

large categories whose objects are algebraic structures, and whose morphisms are structure-preserving mappings (e.g., the category of all semigroups with semigroup homomorphisms),

•

small categories that are thought of as algebraic structures in their own right.

Categories of the first kind are treated in a completely standard way (see for example
[
97
]
). In the current section we explain how we will work with categories of the second kind.

We typically identify a small category
\mathcal{C}
with its set of morphisms. We identify the objects of
\mathcal{C}
with the identities, the set of which is denoted
v\mathcal{C}
. We denote the domain and codomain (a.k.a. range) of
a\in\mathcal{C}
by
{\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a)
, respectively. We compose morphisms left to right, so
a\circ b
is defined if and only if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, in which case
{\bf d}(a\circ b)={\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a\circ b)={\bf r}(b)
. (Often functors will be written to the left of their arguments, and so composed right to left; it should always be clear which convention is being used.) For
p,q\in v\mathcal{C}
, we write

\mathcal{C}(p,q)=\{a\in\mathcal{C}:{\bf d}(a)=p,\ {\bf r}(a)=q\}

for the set of all morphisms
p\to q
.

All the small categories we study will have an involution and an order, as made precise in the next two standard definitions.

Definition 2.1
.

A
*
-category
3
3

3

These are often called
dagger-
or
\dagger
-
categories
in the literature, and the involution denoted
a\mapsto a^{\dagger}
. See for example
[
64
]
or
[
146
, Section 9.7]
.
is a small category
\mathcal{C}
with an involution, i.e. a map
{\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}:a\mapsto a^{*}}
satisfying the following, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
:

(I1)

{\bf d}(a^{*})={\bf r}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a^{*})={\bf d}(a)
.

(I2)

(a^{*})^{*}=a
.

(I3)

If
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
(a\circ b)^{*}=b^{*}\circ a^{*}
.

A
groupoid
is a
*
-category for which we additionally have:

(I4)

a\circ a^{*}={\bf d}(a)
(and hence also
a^{*}\circ a={\bf r}(a)
) for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

In a groupoid, we typically write
a^{*}=a^{-1}
for
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

It is easy to show that
p^{*}=p
for all
p\in v\mathcal{C}
, when
\mathcal{C}
is a
*
-category. It is also clear that
(I1)
is a consequence of
(I4)
, so a groupoid is a category with a map
a\mapsto a^{*}
satisfying
(I2)
–
(I4)
.

Definition 2.2
.

An
ordered
*
-category
is a
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a partial order
\leq
satisfying the following, for all
a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\in v\mathcal{C}
:

(O1)

If
a\leq b
, then
{\bf d}(a)\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a)\leq{\bf r}(b)
.

(O2)

If
a\leq b
, then
a^{*}\leq b^{*}
.

(O3)

If
a\leq b
and
c\leq d
, and if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(c)
and
{\bf r}(b)={\bf d}(d)
, then
a\circ c\leq b\circ d
.

(O4)

For all
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, there exists a unique
u\leq a
with
{\bf d}(u)=p
.

It is easy to see that
(O1)
–
(O4)
imply the following, which is a dual of
(O4)
:

(O4)
enumi

For all
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, there exists a unique
v\leq a
with
{\bf r}(v)=q
.

(Here we have
q\leq{\bf d}(a^{*})
, and if
w
is the unique element with
w\leq a^{*}
with
{\bf d}(w)=q
, then we take
v=w^{*}
.)

The elements
u
and
v
in
(O4)
and
(O4)enumi
are denoted
u={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
and
v=a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
, respectively, and called the
left restriction of
a
to
p
and the
right restriction of
a
to
q
. Some authors call
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
a
restriction
, and
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
a
co-restriction
; we prefer the left/right terminology, however, as it does not ‘prioritise’ one over the other.

An
ordered groupoid
is a groupoid with a partial order satisfying
(O1)
–
(O4)
. In fact, when
\mathcal{C}
is a groupoid,
(O2)
,
(O3)
and
(I4)
together imply
(O1)
.

It is easy to see that the object set
v\mathcal{C}
is an
order ideal
in any ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
, meaning that the following holds:

•

For all
a\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\in v\mathcal{C}
,
a\leq p\ \Rightarrow\ a\in v\mathcal{C}
.

Indeed, if
a\leq p
, and if we write
q={\bf d}(a)\in v\mathcal{C}
, then by
(O1)
we have
q\leq{\bf d}(p)=p
. But then
a,q\leq p
and
{\bf d}(a)=q={\bf d}(q)
, so by uniqueness in
(O4)
we have
a=q\in v\mathcal{C}
. It also follows from this that
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}p=q
for any
q\leq p
. We typically use facts such as these without explicit reference.

In what follows, it is typically more convenient to construct an ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
by:

•

defining an order
\leq
on the object set
v\mathcal{C}
,

•

defining left restrictions
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
, for
a\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
,

•

specifying that
a\leq b
(for morphisms
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
) when
a
is a restriction of
b
.

The next lemma axiomatises the conditions required to ensure that we do indeed obtain an ordered
*
-category in this way.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a
*
-category for which the following two conditions hold:

(i)

There is a partial order
\leq
on the object set
v\mathcal{C}
.

(ii)

For all
a\in\mathcal{C}
, and for all
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, there exists a morphism
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\in\mathcal{C}
, such that the following hold, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
and
p,q\in v\mathcal{C}
:

(O1)
′

If
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, then
{\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)=p
and
{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\leq{\bf r}(a)
.

(O2)
′

If
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, and if
q={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
, then
({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)^{*}={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*}
.

(O3)
′

{}_{{\bf d}(a)}{\downharpoonleft}a=a
.

(O4)
′

For all
p\leq q\leq{\bf d}(a)
, we have
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
.

(O5)
′

If
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and if
q={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
, then
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(a\circ b)={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\circ{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}b
.

Then
\mathcal{C}
is an ordered
*
-category with order given by

a\leq b\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pta={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $p\leq{\bf d}(b)$.}

(2.4)

Moreover, any ordered
*
-category has the above form.

Proof.

Beginning with the final assertion, suppose
\mathcal{C}
is an ordered
*
-category. Then
v\mathcal{C}
is a sub-poset of
\mathcal{C}
, and hence
(i)
holds. For
(ii)
, we take
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
to be the morphism
u\leq a
from
(O4)
, and
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
are all easily checked. For example, to verify
(ii)(O2)′
, suppose
a\in\mathcal{C}
and
{p\leq{\bf d}(a)}
, and let
q={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
. Then since
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\leq a
, it follows from
(O2)
that
({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)^{*}\leq a^{*}
, and we have
{\bf d}(({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)^{*})={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)=q
. But
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*}
is the unique element below
a^{*}
with domain
q
, so in fact
{({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)^{*}={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*}}
.

Conversely, suppose conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
both hold.
We first check that the relation
\leq
in (
2.4
) is a partial order. Reflexivity follows immediately from
(ii)(O3)′
, and transitivity from
(ii)(O4)′
. For anti-symmetry, suppose
a\leq b
and
b\leq a
, so that
a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
and
b={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a
for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
q\leq{\bf d}(a)
. Then

a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt{\bf d}(a)={\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)=p\leq{\bf d}(b)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf d}(b)=q\leq{\bf d}(a).

It follows that
p=q={\bf d}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and so
a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b={}_{{\bf d}(b)}{\downharpoonleft}b=b
. Now that we know
\leq
is a partial order, we verify conditions
(O1)
–
(O4)
.

(O1)
and
(O2)
. Suppose
a\leq b
, so that
a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
. Then
(ii)(O1)′
gives

{\bf d}(a)={\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b)=p\leq{\bf d}(b)\and{\bf r}(a)={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b)\leq{\bf r}(b).

We also have
a^{*}=({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b)^{*}={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}b^{*}
by
(ii)(O2)′
, where
q={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b)
, so that
a^{*}\leq b^{*}
.

(O3)
. Suppose
a\leq b
and
c\leq d
are such that
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(c)
and
{\bf r}(b)={\bf d}(d)
. So
a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
and
c={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}d
for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
q\leq{\bf d}(d)
. Since

q={\bf d}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}d)={\bf d}(c)={\bf r}(a)={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b),

it follows from
(ii)(O5)′
that
a\circ c={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}d={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(b\circ d)
, so that
a\circ c\leq b\circ d
.

(O4)
. Given
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, we certainly have
u\leq a
and
{\bf d}(u)=p
, where
u={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
. For uniqueness, suppose also that
x\leq a
for some
x\in\mathcal{C}
with
{\bf d}(x)=p
. Since
x\leq a
, we have
x={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a
for some
q\leq{\bf d}(a)
. But then
q={\bf d}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf d}(x)=p
, so in fact
x={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a=u
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

The previous result referred only to left restrictions. Right restrictions can be defined in terms of left restrictions and the involution:

a{\downharpoonright}_{q}=({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*})^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a\in\mathcal{C}$ and $q\leq{\bf r}(a)$.}

As explained in Definition
2.2
,
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
is the unique element below
a
with codomain
q
. In particular, the following holds in any ordered
*
-category:

(O6)
′

If
a\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, and if
q={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
, then
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a=a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
.

Each of
(ii)(O1)′
–
(O6)′
of course have duals. For example, the dual of
(ii)(O5)′
says:

•

If
q\leq{\bf r}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and if
p={\bf d}(b{\downharpoonright}_{q})
, then
(a\circ b){\downharpoonright}_{q}=a{\downharpoonright}_{p}\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{q}
.

It is also worth noting that for
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
we have

\displaystyle a\leq b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b

for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a=b{\downharpoonright}_{q}

for some
q\leq{\bf r}(b)

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{q}

for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
q\leq{\bf r}(b)
.

The
p,q\in P
here are of course
p={\bf d}(a)
and
q={\bf r}(a)
.

Definition 2.6
.

A
v
-congruence
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence relation
\approx
on
\mathcal{C}
satisfying the following, for all
a,b,u,v\in\mathcal{C}
:

(C1)

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ [{\bf d}(a)={\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a)={\bf r}(b)]
,

(C2)

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ u\circ a\approx u\circ b
, whenever the stated compositions are defined,

(C3)

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ a\circ v\approx b\circ v
, whenever the stated compositions are defined.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a
*
-category, we say that
\approx
is a
*
-congruence
if it satisfies
(C1)
–
(C3)
and the following, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
:

(C4)

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ a^{*}\approx b^{*}
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is an ordered
*
-category, we say that
\approx
is an
ordered
*
-congruence
if it satisfies
(C1)
–
(C4)
and the following, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
:

(C5)

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ {}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
for all
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
.

Given a
v
-congruence
\approx
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
, the quotient category
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
consists of all
\approx
-classes, under the induced composition. We typically write
[a]
for the
\approx
-class of
a\in\mathcal{C}
. It follows immediately from
(C1)
that
p\approx q\ \Rightarrow\ p=q
for objects
p,q\in v\mathcal{C}
, so we can identify the object sets of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
,
viz.

p\equiv[p]
. In this way, for
a\in\mathcal{C}
we have
{\bf d}[a]={\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}[a]={\bf r}(a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
, and

[a]\circ[b]=[a\circ b]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{whenever ${\bf r}[a]={\bf d}[b]$.}

Lemma 2.7
.

(i)

If
\approx
is a
*
-congruence on a
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
is a
*
-category, with involution given by

[a]^{*}=[a^{*}]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a\in\mathcal{C}$.}

(2.8)

If also
a\circ a^{*}\approx{\bf d}(a)
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
is a groupoid.

(ii)

If
\approx
is an ordered
*
-congruence on an ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
is an ordered
*
-category, with involution (
2.8
), and order given by

\alpha\leq\beta\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a\leq b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $a\in\alpha$ and $b\in\beta$.}

(2.9)

Proof.

Part
(i)
is routine, and
(ii)
only a little more difficult, so we just give a sketch for the latter. For this, one begins by showing that
\alpha\leq\beta
is equivalent to the ostensibly stronger condition:

•

For all
b\in\beta
, there exists
a\in\alpha
such that
a\leq b
.

It is then easy to check that
\leq
is indeed a partial order on
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
. Conditions
(O1)
–
(O4)
for
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
follow quickly from the corresponding conditions for
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

We will shortly return to congruences in Lemma
2.16
below, where we give simpler criteria for checking that a
v
-congruence satisfies
(C4)
or
(C5)
. The proof of the lemma will require certain maps defined on any ordered
*
-category. These maps will also be used extensively in the rest of the paper. To define them, fix some such ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
with object set
P=v\mathcal{C}
. For
p\in P
, we write

p^{\downarrow}=\{q\in P:q\leq p\}

for the down-set of
p
in the poset
(P,\leq)
. Consider a morphism
a\in\mathcal{C}
. A restriction
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
is defined precisely when
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, and we write
p\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
for the codomain of
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
; by
(ii)(O1)′
, we have
p\vartheta_{a}\leq{\bf r}(a)
. In other words, we have a map

\vartheta_{a}:{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003ptp\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a).

(2.10)

Note that for
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, we have

{\bf d}(a{\downharpoonright}_{q})={\bf d}(({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*})^{*})={\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*})=q\vartheta_{a^{*}}.

(2.11)

Lemma 2.12
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is an ordered
*
-category, then for any
a\in\mathcal{C}
,

\vartheta_{a}:{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}\and\vartheta_{a^{*}}:{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}

are mutually inverse order-isomorphisms.

Proof.

By symmetry, it is enough to show that

(i)

\vartheta_{a}
is order-preserving, in the sense that
p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ p\vartheta_{a}\leq q\vartheta_{a}
for all
p,q\in{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}
, and

(ii)

\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{*}}=\operatorname{id}_{{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}}
.

(i)
. If
p\leq q\leq{\bf d}(a)
, then
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a\leq{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a
, and so
{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\leq{\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a)
, i.e.
p\vartheta_{a}\leq q\vartheta_{a}
.

(ii)
. Let
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, and write
q=p\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)
. By
(O6)′
we have
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a=a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
. Combining this with
(ii)(O1)′
and (
2.11
), we deduce that
p={\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf d}(a{\downharpoonright}_{q})=q\vartheta_{a^{*}}=p\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{*}}
, as required.
∎

Note that property
(ii)(O5)′
and its dual (cf. Remark
2.5
) can be rephrased in terms of the
\vartheta
maps. Specifically, if
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
(an ordered
*
-category) are such that
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then (keeping (
2.11
) and Lemma
2.12
in mind) we have

{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(a\circ b)={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\circ{}_{p\vartheta_{a}}{\downharpoonleft}b\and(a\circ b){\downharpoonright}_{q}=a{\downharpoonright}_{q\vartheta_{b}^{-1}}\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p\leq{\bf d}(a)$ and $q\leq{\bf r}(b)$.}

(2.13)

This can be extended to restrictions of compositions with an arbitrary number of terms. In a sense, the first part of the next result is a formalisation of the previous observation.
Throughout the paper we write
f|_{A}
for the (set-theoretic) restriction of a function
f
to a subset
A
of its domain.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an ordered
*
-category, and let
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\in v\mathcal{C}
.

(i)

If
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
\vartheta_{a\circ b}=\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{b}
.

(ii)

\vartheta_{p}=\operatorname{id}_{p^{\downarrow}}
.

(iii)

If
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, then
\vartheta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}=\vartheta_{a}|_{p^{\downarrow}}
.

Proof.

(i)
. Write
p={\bf d}(a)
,
q={\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
and
r={\bf r}(b)
. Note that

\vartheta_{a}:p^{\downarrow}\to q^{\downarrow},\hskip 20.00003pt\vartheta_{b}:q^{\downarrow}\to r^{\downarrow}\and\vartheta_{a\circ b}:p^{\downarrow}\to r^{\downarrow},

so that
\vartheta_{a\circ b}
and
\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{b}
are defined on the same domain, i.e. on
p^{\downarrow}
. Now let
s\leq p
, and put
t=s\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}a)
. Then by
(ii)(O5)′
we have

s\vartheta_{a\circ b}={\bf r}({}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}(a\circ b))={\bf r}({}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}a\circ{}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}b)={\bf r}({}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}b)=t\vartheta_{b}=s\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{b}.

(ii)
. By part
(i)
, we have
\vartheta_{p}=\vartheta_{p\circ p}=\vartheta_{p}\vartheta_{p}
, and the result follows since the only idempotent bijection
p^{\downarrow}\to p^{\downarrow}
is the identity.

(iii)
. Both maps have domain
p^{\downarrow}
, so it suffices to show that
t\vartheta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}=t\vartheta_{a}
for all
t\leq p
. For this we use
(ii)(O4)′
to calculate

t\vartheta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}={\bf r}({}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf r}({}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}a)=t\vartheta_{a}.\qed

In later sections we will define
v
-congruences by specifying sets of generating pairs:

Definition 2.15
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, and
\Omega\subseteq\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
a set of pairs satisfying

•

(u,v)\in\Omega\ \Rightarrow\ [{\bf d}(u)={\bf d}(v)
and
{\bf r}(u)={\bf r}(v)]
.

The
(
v
-)congruence generated by
\Omega
, denoted
\Omega^{\sharp}
, is the least congruence on
\mathcal{C}
containing
\Omega
. Specifically, we have
(a,b)\in\Omega^{\sharp}
if and only if there is a sequence

a=a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{k}=b

such that for each
1\leq i<k
we have

a_{i}=b_{i}\circ u_{i}\circ c_{i}\and a_{i+1}=b_{i}\circ v_{i}\circ c_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $b_{i},c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}$ and $(u_{i},v_{i})\in\Omega\cup\Omega^{-1}$.}

(Here
\Omega^{-1}=\{(v,u):(u,v)\in\Omega\}
.)

The next result shows how conditions
(C4)
and
(C5)
regarding a
v
-congruence
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
can be deduced from the corresponding properties of its generating set
\Omega
.

Lemma 2.16
.

Suppose
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
is a
v
-congruence on an ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
.

(i)

If
\Omega
satisfies the condition

(a,b)\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pta^{*}\approx b^{*},

(2.17)

then
\approx
satisfies condition
(C4)
.

(ii)

If
\Omega
satisfies the condition

(a,b)\in\Omega\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b\text{ for all $p\leq{\bf d}(a)$,}

(2.18)

then
\approx
satisfies condition
(C5)
.

Proof.

We just prove the second part, as the first is easier. We first claim that

\vartheta_{a}=\vartheta_{b}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $(a,b)\in\Omega$.}

(2.19)

Indeed, for all
(a,b)\in\Omega
we have
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
for all
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, by (
2.18
). Since
\approx
is a
v
-congruence, it follows that
{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b)
, i.e.
p\vartheta_{a}=p\vartheta_{b}
, for all such
p
, and this says precisely that
\vartheta_{a}=\vartheta_{b}
.

To show that
(C5)
holds, suppose
a\approx b
, and let the
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},u_{i},v_{i}
be as in Definition
2.15
. Of course it suffices to show that
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a_{i}\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a_{i+1}
for each
i
. For this we define

q=p\vartheta_{b_{i}}\and r=q\vartheta_{u_{i}}.

Since
\vartheta_{u_{i}}=\vartheta_{v_{i}}
by (
2.19
), we also have
r=q\vartheta_{v_{i}}
. We then use
(ii)(O5)′
and (
2.18
) to calculate

{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a_{i}={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(b_{i}\circ u_{i}\circ c_{i})={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b_{i}\circ{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}u_{i}\circ{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}c_{i}\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b_{i}\circ{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}v_{i}\circ{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}c_{i}={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(b_{i}\circ v_{i}\circ c_{i})={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a_{i+1}.\qed

3
Regular
*
-semigroups

We now arrive at the main subject of our study, the class of regular
*
-semigroups. In Section
3.1
we recall the relevant definitions, and give some basic results. In Section
3.2
we construct a functor into the category of ordered groupoids; see Proposition
3.27
. We then show how the abstract theory applies in a number of concrete examples: adjacency semigroups and Rees 0-matrix semigroups in Section
3.3
, and diagram monoids in Section
3.4
. We will return to these examples often throughout the paper.

3.1
Definitions and basic properties

Here is the main definition, given by Nordahl and Scheiblich in
[
119
]
:

Definition 3.1
.

A
regular
*
-semigroup
is a semigroup
S
with a unary operation
{{}^{*}:S\to S:a\mapsto a^{*}}
satisfying

(a^{*})^{*}=a=aa^{*}a\and(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

We denote by
{\bf RSS}
the category of all regular
*
-semigroups. A morphism in
{\bf RSS}
is a
*
-semigroup homomorphism
(a.k.a.
*
-morphism
), i.e. a map
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
(for regular
*
-semigroups
S
and
S^{\prime}
) satisfying

(ab)\phi=(a\phi)(b\phi)\and(a^{*})\phi=(a\phi)^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$,}

where for simplicity we have written
∗
for the unary operation on both
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Note that a regular
*
-semigroup
S
is considered to be an algebra of type
(2,1)
, i.e.
∗
is a basic operation of
S
. It is also worth noting that a semigroup homomorphism between regular
*
-semigroups need not be a
*
-morphism. For example, if
e\in S
satisfies
e^{2}=e\not=e^{*}
, then the constant map with image
e
is a semigroup homomorphism
S\to S
, but not a
*
-morphism. This means that
{\bf RSS}
is not a
full
subcategory of the category of all semigroups.

Any inverse semigroup is a regular
*
-semigroup, with the involution being inversion,
{a^{*}=a^{-1}}
. In fact, it has long been known
[
132
]
that inverse semigroups are precisely the regular
*
-semigroups satisfying the additional identity

aa^{*}bb^{*}=bb^{*}aa^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

(3.2)

As we will see, inverse semigroups represent a somewhat ‘degenerate’ case in the theory we develop; see Section
9
.

For a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we write

E=E(S)=\{e\in S:e^{2}=e\}\and P=P(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}.

So
E
consists of all idempotents of
S
. The elements of
P
are called
projections
, and of course we have
P\subseteq E
. Projections play a very important role in virtually every study of regular
*
-semigroups in the literature, and this is also true in the current work.
The following result gathers some of the basic properties of idempotents and projections. Proofs can be found in many places (e.g.,
[
70
]
), but we give some simple arguments here to keep the paper self-contained.

Lemma 3.3
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, with sets of idempotents
E=E(S)
and projections
P=P(S)
, then

(i)

P=\{aa^{*}:a\in S\}=\{a^{*}a:a\in S\}
,

(ii)

E=P^{2}=\{pq:p,q\in P\}
, and consequently
\langle E\rangle=\langle P\rangle
,

(iii)

a^{*}Pa\subseteq P
for all
a\in S
.

Proof.

(i)
. It is enough to show that
P=\{aa^{*}:a\in S\}
. If
p\in P
, then
p=pp=pp^{*}
. Conversely, if
a\in S
, then
(aa^{*})^{2}=aa^{*}aa^{*}=aa^{*}
, and
(aa^{*})^{*}=(a^{*})^{*}a^{*}=aa^{*}
.

(ii)
. If
e\in E
, then
e=ee^{*}e=e(ee)^{*}e=ee^{*}\cdot e^{*}e
, with
ee^{*},e^{*}e\in P
, by part
(i)
. Conversely, for any
p,q\in P
, we have
pq=pq(pq)^{*}pq=pqq^{*}p^{*}pq=pqqppq=pqpq=(pq)^{2}
.

(iii)
. If
p\in P
, then
a^{*}pa=a^{*}p^{*}pa=(pa)^{*}pa\in P
by part
(i)
.
∎

Comparing (
3.2
) with Lemma
3.3
(i)
, it follows that inverse semigroups are precisely the regular
*
-semigroups with commuting projections. In fact, it is easy to see that any idempotent of an inverse semigroup is a projection, so another equivalent condition for a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to be inverse is that
E(S)=P(S)
. Some other conditions will be listed below in Lemma
3.9
.

Consider a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, and let
P=P(S)
. If
p,q\in P
, then it follows from Lemma
3.3
(iii)
that
pqp=p^{*}qp\in P
. Thus, for all
p\in P
, we have a well-defined map

\theta_{p}:P\to P\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003ptq\theta_{p}=pqp\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $q\in P$.}

The next result summarises some of the key properties of these maps.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, then for any
p,q\in P=P(S)
we have

p\theta_{p}=p,\hskip 20.00003pt\theta_{p}\theta_{p}=\theta_{p},\hskip 20.00003ptp\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p},\hskip 20.00003pt\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=\theta_{q\theta_{p}}\and\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}.

Proof.

The first two follow from the fact that projections are idempotents.
The third and fifth follow from the fact that the product of two projections is an idempotent (cf. Lemma
3.3
(ii)
).
For the fourth, given any
t\in P
we have

t\theta_{q\theta_{p}}=t\theta_{pqp}=pqp\cdot t\cdot pqp=t\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}.\qed

Remark 3.5
.

In
[
8
]
, Banaschewski studied the special class of regular
*
-semigroups satisfying the additional identity
abb^{*}a^{*}=aa^{*}
. It is not hard to see that these are precisely the regular
*
-semigroups for which each
\theta_{p}
is a constant map (with image
p
). Indeed, for
p,q\in P
, we take
a=p
and
b=q
in the above identity to see that
q\theta_{p}=pqp=pqq^{*}p^{*}=pp^{*}=p
. Conversely, given any
a,b\in S
, if
\theta_{a^{*}a}
is constant, then

abb^{*}a^{*}=aa^{*}abb^{*}a^{*}aa^{*}=a\cdot(bb^{*})\theta_{a^{*}a}\cdot a^{*}=a\cdot a^{*}a\cdot a^{*}=aa^{*}.

We will say more about these semigroups in Remark
3.14
.

Next we define a relation
\leq
on
P=P(S)
by

p\leq q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=p\theta_{q}=qpq\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p,q\in P$}.

(3.6)

Note that
p\leq q
precisely when
q
is a left and right identity for
p
. Using this it is easy to see that
\leq
is a partial order. It is also possible to deduce this fact from the identities listed in Lemma
3.4
, as we do in Lemma
4.6
below. In fact, since
p=qpq\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=pq=qp
, and since

p=pq\ \Rightarrow\ p=p^{*}=(pq)^{*}=q^{*}p^{*}=qp,

and conversely by symmetry, it follows that

p\leq q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=pq\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=qp.

(3.7)

The partial order on projections can be used to verify the following neat equational (rather than existential) characterisation of Green’s relations on a regular
*
-semigroup. It is again well known, but we provide a simple proof for completeness.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, and if
a,b\in S
, then

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ aa^{*}=bb^{*}\and a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ a^{*}a=b^{*}b.

Proof.

We give the argument for
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
, with
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
being dual.
Suppose first that
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b
, so that
a=bx
for some
x\in S
. Using (
3.7
), it then follows that

aa^{*}=bxa^{*}=bb^{*}bxa^{*}=bb^{*}aa^{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}\leq bb^{*}.

By symmetry, we also have
bb^{*}\leq aa^{*}
, so that
aa^{*}=bb^{*}
.

Conversely, if
aa^{*}=bb^{*}
, then
a=aa^{*}a=b(b^{*}a)
, and similarly
b=a(a^{*}b)
, so that
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b
.
∎

The above properties of projections, and their associated
\theta
mappings, will be of fundamental importance in all that follows. In particular, even though
P=P(S)
is generally not a subsemigroup of the regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we can regard it as a
unary algebra
, whose basic (unary) operations are the
\theta_{p}
(
p\in P
). In Section
4
, we take the properties from Lemma
3.4
as the axioms for what we will call a
projection algebra
(see Definition
4.1
). It has been known for some time that (abstract) projection algebras are precisely the unary algebras of projections of regular
*
-semigroups (see for example
[
72
]
), but we will also verify this with a simple example in Section
4.3
. When
S
is inverse,
P
is simply a subsemigroup of
S
, and there is no real advantage in considering
P
as a unary algebra. In a sense, a projection algebra is the ‘
*
-analogue’ of the semilattice of idempotents of an inverse semigroup.
For this reason, it is convenient to record the following simple result characterising inverse semigroups in terms of properties of their projections.

Lemma 3.9
.

For a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

S
is inverse,

(ii)

E(S)=P(S)
,

(iii)

P(S)
is a subsemigroup of
S
,

(iv)

pq=qp
for all
p,q\in P(S)
,

(v)

p\theta_{q}=q\theta_{p}
for all
p,q\in P(S)
.

Proof.

This is straightforward, and mostly well known. We just give a proof that
(v)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. For this, we first note that
(v)
says
pqp=qpq
for any
p,q\in P(S)
. Combining this with Lemma
3.3
(ii)
, it then follows that

pq=(pq)^{2}=p(qpq)=p(pqp)=pqp\in P.\qed

Remark 3.10
.

One might wonder why the following condition was not included in Lemma
3.9
:

•

the poset
(P(S),{\leq})
is a
\wedge
-semilattice.

While this is a necessary condition for
S
to be inverse, it is not sufficient, as we will see in Example
3.33
below.

As we have already mentioned, projections have played a crucial role in virtually all studies of regular
*
-semigroups. Of particular significance in papers such as
[
24
,
35
,
30
]
are pairs of projections
{p,q\in P=P(S)}
that are mutual inverses, i.e. pairs that satisfy
p=pqp
and
q=qpq
. This is very much the case in the current work: so much so, in fact, that we define relations
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
on
P
by

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=pqp=q\theta_{p}\and p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ [p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\text{ and }q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p].

(3.11)

We think of pairs of
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections as
friends
(hence the symbol
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
). It is clear that
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
are both reflexive, and that
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is symmetric. But neither
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
nor
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is transitive in general (and neither is friendship in ‘real life’); for some examples of this, see Sections
3.3
and
3.4
. It is easy to see that
p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
, where
\leq
is the partial order in (
3.6
). It follows quickly from Lemma
3.8
that

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pq\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}qp\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}q.

(3.12)

It of course follows from this that

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}q,

(3.13)

though the converse does not hold in general; again see Sections
3.3
and
3.4
.

Remark 3.14
.

Following on from Remark
3.5
, when the
\theta
maps in a regular
*
-semigroup
S
are all constant, it follows that all projections are
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related (cf. (
3.11
)), and hence
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-related (cf. (
3.13
)). Consequently, such an
S
is
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-simple. This gives a new (simple) proof of
[
122
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Combining (
3.12
) with
[
110
, Theorem 3]
, it follows that

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ L_{p}\cap R_{q}\text{ contains an idempotent}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ R_{p}\cap L_{q}\text{ contains an idempotent},

and of course these idempotents are

qp\in L_{p}\cap R_{q}\and pq\in R_{p}\cap L_{q}.

This all means that a pair of
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections
p,q\in P(S)
generates a
2\times 2
rectangular band in
S
:

p
pq
qp
q

One can define a graph
\Gamma(S)
with vertex set
P=P(S)
, and with (undirected) edges
\{p,q\}
whenever
p\not=q
and
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
. Connectivity properties of (certain subgraphs of) these graphs played a vital role in
[
35
]
, in studies of idempotent-generated regular
*
-semigroups and their ideals. We will say more about this in Section
3.4
.

The (standard) proof of Lemma
3.3
(ii)
given above involved showing that any idempotent
e\in E=E(S)
of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
satisfies
e=ee^{*}\cdot e^{*}e
, with
ee^{*},e^{*}e\in P=P(S)
. It quickly follows that the idempotents
e,e^{*}\in E(S)
also generate a
2\times 2
rectangular band in
S
:

\phantom{{}_{*}}e\phantom{{}^{*}}
ee^{*}_{\phantom{*}}
e^{*}_{\phantom{*}}e
e^{*}_{\phantom{*}}

This is in fact a characterisation of the biordered sets arising from regular
*
-semigroups, in a sense made precise in
[
118
, Corollary 2.7]
. In any case, it quickly follows that
ee^{*}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e^{*}e
for any
e\in E
, so this shows that any idempotent is a product of two
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections, and it follows that not only do we have
E=P^{2}
(cf. Lemma
3.3
(ii)
), but in fact

E=\{pq:(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\}.

Actually, such factorisations for idempotents are
unique
. Indeed, if
e=pq
with
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
, then

ee^{*}=pq(pq)^{*}=pqq^{*}p^{*}=pqp=p\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pte^{*}e=q.

(3.15)

Consequently, we have
|E|=|{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}|
. A number of studies have enumerated the idempotents in various classes of regular
*
-semigroups
[
23
,
22
,
85
]
. The identity
|E|=|{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}|
played an implicit role in
[
23
]
.

So idempotents are products of
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections. As an application of the theory developed in this paper, we will be able to prove the following result, which extends this to arbitrary products of idempotents, though uniqueness is lost, in general (see for example (
3.54
)); see Proposition
7.17
(iii)
and Remark
7.19
. The result might be known, but we are not aware of its existence in the literature; it does bear some resemblance, however, to a classical result of FitzGerald
[
46
]
on products of idempotents in regular semigroups. Moreover, we believe it could be useful in studies of idempotent-generated regular
*
-semigroups, and might have even led to some simpler arguments in existing studies such as
[
34
,
35
,
24
]
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup, with
E=E(S)
and
P=P(S)
. Then for any
a\in\langle E\rangle=\langle P\rangle
we have

a=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P$ with $p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}$.}

3.2
From regular
*
-semigroups to ordered groupoids

We now show how to use the projections of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to construct an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
. The groupoid
\mathcal{G}
has the same underlying set as
S
, and the (partially defined) composition in
\mathcal{G}
is a restriction of the (totally defined) product in
S
. Roughly speaking,
\mathcal{G}
remembers only the ‘nice’ or ‘easy’ products; see Sections
3.3
and
3.4
for some examples justifying our use of these words. More importantly, the construction leads to a functor from regular
*
-semigroups to ordered groupoids; see Proposition
3.27
.

As it happens, the construction of
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
is
exactly
the same as in the inverse case; see
[
88
, Chapter 4]
. However, we will see that the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
is not a
total invariant
of the regular
*
-semigroup
S
, unlike the case for inverse semigroups; see Examples
3.33
and
3.39
.

For a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, and for
a\in S
, we write

{\bf d}(a)=aa^{*}\and{\bf r}(a)=a^{*}a.

Both of these elements are projections (cf. Lemma
3.3
(i)
), and the identity
a=aa^{*}a
gives

a={\bf d}(a)\cdot a=a\cdot{\bf r}(a).

Moreover, Lemma
3.8
says that

a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\bf d}(a)={\bf d}(b)\and a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\bf r}(a)={\bf r}(b).

Since
p=pp^{*}=p^{*}p
for any projection
p
, we have
{\bf d}(p)={\bf r}(p)=p
for such
p
. It then follows from Lemma
3.8
that the
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
- and
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-classes of
p
are given by

R_{p}=\{a\in S:aa^{*}=pp^{*}\}=\{a\in S:{\bf d}(a)=p\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003ptL_{p}=\{a\in S:{\bf r}(a)=p\}.

(3.17)

Lemma 3.18
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, and if
a,b\in S
satisfy
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then

{\bf d}(ab)={\bf d}(a)\and{\bf r}(ab)={\bf r}(b).

Proof.

We just prove the first claim, as the second is symmetrical. If
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
a^{*}a=bb^{*}
, and then

{\bf d}(ab)=ab(ab)^{*}=abb^{*}a=aa^{*}aa^{*}=aa^{*}={\bf d}(a).\qed

This allows us to make the following definition.

Definition 3.19
.

Given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we define the (small) category
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
as follows.

•

The underlying (morphism) set of
\mathcal{G}
is
S
.

•

The object set of
\mathcal{G}
is
v\mathcal{G}=P=P(S)
.

•

For
a\in\mathcal{G}
we have
{\bf d}(a)=aa^{*}
and
{\bf r}(a)=a^{*}a
.

•

For
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
with
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, we have
a\circ b=ab
.

Since the underlying set of
\mathcal{G}
is
S
, the unary operation
{}^{*}:S\to S
can be thought of as a map
{}^{*}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
. It is a routine matter to verify conditions
(I1)
–
(I4)
from Definition
2.1
, so that
\mathcal{G}
is a groupoid, with inversion given by
{}^{-1}={}^{*}
:

•

For
a\in\mathcal{G}
we have
a^{-1}=a^{*}
.

We will soon show that
\mathcal{G}
is in fact an
ordered
groupoid, for which we need to define suitable restrictions. Let
\leq
be the ordering on
P=v\mathcal{G}
given in (
3.6
).

•

For
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
we define
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a=pa
.

Remark 3.20
.

For projections
p,q\in P
, it follows from (
3.17
) that

\mathcal{G}(p,q)=\{a\in S:{\bf d}(a)=p,\ {\bf r}(a)=q\}=R_{p}\cap L_{q}.

Since
{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}}\circ{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}
, such a morphism set is non-empty precisely when
p\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}q
, and is then in fact an
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-class. It follows that the connected components of
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
are in one-one correspondence with the
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-classes of
S
.

Proposition 3.21
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, then
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
is an ordered groupoid.

Proof.

We need to verify conditions
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
from Lemma
2.3
, with respect to the ordering (
3.6
), and the restrictions given in Definition
3.19
.

(ii)(O1)′
. Consider a morphism
a\in\mathcal{G}
, and let
p\leq{\bf d}(a)=aa^{*}
. It follows from
p\leq aa^{*}
that
p=paa^{*}
, and so

{\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf d}(pa)=pa(pa)^{*}=paa^{*}p^{*}=pp=p.

We also have

{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf r}(pa)=(pa)^{*}pa=a^{*}p^{*}pa=a^{*}pa.

(3.22)

It then follows that

{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)=a^{*}pa=a^{*}a\cdot a^{*}pa\cdot a^{*}a={\bf r}(a)\cdot{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\cdot{\bf r}(a).

By (
3.6
), the previous conclusion says precisely that
{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\leq{\bf r}(a)
.

(ii)(O2)′
. By (
3.22
) we have
q=a^{*}pa
, and again
p=paa^{*}
follows from
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
. It follows that

({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)^{*}=(pa)^{*}=a^{*}p^{*}=a^{*}p=a^{*}paa^{*}=qa^{*}={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*}.

(ii)(O3)′
. Here
{}_{{\bf d}(a)}{\downharpoonleft}a={\bf d}(a)a=aa^{*}a=a
.

(ii)(O4)′
. It follows from
p\leq q
that
p=pq
, and so
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a=p(qa)=(pq)a=pa={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
.

(ii)(O5)′
. Here we again have
q=a^{*}pa
. Since
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
we have
p=aa^{*}p
, so
pa=aa^{*}pa=aq
. It follows that

{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(a\circ b)=p(ab)=pp(ab)=paqb={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\circ{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}b.\qed

As in Remark
2.5
, right restrictions in
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
are given by
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}=({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{-1})^{-1}
, for
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, and in fact we have

a{\downharpoonright}_{q}=(qa^{*})^{*}=aq

for such
q
. For
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
, we have

\displaystyle a\leq b

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pta={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b

for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pta=b{\downharpoonright}_{q}

for some
q\leq{\bf r}(b)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pta={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{q}

for some
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
q\leq{\bf r}(b)
,

(3.23)

and then of course
p={\bf d}(a)
and
q={\bf r}(a)
.

Remark 3.24
.

In particular, the relation
\leq
on the regular
*
-semigroup
S(=\mathcal{G})
given in (
3.23
) is a partial order. This order was in fact used by Imaoka in
[
71
]
, but in a different form. Imaoka’s order, which for clarity we will denote by
\leq^{\prime}
, was defined by

a\leq^{\prime}b\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a\in Pb\cap bP\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where $P=P(S)$.}

(3.25)

In light of the rules
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b=pb
and
b{\downharpoonright}_{q}=bq
, it is clear that
a\leq b\ \Rightarrow\ a\leq^{\prime}b
. For the converse, suppose
a\leq^{\prime}b
, so that

a=pb=bq\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $p,q\in P$.}

In the following calculations, we make extensive use of (
3.7
). We first note that

a=bq\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pta=bb^{*}a\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}=bb^{*}aa^{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}\leq bb^{*}.

We also have

a=pb\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pta=pa\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}=paa^{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}\leq p\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptaa^{*}=aa^{*}p.

We then calculate

a=aa^{*}a=aa^{*}(pb)=(aa^{*}p)b=aa^{*}b.

Writing
r=aa^{*}\in P
, we have already seen that
r=aa^{*}\leq bb^{*}={\bf d}(b)
, so that in fact
a=aa^{*}b=rb={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}b
, and hence
a\leq b
.

Remark 3.26
.

Recall that any regular semigroup
S
has a so-called
natural partial order
. This order, which for clarity we will denote by
\preceq
, has
many
different formulations; see for example
[
63
,
115
,
65
]
, and especially
[
112
]
for a discussion of the variations, and an extension to arbitrary semigroups. The most straightforward definition of the natural partial order on the regular semigroup
S
is:

a\preceq b\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a\in Eb\cap bE\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where $E=E(S)$.}

It is natural to ask how the orders
\leq
and
\preceq
are related when
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, where
\leq
is the order from (
3.23
). Since
P\subseteq E
(and since
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b\in Pb
and
b{\downharpoonright}_{q}\in bP
for
p\leq{\bf d}(b)
and
q\leq{\bf r}(b)
), the order
\leq
is of course contained in
\preceq
, meaning that
a\leq b\ \Rightarrow\ a\preceq b
. But the converse does not hold in general. For example, if
S
is a regular
*
-
monoid
with identity
1
, and if
e\in E\setminus P
is any non-projection idempotent, then
e\preceq 1
but
e\not\leq 1
.

The construction of
\mathcal{G}(S)
from
S
can be thought of as an object map from the category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups (with
*
-morphisms) to the category
{\bf OG}
of ordered groupoids (with ordered groupoid functors). Since the underlying sets of
S
and
\mathcal{G}(S)
are the same, any
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
can be thought of as a map
\mathcal{G}(\phi)=\phi:\mathcal{G}(S)\to\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
. The main result of this section shows that
\mathcal{G}
(interpreted in this way) is a functor:

Proposition 3.27
.

\mathcal{G}
is a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf OG}
.

Proof.

It remains to show that any
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
in
{\bf RSS}
is an ordered groupoid morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
, i.e. that

(i)

(a\circ b)\phi=(a\phi)\circ(b\phi)
for
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
with
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and

(ii)

({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\phi={}_{p\phi}{\downharpoonleft}a\phi
for
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
.
4
4

4

Strictly speaking, we must also check that
\mathcal{G}(\phi\circ\phi^{\prime})=\mathcal{G}(\phi)\circ\mathcal{G}(\phi^{\prime})
for composable morphisms
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
in
{\bf RSS}
, and that
\mathcal{G}(\operatorname{id}_{S})=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}(S)}
for all
S
. However, these are trivial. This is the case for all other functors constructed in the paper, so we will pass over this step in all such proofs.

Before we do this, we first note that for any
a\in\mathcal{G}
we have

{\bf d}(a\phi)=(a\phi)(a\phi)^{*}=(aa^{*})\phi={\bf d}(a)\phi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf r}(a\phi)={\bf r}(a)\phi.

We make repeated use of this in the following, as well as (
3.7
) and Definition
3.19
.

(i)
. If
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
{\bf r}(a\phi)={\bf r}(a)\phi={\bf d}(b)\phi={\bf d}(b\phi)
, and so

(a\circ b)\phi=(ab)\phi=(a\phi)(b\phi)=(a\phi)\circ(b\phi).

(ii)
. If
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, then
p=p\cdot{\bf d}(a)
and so
p\phi=(p\phi)({\bf d}(a)\phi)=(p\phi)\cdot{\bf d}(a\phi)
, so that
p\phi\leq{\bf d}(a\phi)
, and then

({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\phi=(pa)\phi=(p\phi)(a\phi)={}_{p\phi}{\downharpoonleft}a\phi.\qed

Remark 3.28
.

The functor
\mathcal{G}
is not surjective (on objects). To see this, we first note that any poset can be thought of as an ordered groupoid consisting entirely of (pairwise-noncomposable) identities. Consider the case in which
\mathcal{G}=\{p,q\}
is a two-element anti-chain. Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
for some regular
*
-semigroup
S
. We then have
P(S)=\{p,q\}
, and we claim that
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
. Indeed, if this was not the case then either
p=p\theta_{q}\leq q
or
q=q\theta_{p}\leq p
, neither of which hold. Since
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
, it follows that
e=pq\in S(=\mathcal{G})
satisfies
{\bf d}(e)=p
and
{\bf r}(e)=q
(cf. (
3.15
)), i.e.
e\in\mathcal{G}(p,q)
, contradicting
\mathcal{G}(p,q)=\varnothing
.

We will see in Section
3.3
that
\mathcal{G}
is not injective (on objects) either. That is, it is possible to have
\mathcal{G}(S)=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
for distinct regular
*
-semigroups
S
and
S^{\prime}
. In a sense, the rest of the paper is devoted to the task of finding a suitable enrichment of the groupoids
\mathcal{G}(S)
to obtain a faithful representation of regular
*
-semigroups by ordered groupoids.

On the other hand, the ESN Theorem tells us that the restriction of the functor
\mathcal{G}
to the category of inverse semigroups
{\bf IS}\subseteq{\bf RSS}

is
injective, with image
{\bf IG}
, the category of inductive groupoids.

Remark 3.29
.

The (partial) composition of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
is a restriction of the (total) product of the regular
*
-semigroup
S
. Nevertheless, the order on
\mathcal{G}
can be used to reduce the calculation of an arbitrary product
ab
in
S
to an associated composition in
\mathcal{G}
:

ab=a^{\prime}\circ e\circ b^{\prime},

(3.30)

where
a^{\prime}\leq a
and
b^{\prime}\leq b
, and where
e\in\mathcal{G}
is a special morphism
{\bf r}(a^{\prime})\to{\bf d}(b^{\prime})
. Specifically, let us write

p={\bf r}(a)=a^{*}a\and q={\bf d}(b)=bb^{*},

noting that
a=ap
and
b=qb
. We also define the additional projections

p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}=pqp\leq p\and q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}=qpq\leq q.

Then since
pq
is an idempotent (cf. Lemma
3.3
(ii)
), we have
p^{\prime}q^{\prime}=(pq)^{3}=pq
, and so

ab=ap\cdot qb=a\cdot p^{\prime}q^{\prime}\cdot b=ap^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}q^{\prime}\cdot q^{\prime}b.

Then with
a^{\prime}=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}=ap^{\prime}
,
e=p^{\prime}q^{\prime}(=pq)
and
b^{\prime}={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}b=q^{\prime}b
, one can check that
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q^{\prime}
, from which it follows (as in (
3.15
)) that

{\bf d}(e)=p^{\prime}={\bf r}(a^{\prime})\and{\bf r}(e)=q^{\prime}={\bf d}(b^{\prime}).

Thus, continuing from above, it follows that indeed

ab=ap^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}q^{\prime}\cdot q^{\prime}b=a^{\prime}\cdot e\cdot b^{\prime}=a^{\prime}\circ e\circ b^{\prime}.

Remark 3.31
.

There is a rather subtle point regarding Remark
3.29
that is far from obvious at this stage, but ought to be mentioned now. It may seem as if we are implying that the structure of the regular
*
-semigroup
S
is completely determined by that of the ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
. That is, if we are given a complete description of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, including its composition, inversion and ordering,
then it might seem that we ought to be able to construct the entire multiplication table of
S
.
However, this is far from the truth. Indeed, in Section
3.3
, we will see that it is possible for distinct (even non-isomorphic) regular
*
-semigroups
S
and
S^{\prime}
to have exactly the same groupoids
\mathcal{G}(S)=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
; see Examples
3.33
and
3.39
.

If the reader is worried that this contradicts the previous remark, the source of the subtlety is in the precise identity of the element
e
from (
3.30
) that allowed us to reduce a product
ab
in
S
to a composition in
\mathcal{G}
:

ab=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ e\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b.

(On the other hand, the projections
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
can be found directly using the so-called
projection algebra
structure of
P=v\mathcal{G}
.) We were able to locate this element
e
in Remark
3.29
because we began with full knowledge of the semigroup
S
; we simply took
e=pq=p^{\prime}q^{\prime}
. However, if we begin with an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, it is not so obvious what this element
e
should be, even if we know that
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
for some (unknown) regular
*
-semigroup
S
. Certainly
e
should be a morphism
p^{\prime}\to q^{\prime}
, but when
p^{\prime}\not=q^{\prime}
, it is not so easy to distinguish any such morphism. Getting around this problem is one of the main sources of difficulty encountered in the current work. Our eventual solution utilises what we will call the ‘chain groupoid’
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
associated to an (abstract) projection algebra
P
, and a certain ‘evaluation map’
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
. We will find our element
e
in the image of this map.

Remark 3.32
.

Before moving on, it is worth commenting on another related notion, namely the so-called
trace
of a semigroup. This goes back to a classical result of Miller and Clifford. Among other things,
[
110
, Theorem 3]
says that for elements
a,b
of a semigroup
S
, we have

ab\in R_{a}\cap L_{b}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ L_{a}\cap R_{b}\text{ contains an idempotent}.

Such products
ab\in R_{a}\cap L_{b}
are often called
trace products
in the literature. We can then define a partial binary operation
\odot
on
S
by

a\odot b=\begin{cases}ab&\text{if $L_{a}\cap R_{b}$ contains an idempotent}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and the resulting partial algebra
(S,{\odot})
is often called the
trace of
S
. Following Definition
3.19
, it is not hard to see that in a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, a composition
a\circ b
exists precisely when
L_{a}\cap R_{b}
contains a projection, which must of course be
a^{*}a=bb^{*}
, i.e.
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
. Since projections are idempotents, it follows that
a\circ b
being defined forces
a\odot b
to be defined, and then of course
a\circ b=a\odot b=ab
, meaning that
{\circ}\subseteq{\odot}
, i.e. that
\odot
is an extension of
\circ
. We have already noted that
S
is inverse if and only if
P(S)=E(S)
, and it now quickly follows that this is also equivalent to having
{\circ}={\odot}
. In this inverse case, the trace
(S,{\odot})
is then precisely the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
from Definition
3.19
, but this is not true of non-inverse regular
*
-semigroups.

3.3
Adjacency semigroups and Rees 0-matrix semigroups

The rest of Section
3
is devoted to a discussion of some natural examples of regular
*
-semigroups. We will return to these over the course of the theoretical development below, as they will provide concrete instances of some of the subtleties that arise; see for example Remarks
6.15
,
6.32
and
6.41
. In the current section we treat adjacency semigroups and Rees 0-matrix semigroups. We look at diagram semigroups in Section
3.4
.

Example 3.33
.

Let
\Gamma=(P,E)
be a digraph, so that
P
is a vertex set, and
E\subseteq P\times P
an edge set. The
adjacency semigroup
of
\Gamma
, denoted
A(\Gamma)
, has underlying set
(P\times P)\cup\{0\}
, and product defined by

a0=0a=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $a\in A(\Gamma)$}\and(p,q)(r,s)=\begin{cases}(p,s)&\text{if $(q,r)\in E$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.34)

We also have a unary operation
∗
defined by

0^{*}=0\and(p,q)^{*}=(q,p).

(3.35)

Clearly
A(\Gamma)
satisfies the identity
(a^{*})^{*}=a
. Adjacency semigroups were studied by Jackson and Volkov in
[
73
]
, who observed that:

•

the identity
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
is satisfied if and only if the adjacency relation is symmetric, i.e.
(p,q)\in E\ \Leftrightarrow\ (q,p)\in E
for all
p,q\in P
, and

•

the identity
a=aa^{*}a
is satisfied if and only if the adjacency relation is reflexive, i.e.
(p,p)\in E
for all
p\in P
.

Thus,
S=A(\Gamma)
is a regular
*
-semigroup (under the above operations) precisely when
E
is reflexive and symmetric, i.e. when
\Gamma
is an undirected graph with loops at every vertex. When these conditions hold, and identifying
p\equiv(p,p)
for
p\in P
, we have
P(S)\equiv P\cup\{0\}
, which for convenience we will denote by
P_{0}
.

The
\theta_{0}
operation on
P(S)=P_{0}
is of course the constant map with image
0
, while for
p\in P
and
q\in P_{0}
we have

q\theta_{p}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if $q\in P$ and $(p,q)\in E$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.36)

It follows from this that the poset
(P_{0},{\leq})
, as in (
3.6
), is the following
fan semilattice
,
5
5

5

Note that the ordered set
(P_{0},{\leq})
being a semilattice does not imply that
(P_{0},{\cdot})
is a semilattice in the semigroup-theoretic sense (i.e. a commutative band). Consulting Lemma
3.9
(or using (
3.34
)), the latter is the case precisely when
S
is inverse, which occurs when
\Gamma
is a disjoint union of loops.

shown in the case
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{5}\}
:

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
p_{4}
p_{5}
0

(3.37)

Indeed, we clearly have
0\leq p
for all
p\in P_{0}
. If
p\leq q
for
p,q\in P
, then
p=p\theta_{q}
; since
\operatorname{im}(\theta_{q})=\{0,q\}
(cf. (
3.36
)), it follows that
p=q
.
On the other hand, it follows from (
3.36
) that the
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relation on
P_{0}
is given by
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=E\cup\{(0,0)\}
, i.e. that

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptp=q=0\text{ \ \ or \ \ $(p,q)$ is an edge of $\Gamma$.}

(3.38)

Consequently,
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is transitive if and only if
\Gamma
is a disjoint union of complete graphs. Combining
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=E\cup\{(0,0)\}
with Lemma
3.3
(ii)
, it also follows that
E(S)=P_{0}^{2}=E\cup\{0\}
.

We now consider the ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
for the adjacency graph
S=A(\Gamma)
. First, we have
v\mathcal{G}=P(S)=P_{0}
. It is easy to check that

(p,q)\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}(r,s)\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=r\and(p,q)\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}(r,s)\ \Leftrightarrow\ q=s,

so that the non-empty morphism sets of
\mathcal{G}
are

\mathcal{G}(0,0)=\{0\}\and\mathcal{G}(p,q)=\{(p,q)\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $p,q\in P$.}

Thus, the only compositions in
\mathcal{G}
are
(p,q)\circ(q,r)=(p,r)
for all
p,q,r\in P
, and
0\circ 0=0
. Moreover, due to the nature of the order
(P_{0},{\leq})
, the only non-trivial restrictions in
\mathcal{G}
are
{}_{0}{\downharpoonleft}(p,q)=(p,q){\downharpoonright}_{0}=0
for
p,q\in P
. This all shows that the groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
depends only on the vertex set
P
of the underlying graph
\Gamma=(P,E)
, and not on its edge set
E
.

Consequently, two different graphs
\Gamma=(P,E)
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=(P,E^{\prime})
give rise to distinct adjacency semigroups
S=A(\Gamma)
and
S^{\prime}=A(\Gamma^{\prime})
, even though
\mathcal{G}(S)=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
are identical as ordered groupoids. Moreover, the semigroups
S
and
S^{\prime}
need not even be isomorphic. As an extreme example, we can take
\Gamma
to be the discrete graph (where
E
consists only of loops at each vertex), and
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be the complete graph. Here
S
is a
P\times P

combinatorial Brandt semigroup
and is hence inverse, while
S^{\prime}
is a
P\times P
square band with a zero adjoined. Since
(P_{0},{\leq})
is a semilattice (cf. (
3.37
)), the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
is inductive, even though
S^{\prime}
is not inverse for
|P|\geq 2
.

Regular Rees 0-matrix semigroups are one of the most important families of semigroups, being (up to isomorphism) precisely the completely 0-simple semigroups, as per the celebrated
Rees Theorem
; see
[
130
]
, and also
[
19
, Chapter 3]
or
[
69
, Section 3.2]
. It is therefore important to know which of these are regular
*
-semigroups:

Example 3.39
.

Let
P
and
Q
be arbitrary non-empty sets,
G
a group,
0
a symbol not belonging to
G
, and
M=(m_{qp})
a
Q\times P

regular sandwich matrix
over
G\cup\{0\}
. Regularity here means that
M
has at least one non-zero entry in each row and in each column. The
Rees 0-matrix semigroup

S=\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,Q,G,M)
has underlying set
(P\times G\times Q)\cup\{0\}
and product

a0=0a=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $a\in S$}\and(p,g,q)(r,h,s)=\begin{cases}(p,gm_{qr}h,s)&\text{if $m_{qr}\not=0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.40)

The non-zero
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
- and
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-classes of
S
are respectively the sets

R_{p}=\{p\}\times G\times Q\and L_{q}=P\times G\times\{q\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p\in P$ and $q\in Q$,}

the non-zero
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes are the sets

H_{pq}=R_{p}\cap L_{q}=\{p\}\times G\times\{q\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p\in P$ and $q\in Q$,}

and the non-zero idempotents have the form
(p,m_{qp}^{-1},q)
for each
p\in P
and
q\in Q
with
m_{pq}\not=0
. In particular,
H_{pq}
contains an idempotent if and only if
m_{qp}\not=0
. One can
scale
the matrix
M
by left-multiplying each column by any non-zero group element to obtain an isomorphic Rees 0-matrix semigroup.

Suppose now that
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, with respect to some involution
{}^{*}:S\to S
. Since
∗
maps
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-classes to
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-classes, and vice versa, there is a bijection
P\to Q:p\mapsto p^{\prime}
such that
R_{p}^{*}=L_{p^{\prime}}
for all
p\in P
. Thus, re-naming the elements of
Q
, we can assume that
Q=P
and
p^{\prime}=p
for all
p\in P
. Note then that
H_{pq}^{*}=H_{qp}
for all
p,q\in P
. In particular, the only
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes fixed by
∗
are
\{0\}
and
H_{pp}
(
p\in P
). Since each
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-class contains a projection, it follows that the projection in the non-zero
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-class
R_{p}
is the idempotent of
H_{pp}
. In particular, we have
m_{pp}\not=0
for all
p\in P
. We can scale the sandwich matrix
M
by multiplying each column
p
by
m_{pp}^{-1}
, in order to assume that in fact
m_{pp}=1
for all
p
. In this way, we can identify

p\equiv(p,1,p)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p\in P$,}

and again we have
P(S)\equiv P_{0}=P\cup\{0\}
.
Since
∗
maps group
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes to group
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes, we must have the condition

m_{qp}=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ m_{pq}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$.}

Now consider a non-zero idempotent
e
, say with
e\in H_{pq}
. As noted above, we have
e=(p,m_{qp}^{-1},q)
. But since also
e\in H_{pq}=R_{p}\cap L_{q}
, we have
e=pq\equiv(p,1,p)(q,1,q)=(p,m_{pq},q)
, and this shows that
m_{pq}=m_{qp}^{-1}
. If we write
0^{-1}=0
as well, then it follows that the sandwich matrix
M
has the skew-symmetric condition

\displaystyle m_{pq}

\displaystyle=m_{qp}^{-1}

for all
p,q\in P
,

(3.41)

and we recall that necessarily

\displaystyle m_{pp}

\displaystyle=1

for all
p\in P
.

(3.42)

Now consider some non-zero element
a=(p,g,q)\in S
. Since
aa^{*}
is the unique projection in the
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-class
R_{a}=R_{p}
, and since
a^{*}\in H_{pq}^{*}=H_{qp}
, we have
a^{*}=(q,h,p)
for some
h\in G
, and then

(p,1,p)\equiv p=aa^{*}=(p,g,q)(q,h,p)=(p,gm_{qq}h,p)=(p,gh,p),

so that
h=g^{-1}
. In other words, we have

(p,g,q)^{*}=(q,g^{-1},p)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$ and $g\in G$,}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and of course}\hskip 20.00003pt0^{*}=0.

(3.43)

To summarise, up to isomorphism, the Rees 0-matrix regular
*
-semigroups are precisely those of the form
\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
whose sandwich matrix
M=(m_{qp})
satisfies (
3.41
) and (
3.42
), and then the product and involution are given in (
3.40
) and (
3.43
).

Again
\theta_{0}
is a constant map, while for
p\in P
and
q\in P_{0}
we have

q\theta_{p}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if $q\in P$ and $m_{pq}\not=0$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.44)

The poset
(P_{0},{\leq})
is again the fan semilattice, as in (
3.37
). As in (
3.38
), the
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relation on
P_{0}
is given by

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptp=q=0\text{ \ \ or \ \ $m_{pq}\not=0$.}

(3.45)

The groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
can also be easily described. We again have
v\mathcal{G}=P(S)=P_{0}
, but this time the morphism sets are

\mathcal{G}(0,0)=\{0\}\and\mathcal{G}(p,q)=H_{pq}=\{p\}\times G\times\{q\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for $p,q\in P$.}

Composition is given by
(p,g,q)\circ(q,h,r)=(p,gh,r)
, remembering that
m_{qq}=1
, and inversion by
(p,g,q)^{-1}=(q,g^{-1},r)
. Again, the only non-trivial restrictions are
{}_{0}{\downharpoonleft}(p,g,q)=(p,g,q){\downharpoonright}_{0}=0
. It follows that the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
is independent of the sandwich matrix
M
, and depends only on the indexing set
P
and the group
G
.

Remark 3.46
.

If all entries of the sandwich matrix
M=(m_{qp})
are non-zero, then the
Rees matrix semigroup

\mathcal{M}(P,P,G,M)=P\times G\times P
is a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
. It is a regular
*
-semigroup under

(p,g,q)(r,h,s)=(p,gm_{qr}h,s)\and(p,g,q)^{*}=(q,g^{-1},p).

Square bands are a special case, where
G=\{1\}
is trivial. Involutions on Rees matrix semigroups were described in
[
122
, Section 3]
, but Rees 0-matrix semigroups were not discussed.

Remark 3.47
.

Adjacency semigroups are special cases of Rees 0-matrix semigroups. Given a graph
\Gamma=(P,E)
, we take
G=\{1\}
to be a trivial group, and we have
A(\Gamma)\cong\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
, where
M
is the adjacency matrix of
\Gamma
, i.e.
m_{pq}=1\ \Leftrightarrow\ (p,q)\in E
.

3.4
Diagram monoids

The theory of regular
*
-semigroups has seen quite a resurgence in recent years, partly due to the importance of so-called
diagram monoids
. Here we recall the definition of these monoids, and use them to illustrate some of the new ideas and constructions of the current paper, but we also relate this to previous studies. We will return to these monoids at various points during the rest of the paper (see for example Remarks
4.29
and
6.41
), and in the sequels
[
33
,
37
]
. Here we focus exclusively on the
partition monoids

[
101
,
77
]
