, although similar comments could be made for other diagram monoids, such as (partial) Brauer, Temperley-Lieb and Motzkin monoids
[
13
,
145
,
24
,
35
,
9
,
103
]
.

Let
X
be a set, and let
X^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:x\in X\}
be a disjoint copy of
X
. The
partition monoid over
X
, denoted
\mathcal{P}_{X}
, is defined as follows. The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
are the set partitions of
X\cup X^{\prime}
, and the operation will be defined shortly. A partition from
\mathcal{P}_{X}
will be identified with any graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime}
whose connected components are the blocks of the partition. The elements of
X
and
X^{\prime}
are called
upper
and
lower
vertices, respectively, and are generally displayed in two parallel rows, as in the various figures below.

To define the product on
\mathcal{P}_{X}
, consider two partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
. Let
X^{\prime\prime}=\{x^{\prime\prime}:x\in X\}
be another disjoint copy of
X
, and define three new graphs:

•

\alpha^{\vee}
, the graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime\prime}
obtained from
\alpha
by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\beta^{\wedge}
, the graph on vertex set
X^{\prime\prime}\cup X^{\prime}
obtained from
\beta
by changing each upper vertex
x
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
, the graph on vertex set
X\cup X^{\prime\prime}\cup X^{\prime}
, whose edge set is the union of the edge sets of
\alpha^{\vee}
and
\beta^{\wedge}
.

The graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
is called the
product graph
of
\alpha
and
\beta
; it is generally drawn with
X^{\prime\prime}
as the middle row of vertices. The product
\alpha\beta
is defined to be the partition of
X\cup X^{\prime}
with the property that
u,v\in X\cup X^{\prime}
belong to the same block of
\alpha\beta
if and only if
u,v
are connected by a path in
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
.

If
X=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for a non-negative integer
n
, we write
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{X}
.

Example 3.48
.

To illustrate the product above, consider the partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
defined by

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,2,3,1^{\prime}\},\{4,4^{\prime},5^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{5\},\{6\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\}\big\},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,4^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{2,3\},\{4,5,6,1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\},\{5^{\prime}\}\big\}.

Figure
1
illustrates (graphs representing)
\alpha
and
\beta
, their product

\displaystyle\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,2,3,4^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{4,1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\},\{5\},\{6\},\{5^{\prime}\}\big\},

as well as the product graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
. Here and elsewhere, vertices are assumed to increase from left to right,
1,\ldots,n
, and similarly for (double) dashed vertices.

\alpha=
\beta=
=\alpha\beta

Figure 1:
Left to right: partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
, the product graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
, and the product
\alpha\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
. For more information, see Example
3.48
.

A reader familiar with partition monoids might protest that we have ‘cheated’ in Example
3.48
, and that our choice of
\alpha
and
\beta
was too ‘easy’ to fully illustrate the complexities of calculating products of partitions. This is indeed the case, as the bottom half of
\alpha
‘matches’ the top half of
\beta
in a way that can hopefully be understood by examining Figure
1
, but which will be made precise shortly. Before this, we briefly consider a more ‘difficult’ product:

Example 3.49
.

Figure
2
gives another product, this time with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{20}
. One can immediately see that there is no such ‘matching’ between the bottom of
\alpha
and the top of
\beta
. Rather, to calculate the product
\alpha\beta
, one needs to follow paths in the product graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
, often alternating several times between edges coming from
\alpha
or
\beta
. For example, to see that
\{1^{\prime},4^{\prime}\}
is a block of
\alpha\beta
, one needs to trace the following path (or its reverse):

1^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\ \beta\ }1^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\ \alpha\ }2^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\ \beta\ }3^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\ \alpha\ }4^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\ \beta\ }4^{\prime}.

\alpha=
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
\beta=
\alpha\beta=

Figure 2:
Top: partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{20}
, already connected to create the product graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
. Bottom: the product
\alpha\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{20}
. For more information, see Example
3.49
.

We hope that Example
3.49
convinces the reader that products in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
can be ‘messy’. Of course things get worse when we increase the number of vertices, even more so when
X
is infinite
[
32
]
. Nevertheless, it is actually quite easy to see that
\mathcal{P}_{X}
is a regular
*
-semigroup. The involution

{}^{*}:\mathcal{P}_{X}\to\mathcal{P}_{X}:\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{*}

can be defined diagrammatically as a reflection in a horizontal axis; see Figure
3
. Formally,
\alpha^{*}
is obtained from
\alpha
by swapping dashed and undashed vertices,
x\leftrightarrow x^{\prime}
. It is not hard to see that

(\alpha^{*})^{*}=\alpha=\alpha\alpha^{*}\alpha\and(\alpha\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{X}$.}

\alpha=
=\alpha^{*}

Figure 3:
A partition
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
(left) and its image
\alpha^{*}
(right) under the involution of
\mathcal{P}_{6}
.

At this point it is necessary to recall some further notation and terminology. First, we say a non-empty subset
\varnothing\not=A\subseteq X\cup X^{\prime}
is:

•

a
transversal
if
A\cap X\not=\varnothing
and
A\cap X^{\prime}\not=\varnothing
(i.e. if
A
contains both upper and lower vertices),

•

an
upper non-transversal
if
A\subseteq X
(i.e. if
A
contains only upper vertices), or

•

a
lower non-transversal
if
A\subseteq X^{\prime}
(i.e. if
A
contains only lower vertices).

We can then describe a partition
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
using a convenient two-line notation from
[
34
]
. Specifically, we write

\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)_{i\in I,\ j\in J,\ k\in K}

to indicate that
\alpha
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(
i\in I
), upper non-transversals
C_{j}
(
j\in J
) and lower non-transversals
D_{k}
(
k\in K
). We often abbreviate this to
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, with the indexing sets
I
,
J
and
K
being implied, rather than explicitly listed. When
X
is finite we list the blocks of partitions,
viz.

\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\dots&A_{q}&C_{1}&\dots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\dots&B_{q}&D_{1}&\dots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Thus, for example, the partition
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
1
can be expressed as

\beta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&4,5,6&2,3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 4,6&1,2,3&5\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

With this notation, the identity of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
is the partition
\operatorname{id}_{X}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)_{x\in X}
, and the involution is given by

\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\alpha^{*}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Moreover, one can easily see that with
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, and with the notation of Section
3
, we have

{\bf d}(\alpha)=\alpha\alpha^{*}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{i}&C_{j}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and{\bf r}(\alpha)=\alpha^{*}\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{i}&D_{k}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Thus, consulting Lemma
3.8
, it follows that partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
are
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-related (or
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-related) precisely when the ‘top halves’ (or ‘bottom halves’) of
\alpha,\beta
‘match’ in a sense that we again hope is clear. Nevertheless, this ‘matching’ can be formalised by defining some further notation.

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
, we define the
(co)domain
and
(co)kernel
of
\alpha
by:

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{x\in X:x\text{ belongs to a transversal of }\alpha\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{x\in X:x^{\prime}\text{ belongs to a transversal of }\alpha\},

\displaystyle\ker(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in X\times X:x\text{ and }y\text{ belong to the same block of }\alpha\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in X\times X:x^{\prime}\text{ and }y^{\prime}\text{ belong to the same block of }\alpha\}.

The
rank
of
\alpha
, denoted
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)
, is the number of transversals of
\alpha
.
Thus,
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)
are subsets of
X
,
\ker(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)
are equivalences on
X
, and
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)
is a cardinal between
0
and
|X|
. For example, with
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{1,2,3,4\},

\displaystyle\ker(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(1,2,3\mathrel{|}4\mathrel{|}5\mathrel{|}6),

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{1,4,5,6\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(1\mathrel{|}2,3\mathrel{|}4,5,6),

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=2,

where we have indicated equivalences by listing their classes.
The various parts of the following result are contained in
[
154
,
50
,
40
]
, though some of those papers use different terminology.

Lemma 3.50
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{X}
, we have

(i)

\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ [\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=\operatorname{dom}(\beta)
and
\ker(\alpha)=\ker(\beta)]
,

(ii)

\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ [\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)=\operatorname{codom}(\beta)
and
\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)=\operatorname{coker}(\beta)]
,

(iii)

\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta)
.

The
{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}={\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}}
-classes of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
are the sets

D_{\mu}=D_{\mu}(\mathcal{P}_{X})=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{X}:\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\mu\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for cardinals $0\leq\mu\leq|X|$.}

Group
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes contained in
D_{\mu}
are isomorphic to the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}
. ∎

We conclude this section with a brief discussion of the relations
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
on the set
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{X})
of projections of a partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{X}
, beginning with the case
|X|=2
.

Example 3.53
.

Let
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be the projection algebra of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. The six elements of
P
are shown in Figure
7
.
Figure
8
shows the partial order
\leq
on
P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
; as usual for Hasse diagrams, only covering relationships are shown, and the rest can be deduced from transitivity (and reflexivity). Figure
9
shows the relations
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
on
P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, and again one can see that these are not transitive.

In both Figures
8
and
9
, the elements of
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
have been arranged so that each row consists of
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-related elements; cf. Lemma
3.50
. So from top to bottom, the rows correspond to the projections from the
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-classes
D_{2}
,
D_{1}
and
D_{0}
, where

D_{i}=D_{i}(\mathcal{P}_{2})=\{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{2}:\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=i\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i=0,1,2$.}

For
i=0,1,2
, we write
P_{i}=P\cap D_{i}
for the set of projections from
D_{i}
. The right-hand graph in Figure
9
is the graph
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, as defined at the end of Section
3
. We denote this by
\Gamma=\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{2})
; so the vertex set of
\Gamma
is
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, and
\Gamma
has an undirected edge
\{p,q\}
precisely when
p\not=q
and
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
.
As in (
3.13
),
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections are
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-related. Thus,

\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}\sqcup\Gamma_{1}\sqcup\Gamma_{2}

decomposes as the disjoint union of the induced subgraphs
\Gamma_{i}
on the vertex sets
P_{i}
, for
i=0,1,2
. It is clear that each
\Gamma_{i}
is connected, but this is not necessarily the case for an arbitrary regular
*
-semigroup
S
.

Figure 7:
The projections of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Figure 8:
Hasse diagram of the poset
P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, with respect to the order
\leq
given in (
3.6
). An arrow
p\to q
means
p\leq q
.

Figure 9:
Left: the relation
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
on
P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
; an arrow
p\to q
means
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
. Right: the relation
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
on
P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
; an edge
p-q
means
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
. These relations are given in (
3.11
). In both diagrams, loops are omitted.

In general, for a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we still have the graph
\Gamma(S)
, and we still have the decomposition

\Gamma(S)=\bigsqcup_{D\in S/{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}}\Gamma(D),

where
\Gamma(D)
is the induced subgraph of
\Gamma(S)
on vertex set
P(S)\cap D
, for each
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-class
D
of
S
. However, the subgraphs
\Gamma(D)
need not be connected in general. For example, if
S
is inverse, then the
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-relation is trivial; it follows that each
\Gamma(D)
is discrete (has empty edge set), and hence is disconnected if
D
contains more than one idempotent. As another example, if
S=A(\Gamma)
is an adjacency semigroup, as in Example
3.33
, then the graph
\Gamma(S)
is simply
\Gamma
with a new vertex
0
added; the
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-classes of
S
are
D_{0}=\{0\}
and
D_{1}=S\setminus\{0\}=P\times P
, and we have
\Gamma(D_{1})=\Gamma
.
On the other hand, it follows from results of
[
35
]
that the graphs
\Gamma(D)
are connected for any
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-class
D
of a finite partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and this turns out to be equivalent to certain facts about minimal idempotent-generation of the proper ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. We have seen this connectivity property in the case
n=2
, above. Figure
10
shows the graph
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{3})
, produced using the Semigroups package for GAP
[
111
,
51
]
. From left to right, the connected components of this graph are the induced subgraphs
\Gamma(D_{3})
,
\Gamma(D_{2})
,
\Gamma(D_{1})
and
\Gamma(D_{0})
.

Figure 10:
The graph
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{3})
, produced by GAP.

As a special case, the induced subgraph
\Gamma(D_{n-1})
of the graph
\Gamma(\mathcal{P}_{n})
corresponding to the
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-class
D_{n-1}=D_{n-1}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
has a very interesting structure. First, one can easily check that the projections of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
contained in
D_{n-1}
have the form

\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
1
i
n
\pi_{i}=
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
1
j
k
n
and
\pi_{jk}=

for each
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j<k\leq n
. It is also easy to check that the only non-identical
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-relations among these projections are

\pi_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\pi_{ij}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\pi_{j}.

The graph
\Gamma(D_{n-1})
is shown in Figure

in the case
n=5
. In the figure, vertices representing projections
\pi_{i}
or

or

.

Figure 11:
The graph
\Gamma(D_{4})
, where
D_{4}=D_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{5})
.

The idempotent-generated subsemigroup
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{P}_{X})=\langle E(\mathcal{P}_{X})\rangle=\langle P(\mathcal{P}_{X})\rangle
of a partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{X}
was described for finite and infinite
X
in
[
31
]
and
[
34
]
, respectively. One of the main results of
[
31
]
is that finite
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
is (minimally) generated as a monoid by the set

\Omega=\{\pi_{i},\pi_{jk}:1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j<k\leq n\}

of all projections of rank
n-1
, and that in fact

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=\{\operatorname{id}_{n}\}\cup\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n}),

where here
{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}}
is the
singular ideal
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, consisting of all non-invertible elements. The second main result was a presentation for
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
in terms of the above generating set
\Omega
. For example, one of the defining relations was

\pi_{i}\pi_{ij}\pi_{j}\pi_{jk}\pi_{k}\pi_{ki}\pi_{i}=\pi_{i}\pi_{ik}\pi_{k}\pi_{kj}\pi_{j}\pi_{ji}\pi_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for distinct $1\leq i,j,k\leq n$,}

(3.54)

where here we use symmetrical notation
\pi_{st}=\pi_{ts}
. Examining Figure
11
, one can see that the two words in this equation represent two different triangular paths in the graph
\Gamma(D_{n-1})
:

i\rightsquigarrow j\rightsquigarrow k\rightsquigarrow i\and i\rightsquigarrow k\rightsquigarrow j\rightsquigarrow i.

(In representing the above paths we have omitted the intermediate vertices, so
i\rightsquigarrow j
is shorthand for
i\to ij\to j
, and so on.) Since paths in this graph correspond to lists of sequentially
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections, the above words represent factorisations of the form described in Proposition
3.16
, and the corresponding tuples
(\pi_{i},\pi_{ij},\pi_{j},\ldots)
and
(\pi_{i},\pi_{ik},\pi_{k},\ldots)
are examples of what we will later call
P
-paths
; see Section
5.1
. The two factorisations in (
3.54
) represent the following partition, pictured in the case
{i<j<k}
:

\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
1
i
j
k
n

4
Projection algebras

One of the key ideas in this paper is that of a
projection algebra
. Such an algebra consists of a set
P
, along with a family
\theta_{p}
(
p\in P
) of unary operations, one for each element of
P
. These operations are required to satisfy certain axioms, as listed in Definition
4.1
below, and are meant to abstractly model the unary algebras of projections of regular
*
-semigroups encountered in Section
3.1
. This is in fact not a new concept. Indeed, projection algebras have appeared in a number of settings, under a variety of names, including the
P
-groupoids
of Imaoka
[
72
]
, the
P
-sets
of Yamada
[
155
]
, (certain special)
P
-sets
of Nambooripad and Pastijn
[
118
]
, and the
(left and right) projection algebras
of Jones
[
74
]
. (We also mention Yamada’s
p
-systems

[
156
]
; these are defined in a very different way, but for a similar purpose. Strictly speaking, Yamada’s
P
-sets are different to Imaoka’s
P
-groupoids, but are ultimately equivalent.) We prefer Jones’ terminology ‘projection algebras’, since these are indeed algebras, not just sets, though we note that Jones considered
binary
algebras rather than Imaoka’s
unary
algebras, which we use here; see Remark
4.2
. We also only use the term ‘groupoid’ in its usual categorical sense. Imaoka used it in one of its less-common meanings, stemming from the fact that a projection algebra also has a partially defined binary operation; curiously this partial operation plays no role in the current work.

Although the concept of a projection algebra is not new, here we build several new categorical structures on top of such algebras. The most general situation is covered in Section
6
below, where we have an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}
whose object set
v\mathcal{G}=P
is a projection algebra (with certain algebraic and order-theoretic ties between
\mathcal{G}
and
P
). Section
5
lays the foundation for this, by showing how to construct various categories directly from a projection algebra. The main such construction is the
chain groupoid

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
; this will be used in the definition of the groupoids in Section
6
, but we will uncover its deeper categorical significance in the sequel
[
37
]
.

Before all of this, however, we need to discuss projection algebras themselves.
We begin in Section
4.1
by giving/recalling their definition, and establishing some of their important properties. We discuss the category
{\bf PA}
of all projection algebras in Section
4.2
, where in Proposition
4.25
we construct a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
from the category of regular
*
-semigroups. Finally, in Section
4.3
we give a new proof of the known fact
[
72
]
that each (abstract) projection algebra is the unary algebra of projections of some regular
*
-semigroup.

4.1
Definitions and basic properties

Here then is the key definition:

Definition 4.1
.

A
projection algebra
is a set
P
, together with a collection of unary operations
\theta_{p}
(
p\in P
) satisfying the following axioms, for all
p,q\in P
:

(P1)

p\theta_{p}=p
,

(P2)

\theta_{p}\theta_{p}=\theta_{p}
,

(P3)

p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p}
,

(P4)

\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=\theta_{q\theta_{p}}
,

(P5)

\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}
.

The elements of a projection algebra are called
projections
.

Strictly speaking, one should refer to ‘a projection algebra
(P,\theta)
’, but we will almost always use the symbol
\theta
to denote the unary operations of such an algebra, and so typically refer to ‘a projection algebra
P
’. When we need to refer simultaneously to more than one projection algebra (as for example in Definition
4.22
), we will be careful to distinguish the operations.

Remark 4.2
.

It is worth noting at the outset that the collection of unary operations
\theta_{p}
(
p\in P
) of a projection algebra
P
could be replaced by a single binary operation
\diamond
, defined by
q\diamond p=q\theta_{p}
, and resulting in what we will here call a
binary projection algebra
.
(For example, Jones took this binary approach in
[
74
]
, in his study of the more general
P
-restriction semigroups, though his preferred symbol was
\star
, which we reserve for other uses throughout the paper.)
The binary form of the axioms
(P1)
–
(P5)
are as follows:

(P1)
′

p\diamond p=p
,

(P2)
′

(q\diamond p)\diamond p=q\diamond p
,

(P3)
′

(p\diamond q)\diamond p=q\diamond p
,

(P4)
′

((r\diamond p)\diamond q)\diamond p=r\diamond(q\diamond p)
,

(P5)
′

(((r\diamond p)\diamond q)\diamond p)\diamond q=(r\diamond p)\diamond q
.

(Jones’ axioms are slightly different to these, though they are equivalent, as he explains in
[
74
, Section 7]
.)
Note that
(P2)′
requires one more variable than
(P2)
for its quantification; indeed, the latter is expressed in terms of equality of the term function
\theta_{p}\theta_{p}
and the basic operation
\theta_{p}
, which of course means that these functions agree on every input, i.e. that
q\theta_{p}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p}
for all
p,q\in P
. Similar comments apply to
(P4)′
and
(P5)′
. In this way, axioms
(P1)′
–
(P5)′
are all
identities
in the signature of a single binary operation, meaning that binary projection algebras form a
variety
. On the other hand, axioms
(P1)
and
(P3)
do not have the form of identities, as
p\theta_{p}
and
p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
are not
terms
. In fact, unary projection algebras (as in Definition
4.1
)
cannot
be defined by any set of identities, as they do not form varieties; indeed, the elements of a projection algebra are in one-one correspondence with its (unary) operations, and this property is not generally inherited by subalgebras.

Nevertheless, we do prefer the unary approach of Imaoka
[
72
]
, for three main reasons:

(i)

We feel that some of axioms
(P1)
–
(P5)
are clearer than their binary counterparts
(P1)′
–
(P5)′
.
For example, and as discussed in more detail in Remark
6.9
, axiom
(P4)
can be thought of as a rule for ‘iterating’ the unary operations, and we feel that this intuition is somewhat lost in the binary form
(P4)′
, although this is of course entirely subjective.

(ii)

The non-associativity of
\diamond
means that brackets are necessary when working with
\diamond
-terms. On the other hand, associativity of function composition allows us to dispense with bracketing when using the
\theta
maps, and this is a significant advantage when dealing with lengthy terms.

(iii)

In later sections, we will consider groupoids
\mathcal{G}
whose object sets are projection algebras,
{v\mathcal{G}=P}
. One of the key tools when studying such groupoids are certain maps

\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}:P\to P

that are built from the unary operations of
P
and the maps
\vartheta_{a}:{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}
defined in (
2.10
). These
\Theta
maps could certainly be defined in terms of
\diamond
and the
\vartheta
maps,
viz.

p\Theta_{a}=(p\diamond{\bf d}(a))\vartheta_{a},

but their definition as a composition
\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}
is more direct, and leads to more succinct statements and proofs, as for example with Proposition
6.7
and Definition
6.10
.

As we have already observed, axioms
(P1)
–
(P5)
are abstractions of the properties of projections of regular
*
-semigroups. Specifically, we can make the following definition because of Lemma
3.4
.

Definition 4.3
.

The projection algebra of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
has:

•

underlying set
P(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}
, i.e. the set of all projections of
S
, and

•

unary operations
\theta_{p}

(p\in P)
, defined by
q\theta_{p}=pqp
for all
q\in P
.

For the rest of this section we fix a projection algebra
P
, as in Definition
4.1
. In what follows, for
x,y\in P
, we write
x=_{1}y
to indicate that
x=y
by an application of
(P1)
, and similarly for
=_{2}
, and so on.

A number of relations on
P
will play a crucial role in all that follows. The first of these is defined by

\displaystyle p\leq q

\displaystyle\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=p\theta_{q}.

(4.4)

By
(P2)
, it quickly follows that

\displaystyle p\leq q

\displaystyle\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=r\theta_{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $r\in P$.}

(4.5)

Lemma 4.6
.

\leq
is a partial order on
P
.

Proof.

Reflexivity follows from
(P1)
. For anti-symmetry, suppose
p\leq q
and
q\leq p
, so that
p=p\theta_{q}
and
q=q\theta_{p}
. Then

p=_{1}p\theta_{p}=p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=_{3}q\theta_{p}=q.

For transitivity, suppose
p\leq q
and
q\leq r
, so that
p=p\theta_{q}
and
q=q\theta_{r}
. Then

p=p\theta_{q}=p\theta_{q\theta_{r}}=_{4}p\theta_{r}\theta_{q}\theta_{r}.

By (
4.5
), this implies
p\leq r
.
∎

The next lemma gives a simple criteria for the meet
p\wedge q
of two projections
p,q\in P
to exist, with respect to the partial order
\leq
.

Lemma 4.7
.

If
p,q\in P
satisfy
p\theta_{q}=q\theta_{p}
, then
p\wedge q
exists in
(P,{\leq})
, and
p\wedge q=p\theta_{q}=q\theta_{p}
.

Proof.

Let
r=p\theta_{q}=q\theta_{p}
. Certainly
r\leq p,q
by (
4.5
). Now suppose
s\leq p,q
; we must show that
s\leq r
, i.e. that
s=s\theta_{r}
. Since
s\leq p,q
it follows that
s=s\theta_{p}=s\theta_{q}
, and so

s\theta_{r}=s\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}s\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=s,

as required.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Lemma
4.7
gave a sufficient condition for the meet
p\wedge q
to exist, but this condition is not necessary. Indeed, let
S=A(\Gamma)
be the adjacency semigroup of a symmetric, reflexive digraph
\Gamma=(P,E)
, as in Example
3.33
, and suppose distinct vertices
p,q\in P
are connected by an edge in
\Gamma
. Then
p\wedge q=0
(cf. (
3.37
)), yet
p\theta_{q}=q\not=p=q\theta_{p}
(cf. (
3.36
)).

By (
4.5
), the image of the basic operation
\theta_{p}:P\to P
is precisely

\operatorname{im}(\theta_{p})=p^{\downarrow}=\{q\in P:q\leq p\},

(4.9)

the down-set of
p
in the poset
(P,\leq)
.

Lemma 4.10
.

If
p\leq q
, then
\theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
.

Proof.

Since
p=p\theta_{q}
, we have
\theta_{p}=\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}
. The claim then quickly follows from
(P2)
.
∎

An equally important role will be played by two further relations,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
, defined as follows. For
p,q\in P
, we say that

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=q\theta_{p}.

(4.11)

By
(P1)
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
is reflexive, but it need not be symmetric or transitive. We also define

{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}={\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}},

(4.12)

which is the largest symmetric (and reflexive) relation contained in
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
. So

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=q\theta_{p}\text{ and }q=p\theta_{q}.

Lemma 4.13
.

For any
p,q\in P
,

(i)

p\theta_{q}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
,

(ii)

p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
,

(iii)

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Rightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
.

(iv)

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Rightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
and
\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}
.

Proof.

(i)
. We have
p\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}q\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}p\theta_{q}
.

(ii)
. If
p\leq q
, then
p=p\theta_{q}
, so
q\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}q\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{1}q\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}p\theta_{q}=p
.

(iii)
and
(iv)
. These follow immediately from
(P4)
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

The partial order
\leq
from (
4.4
) was used by Imaoka in
[
72
]
, where it was defined by

p\leq q\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ p=p\theta_{q}=q\theta_{p}.

Note that part
(ii)
of the lemma just proved means that the ‘
=q\theta_{p}
’ part of Imaoka’s definition is superfluous. Jones also used the order
\leq
in
[
74
]
, defined exactly as in (
4.4
), albeit in binary form,
p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=p\diamond q
(cf. Remark
4.2
). We are not aware of any previous use of the
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
relation in the literature, however, despite its central importance in the current work. (In binary form we have
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=q\diamond p
).

Remark 4.15
.

Some of the above implications are reversible. For example, we have

(i)

p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}
(cf. Lemma
4.10
), and

(ii)

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
(cf. Lemma
4.13
(iii)
).

Indeed, to establish
(i)
, it suffices (by Lemma
4.10
) to note that

\theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp=_{1}p\theta_{p}=p\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{1}p\theta_{q}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp\leq q.

For the backward implication in
(ii)
, we have

\theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp=_{1}p\theta_{p}=p\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=_{1}p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=_{3}q\theta_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q.

The following simple result will be crucial in later constructions. Roughly speaking, it says that any pair of projections
p,q\in P
sit above an
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related pair
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in P
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
p,q\in P
be arbitrary, and let
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}
and
q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}
. Then

p^{\prime}\leq p,\hskip 20.00003ptq^{\prime}\leq q\and p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q^{\prime}.

Proof.

We obtain
p^{\prime}\leq p
and
q^{\prime}\leq q
directly from (
4.5
). We also have

p^{\prime}\theta_{q^{\prime}}=q\theta_{p}\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}q\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}q\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{3}p\theta_{q}=q^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003ptq^{\prime}\theta_{p^{\prime}}=p^{\prime}.\qed

Although
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
need not be transitive,
\leq
and
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
have some ‘transitivity-like’ properties in combination:

Lemma 4.17
.

If
p,q,r\in P
are such that
p\leq q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
or
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\leq r
, then

(i)

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
,

(ii)

p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p\theta_{r}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we assume that
p\leq q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
. The arguments for the case of
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\leq r
are similar, and are omitted.

Since
p\leq q
and
q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
, we have
p=p\theta_{q}
and
q=r\theta_{q}
. Since also
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
by Lemma
4.13
(ii)
, we also have
p=q\theta_{p}
.

(i)
. We must show that
p=r\theta_{p}
. Since
p\leq q
, Lemma
4.10
gives
\theta_{p}=\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
. Combining all of the above gives
r\theta_{p}=r\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p}=p
.

(ii)
. We have
p\theta_{r}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
by Lemma
4.13
(i)
, so we just need to show that
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p\theta_{r}
, i.e. that
p=(p\theta_{r})\theta_{p}
. But
p\theta_{r}\theta_{p}=r\theta_{p}
by
(P3)
, and we saw in the previous part that
r\theta_{p}=p
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Most of the results of this section have concerned properties of the
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relations on a projection algebra
P
. These relations were defined in (
4.4
), (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), from the algebraic structure of
P
, i.e. in terms of the
\theta
operations. One might wonder if, conversely, the
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relations uniquely determine the algebraic structure of
P
. This turns out not to be the case, however. Indeed, Example
4.31
and Remark
4.35
give two projection algebras on the same underlying set, with the same
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relations, but with different
\theta
operations.

We now record two simple results that will be used in Section
4.3
.

Lemma 4.19
.

For
p,q\in P
, we have
\theta_{p}=\theta_{q}\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=q
.

Proof.

If
\theta_{p}=\theta_{q}
, then
p=_{1}p\theta_{p}\theta_{p}=p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=_{3}q\theta_{p}=q\theta_{q}=_{1}q
. The converse is trivial.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

If
\alpha=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}
and
\beta=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}
, for some
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P
, then
\alpha=\alpha\beta\alpha
and
\beta=\beta\alpha\beta
.

Proof.

By symmetry it suffices to show that
\alpha=\alpha\beta\alpha
, and we do this by induction on
k
. The
k=1
case follows immediately from
(P2)
. Now suppose
k\geq 2
, and write

\alpha^{\prime}=\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\and\beta^{\prime}=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{3}}\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}.

Then
\alpha=\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=_{5}\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=_{4}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdot\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}
, and

\displaystyle\beta\alpha=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{3}}\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdot\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}

\displaystyle=_{2}\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{3}}\cdot\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\cdot\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}

\displaystyle=_{4}\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{3}}\cdot\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}\cdot\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}

\displaystyle=_{2}\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{3}}\cdot\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}\theta_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}\cdot\theta_{p_{3}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}.

Combining the last two calculations with
\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}
(which holds by induction), we obtain

\alpha\beta\alpha=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdot\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha.\qed

Remark 4.21
.

It is worth noting that the proof of Lemma
4.20
was the only place in Section
4.1
that we used
(P5)
.

4.2
The category of projection algebras

We have now proved a number of results on projection algebras themselves, and we now turn to the category they form.

Definition 4.22
.

We denote by
{\bf PA}
the (large) category of projection algebras. A morphism in
{\bf PA}
is a map
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
(for projection algebras
P
and
P^{\prime}
) satisfying

(p\theta_{q})\phi=(p\phi)\theta^{\prime}_{q\phi}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$.}

Here we use
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
to denote the unary operations on
P
and
P^{\prime}
, respectively.

One can also think of a projection algebra morphism as a morphism of binary algebras, in the sense discussed in Remark
4.2
. Indeed, using
\diamond
and
\diamond^{\prime}
to denote the binary operations of
P
and
P^{\prime}
(as in that remark),
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
is a projection algebra morphism if and only if

(p\diamond q)\phi=(p\phi)\diamond^{\prime}(q\phi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$.}

In the next result we also write
\leq
and
\leq^{\prime}
for the partial orders on
P
and
P^{\prime}
, as in (
4.4
), and similarly for the
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relations from (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).

Lemma 4.23
.

If
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
is a projection algebra morphism, then for any
p,q\in P
,

p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ p\phi\leq^{\prime}q\phi,\hskip 20.00003ptp\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Rightarrow\ p\phi\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}^{\prime}q\phi\and p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Rightarrow\ p\phi\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}^{\prime}q\phi.

Proof.

The proofs are all essentially the same, so we just prove the first. For this we have

p\leq q\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp=p\theta_{q}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp\phi=(p\theta_{q})\phi=(p\phi)\theta^{\prime}_{q\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptp\phi\leq^{\prime}q\phi.\qed

Remark 4.24
.

The first implication in Lemma
4.23
says that any projection algebra morphism
P\to P^{\prime}
is a poset morphism
(P,{\leq})\to(P^{\prime},{\leq^{\prime}})
. The converse of this is not true, however, as we will see in Example
4.31
and Remark
4.34
.

The construction of the projection algebra
P(S)
from a regular
*
-semigroup
S
(cf. Definition
4.3
) can be thought of as an object map from the category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups to the category
{\bf PA}
of projection algebras. It is easy to see that any
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
maps projections to projections, so we can define
P(\phi)=\phi|_{P(S)}:P(S)\to P(S^{\prime})
. The next result shows that
P
(interpreted in this way) is a functor:

Proposition 4.25
.

P
is a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
.

Proof.

It remains to check that
P(\phi):P(S)\to P(S^{\prime})
, as above, is a projection algebra morphism. And indeed, for any
p,q\in P(S)
we have

(q\theta_{p})\phi=(pqp)\phi=(p\phi)(q\phi)(p\phi)=(q\phi)\theta_{p\phi}.\qed

4.3
From projection algebras to regular
*
-semigroups

We have already seen that any regular
*
-semigroup
S
naturally defines a projection algebra
P(S)
. We now give a construction to show that we can also go in the reverse direction; Proposition
4.26
below shows that any (abstract) projection algebra is the projection algebra of some regular
*
-semigroup. As we have already noted, this is well known, and follows from results in
[
72
,
74
]
; see also
[
33
,
37
]
for other proofs. We include the present construction for completeness, and because it is considerably simpler than those from
[
72
,
74
,
33
,
37
]
, as those papers concern larger problems than simply exhibiting a regular
*
-semigroup with a given projection algebra.

Consider an arbitrary semigroup
S
, and let
S^{\operatorname{op}}
be the
opposite semigroup
. So the underlying set of
S^{\operatorname{op}}
is
S
, and the product
\star
in
S
is defined by
x\star y=yx
. Multiplication in the direct product
S\times S^{\operatorname{op}}
is given by the rule
(x,y)(u,v)=(xu,vy)
. It is well known, and easy to see, that the operation
(x,y)^{*}=(y,x)
is an involution on
S\times S^{\operatorname{op}}
, meaning that the laws
(a^{*})^{*}=a
and
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
are satisfied.

Now consider a projection algebra
P
. Since an operation
\theta_{p}
is a map
P\to P
, we can think of it as an element of the
full transformation monoid

\mathcal{T}_{P}
. (This is the monoid of
all
maps
P\to P
, under composition.) We define
\mathcal{F}_{P}
to be the subsemigroup of the direct product
\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}
generated by all pairs
(\theta_{p},\theta_{p})
:

\mathcal{F}_{P}=\langle(\theta_{p},\theta_{p}):p\in P\rangle=\{(\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}},\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}):k\geq 1,\ p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P\}\leq\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{P}
is closed under the
∗
operation on
\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}
(defined above), it is an involution semigroup.

Proposition 4.26
.

If
P
is an (abstract) projection algebra, then
\mathcal{F}_{P}
is a regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra (isomorphic to)
P
.

Proof.

We have already observed that the laws
(a^{*})^{*}=a
and
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
are satisfied. For the remaining law, let
a=(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{F}_{P}
, so that
\alpha=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}
and
\beta=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}
for some
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P
. Using Lemma
4.20
, we have
aa^{*}a=(\alpha\beta\alpha,\beta\alpha\beta)=(\alpha,\beta)=a
.

Now that we know
\mathcal{F}_{P}
is a regular
*
-semigroup, we claim that its set of projections is

P(\mathcal{F}_{P})=\{\overline{p}:p\in P\},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where for simplicity we write $\overline{p}=(\theta_{p},\theta_{p})$.}

(4.27)

Clearly each
\overline{p}
is a projection (cf.
(P2)
). Conversely, Lemma
3.3
(i)
tells us that each projection is of the form
aa^{*}
for some
a\in\mathcal{F}_{P}
. Again writing
a=(\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}},\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}})
, we see that

\displaystyle aa^{*}

\displaystyle=(\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\cdot\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}},\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\cdot\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}})

\displaystyle=_{2}(\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k-1}}\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}},\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k-1}}\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}})

\displaystyle=_{4}(\theta_{p_{k}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}},\theta_{p_{k}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}})=\overline{p_{k}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}}

has the claimed form. It remains to check that the map

\phi:P\to P(\mathcal{F}_{P}):p\mapsto\overline{p}

is a projection algebra isomorphism. Surjectivity of
\phi
follows from (
4.27
), and injectivity from Lemma
4.19
. It remains to check that
\phi
is a projection algebra morphism, i.e. that

\overline{q\theta_{p}}=\overline{q}\theta_{\overline{p}}
for all
p,q\in P
,
and for this we have

\overline{q\theta_{p}}=(\theta_{q\theta_{p}},\theta_{q\theta_{p}})=_{4}(\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p},\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p})=(\theta_{p},\theta_{p})(\theta_{q},\theta_{q})(\theta_{p},\theta_{p})=\overline{p}\ \overline{q}\ \overline{p}=\overline{q}\theta_{\overline{p}}.\qed

Remark 4.28
.

We will see in
[
33
]
that the semigroup
\mathcal{F}_{P}
constructed above is (up to isomorphism) the unique fundamental, idempotent-generated regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra (isomorphic to)
P
.

Remark 4.29
.

One might wonder if the construction
P\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{P}
is the object part of a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
. This turns out not to be the case. Indeed, if there was such a functor, any morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
in
{\bf PA}
would need to induce a
*
-morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{F}_{P}\to\mathcal{F}_{P^{\prime}}
under which
(\theta_{p},\theta_{p})\Phi=(\theta^{\prime}_{p\phi},\theta^{\prime}_{p\phi})
for all
p\in P
. This map being well defined is equivalent to the implication

\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=\theta_{q_{1}}\cdots\theta_{q_{l}}\text{ \ (in $P$)}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\theta^{\prime}_{p_{1}\phi}\cdots\theta^{\prime}_{p_{k}\phi}=\theta^{\prime}_{q_{1}\phi}\cdots\theta^{\prime}_{q_{l}\phi}\text{ \ (in $P^{\prime}$)}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p_{i},q_{i}\in P$.}

(4.30)

To see that this does not always hold, let
P=\{p,q\}
be the projection algebra in which
\theta_{p}
and
\theta_{q}
are both constant maps, and let
P^{\prime}=P(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be the projection algebra of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
; cf. Example
3.53
. One can easily check that the mapping

}}(=q^{\prime}).

In the sequel
[
37
]
, we will exhibit a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
that is in fact a left adjoint to the (forgetful) functor
P:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
from Proposition
4.25
. This shows that
{\bf PA}
is (isomorphic to) a coreflective subcategory of
{\bf RSS}
, and demonstrates the existence of a free regular
*
-semigroup with a given projection algebra. We will say more about this in Section
10.2
.

4.4
Another example

We conclude Section
4
with another interesting projection algebra, again included to illustrate the above ideas, and highlight some subtleties; see Remarks
4.34
,
4.35
and
5.19
. The example was communicated to us by Michael Kinyon, who discovered it using Mace4
[
107
]
.

Example 4.31
.

Consider the five-element set
P=\{1,e,p,q,z\}
, and define the following unary operations on
P
, each written using standard two-line notation for mappings (for now the reader can ignore the colouring of
\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p}=z\theta_{e}
):

\theta_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&e&p&q&z\\
1&e&p&q&z\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{e}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&e&p&q&z\\
e&e&p&q&\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{p}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&e&p&q&z\\
p&p&p&p&p\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{q}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&e&p&q&z\\
q&q&q&q&q\end{smallmatrix}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{z}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&e&p&q&z\\
z&z&z&z&z\end{smallmatrix}\right).

(4.32)

One can easily check (by hand, or with a computer) that these give
P
the structure of a projection algebra. Note that
\theta_{1}
is the identity map, while
\theta_{p}
,
\theta_{q}
and
\theta_{z}
are all constant maps. The multiplication table for
P
, considered as a binary algebra under
u\diamond v=u\theta_{v}
(cf. Remark
4.2
), is as follows (and again the reader can ignore the colouring of
\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p}=z\diamond e
):

\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc}\diamond&1&e&p&q&z\\
\hline\cr 1&1&e&p&q&z\\
e&e&e&p&q&z\\
p&p&p&p&q&z\\
q&q&q&p&q&z\\
z&z&\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p}&p&q&z\end{array}

(4.33)

Figure
12
shows the Hasse diagram of the
\leq
order on
P
, and also the
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relations; cf. (
4.4
), (
4.11
) and (
4.12
). The diagrams for
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
omit the loops at each vertex.

p
q

Figure 12:
The relations
\leq
(left),
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
(middle) and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
(right) on the projection algebra
P=\{1,e,p,q,z\}
from Example
4.31
.

The construction outlined in Section
4.3
(cf. Proposition
4.26
) yields the regular
*
-semigroup

\mathcal{F}_{P}=\langle\overline{1},\overline{e},\overline{p},\overline{q},\overline{z}\rangle\leq\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}

with projection algebra (isomorphic to)
P
, where again
\overline{u}=(\theta_{u},\theta_{u})\in\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}
for
u\in P
. Simple computations with GAP
[
51
,
111
]
show that this semigroup
\mathcal{F}_{P}
is in fact a band of size
11
. The element
\overline{1}=(\operatorname{id}_{P},\operatorname{id}_{P})
is of course the identity of
\mathcal{F}_{P}
, while
\overline{p}
,
\overline{q}
and
\overline{z}
generate a
3\times 3
rectangular sub-band of
\mathcal{F}_{P}
. Figure
13
shows an egg-box diagram for
\mathcal{F}_{P}
. Egg-box diagrams are standard visualisation tools in semigroup theory; see for example
[
19
]
. Each cell of the diagram represents an
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-class (all singletons in this case); rows and columns are
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
- and
\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}
-classes, respectively; entire boxes are
\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}
-classes; the ordering on
{\mathrel{\mathscr{D}}}({}={\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}})
-classes is given by
D_{a}\leq D_{b}\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\in S^{1}bS^{1}
.
