Egg-box diagram for the regular
*
-semigroup
\mathcal{F}_{P}
, for the projection algebra
P=\{1,e,p,q,z\}
from Example
4.31
.

Remark 4.34
.

It follows from Lemma
4.23
that any projection algebra automorphism of
P
(as in Example
4.31
) is an order isomorphism of the poset
(P,{\leq})
. Examining Figure
12
(left), it is easy to see that there are two of the latter; as well as the identity map, there is the transposition
\phi:P\to P
swapping
p
and
q
. However, this
\phi
is not a projection algebra morphism, as for example we have

(z\diamond e)\phi=p\phi=q\hskip 20.00003pt\text{while}\hskip 20.00003ptz\phi\diamond e\phi=z\diamond e=p.

Remark 4.35
.

One can modify the definition of the projection algebra
P=\{1,e,p,q,z\}
from Example
4.31
by defining
z\theta_{e}
to be
q
rather than
p
, i.e. by changing the red
\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}p}
to
\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}q}
in (
4.32
) and (
4.33
). Denote the resulting projection algebra by
P^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that the
\leq
and
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
(and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
) relations on
P
and
P^{\prime}
are exactly the same, even though the operations on
P
and
P^{\prime}
are (slightly) different. It follows from this that the relations
\leq
and
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
do not uniquely determine the operations on a projection algebra (cf. Remark
4.18
). On the other hand,
P
and
P^{\prime}
are clearly isomorphic, so we are left with the question of whether the relations
\leq
and
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
determine the algebraic structure of a projection algebra up to isomorphism.

5
The chain groupoid of a projection algebra

We have seen that a regular
*
-semigroup
S
induces a projection algebra
P=P(S)
and an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
. The pair
(P,\mathcal{G})
provides
almost
enough information to construct a total invariant of
S
. The missing ingredient is a certain ‘link’ between
P
and
\mathcal{G}
, which is provided by a functor
\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
is the so-called
chain groupoid
of
P
, the subject of this section. We begin in Section
5.1
by defining the
path category

\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
, and then in Section
5.2
construct
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}
as a quotient of
\mathscr{P}
by a certain congruence
\approx
. The groupoid
\mathscr{C}
is a regular
*
-analogue of Nambooripad’s groupoid of
E
-chains
[
114
]
. As we explain in Remark
5.19
, we can think of
\mathscr{C}
as a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf OG}
, from projection algebras to ordered groupoids.

5.1
The path category

For the duration of this section we fix a projection algebra
P
, as in Definition
4.1
, including the operations
\theta_{p}
(
p\in P
), and the relations
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
from (
4.4
), (
4.11
) and (
4.12
).
Roughly speaking, we wish to provide an abstract setting in which we can think about ‘products’ of projections
p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
, even though
P
itself does not have a binary operation. Guided by the (as-yet unproved) Proposition
3.16
, we are (for now) solely interested in such ‘products’ in the case that
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}
. The
path category

\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
is the first attempt at doing all of this; it represents such a ‘product’ as a tuple
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, which is considered as a morphism
p_{1}\to p_{k}
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
P
-path
in a projection algebra
P
(cf. Definition
4.1
) is a tuple

\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})\in P^{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $k\geq 1$, such that $p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{2}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}$.}

(Since
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
need not be transitive, this does not imply that the
p_{i}
are
all

\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related.) We say that
\mathfrak{p}
is a
P
-path from
p_{1}
to
p_{k}
, and write
{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{1}
and
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{k}
. We identify each
p\in P
with the path
(p)
of length
1
.

The
path category
of
P
is the
*
-category
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
of all
P
-paths, with object set
v\mathscr{P}=P
, under the following operations:

•

For
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
with
p_{k}=q_{1}
, we define

\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1},p_{k}=q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}).

•

For
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}
, we define

\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}=(p_{k},\ldots,p_{1}),

the reverse of
\mathfrak{p}
. (It is convenient to write
\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}
instead of
\mathfrak{p}^{*}
, and it is clear that conditions
(I1)
–
(I3)
from Definition
2.1
all hold.)

So a morphism set
\mathscr{P}(p,q)
consists of all
P
-paths from
p
to
q
. The identities are the paths of the form
p\equiv(p)
. Although
\mathscr{P}
is a
*
-category, it is not a groupoid, as when
\mathfrak{p}
has length
k\geq 2
,
\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}
has length
2k-1>k
. However, and as noted above, a very important role will be played by a certain groupoid quotient of
\mathscr{P}
; see Definition
5.17
.

Remark 5.2
.

It is also worth noting that
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)

is (isomorphic to) the free
*
-category over the relation
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
in the following sense. We define
{\Gamma=\Gamma_{P}}
to be the digraph with vertex set
P
, and an edge
x_{pq}:p\to q
for each
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
. As in
[
97
, p. 49]
, the
free category

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\Gamma)
has object set
v\mathcal{C}=P
, and its morphisms are the paths in
\Gamma
, including the empty path at each vertex
p\in P
(which are identified with
p
, as usual). Non-empty paths can be thought of as words
x_{p_{1}p_{2}}x_{p_{2}p_{3}}\cdots x_{p_{k-1}p_{k}}
(note the matching subscripts between successive letters), and composition of paths is given by concatenation when the endpoints match. The involution of
\mathcal{C}
is given by reversal of paths; this is well-defined since
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is symmetric. It is then clear that

x_{p_{1}p_{2}}x_{p_{2}p_{3}}\cdots x_{p_{k-1}p_{k}}\to(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})

defines a
*
-isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\to\mathscr{P}
.

We now wish to show that
\mathscr{P}
is an
ordered

*
-category, using Lemma
2.3
. To apply the lemma, we need a partial order on the object set
v\mathscr{P}=P
, and a collection of (left) restrictions
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}
. We already have the order
\leq
on
P
given in (
4.4
). To define the restrictions, consider a
P
-path
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, and suppose
q\in P
is such that
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{1}
. We define a tuple

{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptq_{1}=q\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq_{i}=q_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}\text{ for $2\leq i\leq k$.}

(5.3)

Note that
q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. In fact, since
q\leq p_{1}
we have
q=q\theta_{p_{1}}
, so that

q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k$.}

(5.4)

It follows immediately that
q_{i}\leq p_{i}
for all
i
. We now prove a sequence of lemmas that will verify axioms
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
from Lemma
2.3
.

Lemma 5.5
.

If
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
, and if
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
, then
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
. Moreover,
{\bf d}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})=q
and
{{\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})\leq{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
, as above. Since
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i<k
, and we must show that
q_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q_{i+1}
for all such
i
, i.e. that

q_{i}\theta_{q_{i+1}}=q_{i+1}\and q_{i+1}\theta_{q_{i}}=q_{i}.

For the first, we have

q_{i}\theta_{q_{i+1}}=q_{i}\theta_{q_{i}\theta_{p_{i+1}}}=_{4}q_{i}\theta_{p_{i+1}}\theta_{q_{i}}\theta_{p_{i+1}}=_{3}p_{i+1}\theta_{q_{i}}\theta_{p_{i+1}}=_{3}q_{i}\theta_{p_{i+1}}=q_{i+1}.

For the second, it follows from (
5.4
) that
q_{i}=_{1}q_{i}\theta_{p_{i}}
. Combining this with
p_{i}=p_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}
(as
{p_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{i+1}}
), we have

q_{i+1}\theta_{q_{i}}=q_{i}\theta_{p_{i+1}}\theta_{q_{i}}=_{3}p_{i+1}\theta_{q_{i}}=p_{i+1}\theta_{q_{i}\theta_{p_{i}}}=_{4}p_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{q_{i}}\theta_{p_{i}}=p_{i}\theta_{q_{i}}\theta_{p_{i}}=_{3}q_{i}\theta_{p_{i}}=q_{i}.

This all shows that
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
.

By definition, we have
{\bf d}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})=q_{1}=q
, and
{\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})=q_{k}\leq p_{k}={\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})
, where the latter follows from the fact, noted above, that
q_{i}\leq p_{i}
for all
i
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

If
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
and
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
, and if
r={\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})
, then
({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})^{\textup{rev}}={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
, so that
r=q_{k}
. We also of course have
\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}=(p_{k},\ldots,p_{1})
and
({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})^{\textup{rev}}=(q_{k},\ldots,q_{1})
.
To make the subscripts match up, it is convenient to write
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}=(r_{k},\ldots,r_{1})
. So

q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\and r_{i}=r\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k$.}

(5.7)

We show by descending induction that
q_{i}=r_{i}
for all
i
. The
i=k
case is trivial, as
r_{k}=r=q_{k}
. For
1\leq i<k
, we have

\displaystyle r_{i}

\displaystyle=r_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}

by (
5.7
)

\displaystyle=q_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}

by induction

\displaystyle=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{p_{i+1}}\theta_{p_{i}}

by (
5.7
)

\displaystyle=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}

by Lemma
4.13
(iv)

\displaystyle=q_{i}

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eq:qiri}.}\qed

Lemma 5.8
.

If
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
and
q={\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
, then
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, so that
q=p_{1}
. Then
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
, where each
q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}
, and we must show that
p_{i}=q_{i}
for all
i
. This is clear for
i=1
. For
i\geq 2
, we have

q_{i}=q_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}=p_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}=p_{i},

by definition, induction, and the fact that
p_{i}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{i-1}
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

If
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
and
r\leq q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
, then
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof.

Write

\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}),\hskip 20.00003pt{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}),\hskip 20.00003pt{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})\and{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}).

So

q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}},\hskip 20.00003ptr_{i}=r\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\and s_{i}=r\theta_{q_{1}}\cdots\theta_{q_{i}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k$,}

(5.10)

and we must show that
r_{i}=s_{i}
for all
i
. For
i=1
we have
r_{1}=r=s_{1}
. For
i\geq 2
,

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=s_{i-1}\theta_{q_{i}}

by (
5.10
)

\displaystyle=(s_{i-1}\theta_{q_{i-1}})\theta_{q_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}}

by (
5.10
), noting that
s_{i-1}\leq q_{i-1}

\displaystyle=s_{i-1}\theta_{q_{i-1}}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{q_{i-1}}\theta_{p_{i}}

by
(P4)

\displaystyle=s_{i-1}\theta_{q_{i-1}}\theta_{p_{i}}

by
(P5)

\displaystyle=s_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}

by (
5.10
)

\displaystyle=r_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}

by induction

\displaystyle=r_{i}

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eq:pqrsi}.}\qed

Lemma 5.11
.

If
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathscr{P}
with
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{q})
, and if
r\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
and
s={\bf r}({}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})
, then

{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q})={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}\circ{}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{q}.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
, noting that
p_{k}=q_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}

\displaystyle=(r,r\theta_{p_{2}},r\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}},\ldots,r\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{so}\hskip 20.00003pts=r\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q})

\displaystyle={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l})

\displaystyle=(r,r\theta_{p_{2}},r\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}},\ldots,r\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=s,s\theta_{q_{2}},s\theta_{q_{2}}\theta_{q_{3}},\ldots,s\theta_{q_{2}}\cdots\theta_{q_{l}})

\displaystyle=(r,r\theta_{p_{2}},r\theta_{p_{2}}\theta_{p_{3}},\ldots,r\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}})\circ(s,s\theta_{q_{2}},s\theta_{q_{2}}\theta_{q_{3}},\ldots,s\theta_{q_{2}}\cdots\theta_{q_{l}})={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}\circ{}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{q}.\qed

Proposition 5.12
.

For any projection algebra
P
, the path category
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
is an ordered
*
-category, with ordering given by

\mathfrak{p}\leq\mathfrak{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{p}={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $r\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{q})$.}

Proof.

This follows from an application of Lemma
2.3
. The ordering on
v\mathscr{P}=P
is given in (
4.4
). Properties
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
were established in Lemmas
5.5
–
5.11
.
∎

As usual (cf. Remark
2.5
), we can use the involution to define a right-handed restriction:

\mathfrak{p}{\downharpoonright}_{q}=({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}})^{\textup{rev}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}$ and $q\leq{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})$.}

(5.13)

Explicitly, if
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
q\leq{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{k}
, then

\mathfrak{p}{\downharpoonright}_{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptq_{i}=q\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k$.}

(5.14)

5.2
The chain groupoid

We are now almost ready to define the
chain groupoid

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
associated to a projection algebra
P
. This groupoid is defined below as a certain quotient
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}
of the path category
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
from Definition
5.1
. The congruence
\approx
is defined by specifying a generating set:

Definition 5.15
.

Given a projection algebra
P
(cf. Definition
4.1
), let
\Omega=\Omega(P)
be the set of all pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
of the following two forms:

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
1)

\mathfrak{s}=(p,p)
and
\mathfrak{t}=(p)\equiv p
, for some
p\in P
,

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
2)

\mathfrak{s}=(p,q,p)
and
\mathfrak{t}=(p)\equiv p
, for some
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
.

We define
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
to be the congruence on
\mathscr{P}
generated by
\Omega
.

Since
{\bf d}(\mathfrak{s})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{t})
and
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{s})={\bf r}(\mathfrak{t})
for all
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
, it follows that
\approx
is a
v
-congruence.

Lemma 5.16
.

\approx
is an ordered
*
-congruence.

Proof.

We must show that
\approx
satisfies conditions
(C4)
and
(C5)
from Definition
2.6
. By Lemma
2.16
, it suffices to show that the generating set
\Omega
satisfies (
2.17
) and (
2.18
).

For (
2.17
), we must show that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega\ \Rightarrow\ \mathfrak{s}^{\textup{rev}}\approx\mathfrak{t}^{\textup{rev}}
for all
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
. But this is immediate, as
\mathfrak{s}^{\textup{rev}}=\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\textup{rev}}=\mathfrak{t}
for all such
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
.

For (
2.18
), let
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
. We must show that

{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}\approx{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $r\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{s})$.}

We do this separately for the two forms the pair
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
can take.

(

Ω

1)
. Here we have
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}=(r,r)
and
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t}=(r)
. In particular,
({}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s},{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
, so certainly
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}\approx{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t}
.

(

Ω

2)
. To calculate
{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}(p,q,p)
, we first note that
r\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=r\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=r\theta_{p}=r
, where we used
r\leq p
in the first and third steps, and Lemma
4.13
(iv)
in the second. We then have

{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}=(r,r\theta_{q},r\theta_{q}\theta_{p})=(r,r\theta_{q},r)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and again}\hskip 20.00003pt{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t}=(r).

So once again we have
({}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s},{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
.
∎

It follows quickly from iterating
(

Ω

2)
that

\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}\approx{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}$.}

Combining this with Lemmas
2.7
and
5.16
, it follows that the quotient
\mathscr{P}/{\approx}
is an ordered groupoid.

Definition 5.17
.

The
chain groupoid
of a projection algebra
P
(cf. Definition
4.1
) is the quotient

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)=\mathscr{P}/{\approx},

where
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
is the path category of
P
(cf. Definition
5.1
), and where
\approx
is the congruence given in Definition
5.15
.

•

The elements of
\mathscr{C}
, which are
\approx
-classes of
P
-paths, are called
P
-chains
. For
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
, we write
[\mathfrak{p}]\in\mathscr{C}
for the
\approx
-class of
\mathfrak{p}
. If
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, then we write
[\mathfrak{p}]=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]
. We then have
{\bf d}[\mathfrak{p}]={\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{1}
and
{\bf r}[\mathfrak{p}]={\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{k}
.

•

For
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
with
p_{k}=q_{1}
, we have

[\mathfrak{p}]\circ[\mathfrak{q}]=[\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q}]=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1},p_{k}=q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}].

•

For
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}
, we have

[\mathfrak{p}]^{-1}=[\mathfrak{p}^{\textup{rev}}]=[p_{k},\ldots,p_{1}].

•

The order in
\mathscr{C}
is given by

\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{d}\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{p}\leq\mathfrak{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{c}$ and $\mathfrak{q}\in\mathfrak{d}$.}

The next result shows that any projection algebra morphism naturally induces a functor between the corresponding chain groupoids.

Proposition 5.18
.

If
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
is a projection algebra morphism, then there is a well-defined ordered groupoid functor

\Phi=\mathscr{C}(\phi):\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\Phi=[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi].

Proof.

During the proof we write
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})
, and similarly for
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
4.23
, we have a well-defined functor

\varphi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\varphi=[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi].

We first note that
{\approx}\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
. Indeed, for any pair
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
we have
(\mathfrak{s}\varphi,\mathfrak{t}\varphi)\in\Omega^{\prime}
(the generating set for the corresponding congruence on
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}=\mathscr{P}(P^{\prime})
). It follows that we have a well-defined functor

\Phi:\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt[\mathfrak{p}]\Phi=\mathfrak{p}\varphi,

and this is of course the map in the statement of the proposition. It is easy to check that
\phi
being a morphism implies
({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c})\Phi={}_{q\phi}{\downharpoonleft}(\mathfrak{c}\Phi)
for all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}
and
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})
, so that
\Phi
is ordered.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

As in Propositions
3.27
and
4.25
, it follows from Proposition
5.18
that we can think of
\mathscr{C}
as a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf OG}
. (Verification that
\mathscr{C}(\phi\circ\phi^{\prime})=\mathscr{C}(\phi)\circ\mathscr{C}(\phi^{\prime})
for composable morphisms
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
in
{\bf PA}
is routine.)

Interestingly, the functor
\mathscr{C}
is not injective (on objects). For example, let
P=\{1,e,p,q,z\}
be the projection algebra from Example
4.31
, and let
P^{\prime}
be the closely related projection algebra from Remark
4.35
, defined on the same underlying set. Since
P
and
P^{\prime}
have exactly the same
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
relation,
\mathscr{C}(P)
and
\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})
are identical as groupoids.
In fact, they are identical as
ordered
groupoids. Indeed,
P
and
P^{\prime}
have the same
\leq
order, as pictured in Figure
12
(left), and it follows that the only non-trivial restrictions in the two groupoids are
{}_{s}{\downharpoonleft}[1]=[1]{\downharpoonright}_{s}=[s]
(
s=e,p,q,z
) and
{}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}[e]=[e]{\downharpoonright}_{t}=[t]
(
t=p,q
).

The functor
\mathscr{C}
is also not surjective on morphisms, meaning that the image of
{\bf PA}
under
\mathscr{C}
is not a
full
category of
{\bf OG}
. Indeed, consider again the projection algebra
P
from Example
4.31
, and let
\phi
be the order-isomorphism of
(P,{\leq})
swapping
p\leftrightarrow q
, and fixing
1,e,z
pointwise. Then it is easy to see that there is a well-defined ordered functor
\Phi:\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathscr{C}(P)
given by
[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\Phi=[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]
. (That this is a functor follows from the form of chains in
\mathscr{C}(P)
, and that it is ordered again follows from considering the non-trivial restrictions in
\mathscr{C}(P)
.) However, this
\Phi
is not in the image of
\mathscr{C}
, as
\phi=v\Phi
is not a projection algebra morphism, as explained in Remark
4.35
.

Remark 5.20
.

As in Remark
5.2
, we can think of the unordered reduct of a chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}(P)
as the free groupoid over the relation
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\setminus\Delta_{P}
. This time we take
\Gamma^{\prime}_{P}
to be the digraph with vertex set
P
, and an edge
x_{pq}:p\to q
for each
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\setminus\Delta_{P}
. The free groupoid over
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\setminus\Delta_{P}
consists of the paths in
\Gamma^{\prime}_{P}
(as in Remark
5.2
), modulo the relations
x_{pq}x_{qp}=p
(where the latter is identified with the empty path at
p
). Free categories and groupoids over graphs need not be ordered in general, but the projection algebra axioms ensure that those of the form
\mathscr{P}(P)
and
\mathscr{C}(P)
are.

6
Chained projection groupoids

In Sections
3
and
4
we constructed functors

\mathcal{G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf OG}\and P:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}.

At the object level, these functors map a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to the ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
and projection algebra
P(S)
from Definitions
3.19
and
4.3
. It follows from the examples considered in Section
3.3
that neither
\mathcal{G}
nor
P
is injective (on objects).
The purpose of the current section is to provide an appropriate enrichment of ordered groupoids in order to obtain an injective functor from regular
*
-semigroups to a suitable category of groupoids. We do this in stages, beginning with
weak projection groupoids
(Section
6.1
),
projection groupoids
(Section
6.2
) and
weak chained projection groupoids
(Section
6.3
), eventually culminating in the category
{\bf CPG}
of
chained projection groupoids
(Section
6.4
). The main result of the current section is Theorem
6.42
, which establishes the existence of a functor
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
. In Section
7
we construct a functor
{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
in the opposite direction, and in Section
8
we show that
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
are mutually inverse isomorphisms between the categories
{\bf RSS}
and
{\bf CPG}
.

6.1
Weak projection groupoids

In all that follows, we are concerned with ordered groupoids whose object sets form projection algebras. The most basic situation is as follows:

Definition 6.1
.

A
weak projection groupoid
is a pair
(P,\mathcal{G})
, consisting of an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and a projection algebra
P=v\mathcal{G}
, for which
the restriction to
P
of the order on
\mathcal{G}
is the relation
\leq
from (
4.4
).

Given a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
, the ordering on
\mathcal{G}
is inherited from that of
P
in the sense described in Lemma
2.3
. That is, for each
a\in\mathcal{G}
and each
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
we have a left restriction
{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\in\mathcal{G}}
, with respect to which conditions
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
hold. We also have the right restrictions
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}=({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}a^{-1})^{-1}
, defined for
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, and these satisfy the duals of
(ii)(O1)′
–
(ii)(O5)′
. We typically use these conditions without explicit reference, and also
(O6)′
and its dual, which follow from the others.

In a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
, the only ‘link’ between the structures of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and the projection algebra
P=v\mathcal{G}
is at the order-theoretic level. In particular, the link does not incorporate the algebraic structure of
P
. Indeed, while the order on
P
is defined
from
the
\theta
operations (cf. (
4.4
)), the order does not
determine
the
\theta
operations. (We saw in Examples
3.33
,
3.39
and
4.31
that the same order can arise from different projection algebra structures defined on a common set.) In what follows, we will be particularly interested in groupoid/projection algebra pairs with a stronger,
algebraic
link. Such a link manifests itself in a number of equivalent properties listed in Proposition
6.7
below, and stated in terms of certain additional maps
P\to P
whose definition we now give.

Consider a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
, and a morphism
a\in\mathcal{G}
. As in (
2.10
), we have the map

\vartheta_{a}:{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003ptp\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a).

Since
{\bf d}(a)\in P
, we also have the unary operation
\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}
, and by (
4.9
) its image is
{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}
. It follows that we can compose
\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}
with
\vartheta_{a}
, and we denote this composition by

\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}:P\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}.

(6.2)

As a special case, note that by Lemma
2.14
(ii)
, we have

\Theta_{p}=\theta_{{\bf d}(p)}\vartheta_{p}=\theta_{p}\operatorname{id}_{p^{\downarrow}}=\theta_{p}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any projection $p\in P$.}

(6.3)

We begin by recording some basic properties of the
\Theta
maps.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a weak projection groupoid, then for any
a\in\mathcal{G}
we have:

(i)

\vartheta_{a}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}=\vartheta_{a}
,

(ii)

\Theta_{a}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}=\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\Theta_{a}
,

(iii)

\Theta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}
,

(iv)

\Theta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}=\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}
for any
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
.

Proof.

(i)
. Since
\operatorname{im}(\vartheta_{a})={\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}
, this follows immediately from the fact that each operation
\theta_{p}
fixes each element of
\operatorname{im}(\theta_{p})=p^{\downarrow}
.

(ii)
. Since
\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}
, we obtain
\Theta_{a}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}=\Theta_{a}
from part
(i)
, and
\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\Theta_{a}=\Theta_{a}
from
(P2)
.

(iii)
. Since
\operatorname{im}(\theta_{{\bf d}(a)})={\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}
, it follows from Lemma
2.12
that

\Theta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\operatorname{id}_{{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}.

(iv)
. We have

\displaystyle\Theta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}=\theta_{{\bf d}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)}\vartheta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}

\displaystyle=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}|_{p^{\downarrow}}

by Lemma
2.14
(iii)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}

as
p^{\downarrow}=\operatorname{im}(\theta_{p})
by (
4.9
)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p}\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}

by Lemma
4.10
, as
p\leq{\bf d}(a)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}.

Another important property is that when
a
and
b
are composable in
\mathcal{G}
, the map
\Theta_{a\circ b}
is the composition of
\Theta_{a}
and
\Theta_{b}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a weak projection groupoid, and if
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
are such that
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
{\Theta_{a\circ b}=\Theta_{a}\Theta_{b}}
.

Proof.

Write
p={\bf d}(a)
and
q={\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
. Then by Lemmas
2.14
(i)
and
6.4
(i)
we have

\Theta_{a}\Theta_{b}=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}\theta_{q}\vartheta_{b}=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}\vartheta_{b}=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{b}=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a\circ b}=\Theta_{a\circ b}.\qed

Part
(iv)
of Lemma
6.4
above shows how the
\Theta
maps interact with left restrictions, but the lemma did not include a corresponding statement concerning right restrictions. Of course if
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, then
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
where
p=q\vartheta_{a^{-1}}
(cf. (
2.11
)), and then

\Theta_{a{\downharpoonright}_{q}}=\Theta_{{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a}=\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}=\theta_{q\vartheta_{a^{-1}}}\Theta_{a}.

(6.6)

However, the groupoids we will be concerned with satisfy the neater identity
\Theta_{a{\downharpoonright}_{q}}=\Theta_{a}\theta_{q}
. This is in fact equivalent to a number of additional properties linking the groupoid structure of
\mathcal{G}
to the projection algebra structure of
P=v\mathcal{G}
:

Proposition 6.7
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a weak projection groupoid, then the following are equivalent:

(G1a)

\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}
for all
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
,

(G1b)

\theta_{p\Theta_{a}}=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}
for all
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p\in P
,

(G1c)

\Theta_{a{\downharpoonright}_{q}}=\Theta_{a}\theta_{q}
for all
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
,

(G1d)

\vartheta_{a}
is a projection algebra morphism (and hence isomorphism)
{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}
for all
a\in\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

For the duration of the proof we fix
a\in\mathcal{G}
, and we write
s={\bf d}(a)
and
t={\bf r}(a)
. On a number of occasions we will use the fact that

\Theta_{a^{-1}}\Theta_{a}=\Theta_{a^{-1}\circ a}=\Theta_{{\bf r}(a)}=\Theta_{t}=\theta_{t}.

(6.8)

In the above calculation we used Lemma
6.5
and (
6.3
). In what follows, we show that
(G1a)
implies each of
(G1b)
–
(G1d)
, and conversely that any of
(G1b)
–
(G1d)
implies
(G1a)
.

(G1a)
\ \Rightarrow\
(G1b)
–
(G1d)
. Suppose first that
(G1a)
holds. To verify
(G1b)
, let
p\in P
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\theta_{p\Theta_{a}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{(p\theta_{s})\vartheta_{a}}

by definition

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p\theta_{s}}\Theta_{a}

by
(G1a)
, as
p\theta_{s}\leq s={\bf d}(a)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{s}\theta_{p}\theta_{s}\Theta_{a}

by
(P4)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{{\bf r}(a^{-1})}\theta_{p}\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\Theta_{a}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}

by Lemma
6.4
(ii)
.

For
(G1c)
, if
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
then

\displaystyle\Theta_{a{\downharpoonright}_{q}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{q\vartheta_{a^{-1}}}\Theta_{a}

by (
6.6
)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\theta_{q}\Theta_{a^{-1}}\Theta_{a}

by
(G1a)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\theta_{q}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}

by (
6.8
)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\theta_{q}

by Lemma
4.10
, as
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
.

For
(G1d)
, since
\vartheta_{a}
is a bijection
s^{\downarrow}\to t^{\downarrow}
by Lemma
2.12
, it is enough to show that
\vartheta_{a}
is a projection algebra morphism, i.e. that

\displaystyle(p\theta_{q})\vartheta_{a}

\displaystyle=(p\vartheta_{a})\theta_{q\vartheta_{a}}

for all
p,q\leq s={\bf d}(a)
.

But for any such
p,q
, we have

\displaystyle(p\vartheta_{a})\theta_{q\vartheta_{a}}

\displaystyle=p\vartheta_{a}\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{q}\Theta_{a}

by
(G1a)

\displaystyle=p\vartheta_{a}\theta_{t}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{q}\theta_{s}\vartheta_{a}

by definition of the
\Theta
maps

\displaystyle=p\vartheta_{a}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{q}\vartheta_{a}

as
p\vartheta_{a}\leq{\bf r}(a)=t
and
q\leq s
(cf. Lemma
4.10
)

\displaystyle=p\theta_{q}\vartheta_{a}

by Lemma
2.12
.

(G1b)
\ \Rightarrow\
(G1a)
. This is immediate, since for any
p\leq{\bf d}(a)=s
we have
p\vartheta_{a}=(p\theta_{s})\vartheta_{a}=p\Theta_{a}
.

(G1c)
\ \Rightarrow\
(G1a)
. If
(G1c)
holds, then for any
p\leq{\bf d}(a)={\bf r}(a^{-1})
we have

\displaystyle\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a^{-1}{\downharpoonright}_{p}}\Theta_{a}

by
(G1c)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}\Theta_{a}

by (
6.6
)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}

by (
6.8
)

\displaystyle=\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}

by Lemma
4.10
, as
p\vartheta_{a}\leq{\bf r}(a)
.

(G1d)
\ \Rightarrow\
(G1a)
. Suppose
(G1d)
holds, and let
p\leq s
. We must show that

\displaystyle e\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}

\displaystyle=e\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}

for any
e\in P
,

so fix some such
e
. To make the following calculation easier to read, let
f=e\theta_{t}
. Since
{f\leq t={\bf d}(a^{-1})}
, we can also define
g=f\vartheta_{a^{-1}}
. By Lemma
2.12
we have
f=g\vartheta_{a}
, and we note also that
g\leq{\bf r}(a^{-1})=s
. We then have

\displaystyle e\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}=e\theta_{t}\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}

\displaystyle=f\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}

by Lemma
4.10
, as
p\vartheta_{a}\leq{\bf r}(a)=t

\displaystyle=(g\vartheta_{a})\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}

as observed above

\displaystyle=(g\theta_{p})\vartheta_{a}

by
(G1d)
, as
g,p\leq s={\bf d}(a)

\displaystyle=e\theta_{t}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\vartheta_{a}

as
g=f\vartheta_{a^{-1}}
and
f=e\theta_{t}

\displaystyle=e\theta_{t}\vartheta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\theta_{s}\vartheta_{a}

by Lemma
4.10
, as
p\leq s

\displaystyle=e\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}

\displaystyle\text{by definition of the $\Theta$ maps.}\qed

Remark 6.9
.

We briefly discussed property
(G1c)
before the statement of Proposition
6.7
, and we now comment on the others.

First, we note that properties
(G1a)
and
(G1b)
bear some resemblance to the projection algebra axiom
(P4)
. In fact, this is more than just superficial. Recall that (abstract) projection algebras are supposed to ‘model’ projections of regular
*
-semigroups, and that the
\theta_{p}
operations model ‘conjugation’,
viz.

q\theta_{p}\equiv pqp
. In this way, axiom
(P4)
can be thought of as a recipe for ‘iterating’ conjugation by projections. In the next section we will see that the
\vartheta
and
\Theta
maps can also be interpreted as ‘conjugation’ by
morphisms
(see Remark
6.13
). In this way conditions
(G1a)
and
(G1b)
can then be thought of as recipes for iterating these kinds of conjugations.

By Lemma
2.12
,
\vartheta_{a}
is always an order-isomorphism
{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}
in a weak projection groupoid. As we saw in Remark
4.35
, this alone is not enough to guarantee that it is a projection algebra morphism, as in property
(G1d)
.

6.2
Projection groupoids

Proposition
6.7
allows us to formalise the desired algebraic link between the components of a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
:

Definition 6.10
.

A
projection groupoid
is a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
(cf. Definition
6.1
), for which:

(G1)

any (and hence all) of the conditions
(G1a)
–
(G1d)
hold.

We denote by
{\bf PG}
the category of projection groupoids. A morphism
{(P,\mathcal{G})\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
in
{\bf PG}
is an ordered groupoid functor
\phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
whose object map
v\phi=\phi|_{P}
is a projection algebra morphism
P\to P^{\prime}
.

The next result involves the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
and projection algebra
P(S)
, constructed from a regular
*
-semigroup
S
in Definitions
3.19
and
4.3
.

Proposition 6.11
.

The assignment
S\mapsto(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S))
is the object part of a functor
{{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PG}}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup. We first check that
(P,\mathcal{G})=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S))
is a projection groupoid.
It follows quickly from Definition
3.19
that
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a
weak
projection groupoid, so it remains to verify
(G1)
.
For this, we first note that for any
a\in S
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
, we have

p\vartheta_{a}={\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)={\bf r}(pa)=(pa)^{*}pa=a^{*}pa.

(6.12)

To show that
(G1d)
holds, let
a\in\mathcal{G}(=S)
. We must show that
\vartheta_{a}
is a projection algebra morphism,
i.e. that

(q\theta_{p})\vartheta_{a}=(q\vartheta_{a})\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}$.}

For this we use (
6.12
) and
q=aa^{*}qaa^{*}
(as
q\leq{\bf d}(a)=aa^{*}
) to calculate

(q\vartheta_{a})\theta_{p\vartheta_{a}}=(a^{*}qa)\theta_{a^{*}pa}=a^{*}pa\cdot a^{*}qa\cdot a^{*}pa=a^{*}p(aa^{*}qaa^{*})pa=a^{*}pqpa=(pqp)\vartheta_{a}=(q\theta_{p})\vartheta_{a}.

Now let
S^{\prime}
be another regular
*
-semigroup, and write
P^{\prime}=P(S^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime})
. We must show that any
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
is a projection groupoid morphism
(P,\mathcal{G})\to(P,\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
. But this follows from Propositions
3.27
and
4.25
, which respectively show that
\phi
is an ordered groupoid functor
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and
\phi|_{P}
is a projection algebra morphism
P\to P^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

It follows from (
6.12
) that
\vartheta_{a}
acts as a kind of ‘conjugation’ by the morphism
a\in\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
on projections from
{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}
. The
\Theta
maps can be thought of as conjugations as well, but on all of
P
. Indeed, for any
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p\in P
, we use (
6.12
) to calculate

p\Theta_{a}=p\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}=a^{*}\cdot{\bf d}(a)\cdot p\cdot{\bf d}(a)\cdot a=a^{*}\cdot aa^{*}\cdot p\cdot aa^{*}\cdot a=a^{*}pa.

(6.14)

Remark 6.15
.

Certainly the functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PG}
from Proposition
6.11
carries more information than the functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf OG}
from Proposition
3.27
. For example, the latter did not allow us to distinguish adjacency semigroups
S=A(\Gamma)
and
S^{\prime}=A(\Gamma^{\prime})
, where
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
were different (reflexive, undirected) graphs on the same vertex set (cf. Example
3.33
). However, since
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
have different edge sets, it follows from (
3.36
) that the projection algebras
P(S)
and
P(S^{\prime})
are different, and so
(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S))\not=(P(S^{\prime}),\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime}))
.

On the other hand, the functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PG}
from Proposition
6.11
is still not injective (on objects). Indeed, if
S=\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
is a Rees 0-matrix regular
*
-semigroup as in Example
3.39
, then the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(S)
depends only on the indexing set
P
and the group
G
, while the projection algebra
P(S)
depends only on the
locations
of the non-zero entries of the sandwich matrix
M
, and not on the
values
of these entries; cf. (
3.44
).

Before we move on, we record a simple technical result that will be used later.

Lemma 6.16
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a projection groupoid, and if
a\in\mathcal{G}
and
p,q\in P
, then

p\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{q}\Theta_{a}\theta_{p}=q\Theta_{a}\theta_{p}.

Proof.

By
(G1b)
and
(P3)
we have
p\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{q}\Theta_{a}\theta_{p}=p\theta_{q\Theta_{a}}\theta_{p}=q\Theta_{a}\theta_{p}
.
∎

6.3
Weak chained projection groupoids

The functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PG}
from Proposition
6.11
allows us to represent a regular
*
-semigroup
S
by its corresponding projection groupoid
(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S))
, but we saw in Remark
6.15
that this representation is not faithful. We address this by adding an extra layer of structure:

Definition 6.17
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G})
be a projection groupoid (cf. Definition
6.10
), and let
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
be the chain groupoid of
P=v\mathcal{G}
(cf. Definition
5.17
). An
evaluation map
is an ordered
v
-functor
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
, meaning that the following hold:

(E1)

\varepsilon(p)=p
for all
p\in P
(where as usual we identify
p\equiv[p]\in\mathscr{C}
),

(E2)

\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}\circ\mathfrak{d})=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})\circ\varepsilon(\mathfrak{d})
if
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})
,

(E3)

\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{d}\ \Rightarrow\ \varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})\leq\varepsilon(\mathfrak{d})
.

Remark 6.18
.

It is worthwhile noting a number of simple consequences of
(E1)
–
(E3)
. As with group homomorphisms, it is easy to see that

(E4)

\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}^{-1})=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})^{-1}
for all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}
.

It also follows that:

(E5)

{\bf d}(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))={\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})
and
{\bf r}(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))={\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})
for all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}
.

For example,
{\bf d}(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})\circ\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})^{-1}=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}\circ\mathfrak{c}^{-1})=\varepsilon({\bf d}(\mathfrak{c}))
.

It is also easy to see that
(E3)
is equivalent (in the presence of the other axioms) to:

(E6)

\varepsilon({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c})={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})
if
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})
.

For example, if
(E6)
holds, and if
\mathfrak{c}\leq\mathfrak{d}
, then
\mathfrak{c}={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}
for some
p\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})
; it follows from this that
\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})=\varepsilon({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d})={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon(\mathfrak{d})\leq\varepsilon(\mathfrak{d})
.

Remark 6.19
.

The existence of an evaluation map
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathcal{G}
(for a projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
) implies a certain degree of connectivity in the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. Indeed, since there is a
P
-chain
p\to q
for every pair
(p,q)
in the transitive closure of
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
, the morphism set
\mathcal{G}(p,q)

must be non-empty for every such pair. For any groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with (at least) this much connectivity, freeness of
\mathscr{C}
(cf. Remark
5.20
) means that one can define a
v
-functor
\phi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
by arbitrarily choosing morphisms
[p,q]\phi\in\mathcal{G}(p,q)
for each
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\setminus\Delta_{P}
, as long as one ensures that the identity
[q,p]\phi=([p,q]\phi)^{-1}
is satisfied. Whether or not this
\phi
will be an
ordered

v
-functor is another question entirely.

Definition 6.20
.

A
weak chained projection groupoid
is a triple
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, where
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a projection groupoid (cf. Definition
6.10
), and
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
is an evaluation map (cf. Definition
6.17
), where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
.

We write
{\bf WCPG}
for the category of weak chained projection groupoids. Morphisms in
{\bf WCPG}
are called
chained projection functors
. Such a morphism
{(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})}
is a projection groupoid morphism
\phi:(P,\mathcal{G})\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
(cf. Definition
6.10
) that
respects evaluation maps
in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where here
\Phi=\mathscr{C}(v\phi)
is the functor
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})
from Proposition
5.18
, given by
[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\Phi=[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]
. Commutativity of the diagram means that

(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))\phi=\varepsilon^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}\Phi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}$.}

(To check that the composition of chained projection functors is indeed a chained projection functor, one uses the functoriality of
\mathscr{C}
, as in Remark
5.19
.)

Consider a weak chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
.
For
p,q\in P
with
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
, we have the
P
-chain
[p,q]\in\mathscr{C}
, and the morphisms
\varepsilon[p,q]\in\mathcal{G}
will play a crucial role in all that follows. For one thing, these morphisms generate the image of
\mathscr{C}
under
\varepsilon
(as the
[p,q]
generate
\mathscr{C}
). Specifically, if
\mathfrak{c}=[p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}]\in\mathscr{C}
, then

\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})=\varepsilon[p_{1},p_{2}]\circ\varepsilon[p_{2},p_{3}]\circ\cdots\circ\varepsilon[p_{k-1},p_{k}].

(6.21)

The next lemma gathers some important basic properties of the
\varepsilon[p,q]
; in what follows, we will typically use these without explicit reference.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
be a weak chained projection groupoid.

(i)

For any
p\in P
we have
\varepsilon[p,p]=p
.

(ii)

If
p,q\in P
are such that
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
, then

\displaystyle{\bf d}(\varepsilon[p,q])

\displaystyle=p,

\displaystyle\varepsilon[p,q]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[q,p],

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}

\displaystyle=\theta_{q}|_{p^{\downarrow}},

\displaystyle{\bf r}(\varepsilon[p,q])

\displaystyle=q,

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}

\displaystyle=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}.

(iii)

If
p,q,r,s\in P
are such that
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
,
r\leq p
and
s\leq q
, then

{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon[p,q]=\varepsilon[r,r\theta_{q}]\and\varepsilon[p,q]{\downharpoonright}_{s}=\varepsilon[s\theta_{p},s].

Proof.

(i)
. This follows quickly from
(E1)
and
[p,p]=[p]
, keeping in mind the identification of
p\in P
with the chain
[p]\in\mathscr{C}
.

(iii)
. This follows from
(E6)
, together with (
5.3
) and (
5.14
).

(ii)
. The claims concerning domains, ranges and inversion follow from
(E4)
and
(E5)
, and the fact that
[p,q]^{-1}=[q,p]
in
\mathscr{C}
.

Since the maps
\vartheta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}
and
\theta_{q}|_{p^{\downarrow}}
both have domain
p^{\downarrow}
, we can show the maps are equal by showing that

r\vartheta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}=r\theta_{q}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $r\leq p$.}

But for such
r
, we use the definition of the
\vartheta
maps, and parts of the current lemma that have already been proved, to calculate

r\vartheta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}={\bf r}({}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon[p,q])={\bf r}(\varepsilon[r,r\theta_{q}])=r\theta_{q}.

Finally, again using already-proved parts of the current lemma, we have

\Theta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}=\theta_{p}\vartheta_{\varepsilon[p,q]}=\theta_{p}\circ\theta_{q}|_{p^{\downarrow}}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q},

where in the last step we used the fact that
p^{\downarrow}=\operatorname{im}(\theta_{p})
.
∎

Before we move on, it will also be convenient to record the following result.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
be a weak chained projection groupoid. If
p,q\in P
are such that
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
, and if
a\leq\varepsilon[p,q]
, then

a=\varepsilon[r,s]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptr={\bf d}(a)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts={\bf r}(a).

Proof.

We use Lemma
6.22
to calculate

a={}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon[p,q]=\varepsilon[r,r\theta_{q}]\and r\theta_{q}={\bf r}(\varepsilon[r,r\theta_{q}])={\bf r}(a)=s.\qed

For the rest of Section
6.3
we fix a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, and let
(P,\mathcal{G})=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S))
be the projection groupoid associated to
S
(cf. Proposition
6.11
). We wish to extend this to a
weak chained
projection groupoid, for which we must define an evaluation map
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S):\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
is the chain groupoid of
P
. To do so, it is first convenient to define a map

\pi=\pi(S):\mathscr{P}\to\mathcal{G}(=S)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\pi(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})=p_{1}\cdots p_{k},

(6.24)

where
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
is the path category of
P
. So
\pi(\mathfrak{p})
is simply the product (in
S
) of the entries of
\mathfrak{p}
(taken in order). We begin with the following fact:

Lemma 6.25
.

For any
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have
{\bf d}(\pi(\mathfrak{p}))={\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
and
{\bf r}(\pi(\mathfrak{p}))={\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
. A routine calculation shows that

{\bf d}(\pi(\mathfrak{p}))=\pi(\mathfrak{p})\cdot\pi(\mathfrak{p})^{*}=p_{1}\cdots p_{k-1}\cdot p_{k}\cdot p_{k-1}\cdots p_{1}.

Since
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}
, this reduces to
p_{1}={\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
. We obtain
{\bf r}(\pi(\mathfrak{p}))=p_{k}={\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})
by symmetry.
∎

The next result concerns the congruence
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
on
\mathscr{P}
from Definition
5.15
.

Lemma 6.26
.

The map
\pi=\pi(S):\mathscr{P}\to\mathcal{G}
is a
v
-functor, and
{\approx}\subseteq\ker(\pi)
.

Proof.

For the first statement, it suffices by Lemma
6.25
to show that
\pi
is a functor. For this, consider composable paths
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(p_{k},\ldots,p_{l})
. Then since
p_{k}
is an idempotent, and since
{\bf r}(\pi(\mathfrak{p}))=p_{k}={\bf d}(\pi(\mathfrak{q}))
by Lemma
6.25
, we have

\displaystyle\pi(\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q})=\pi(p_{1},\ldots,p_{l})

\displaystyle=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}p_{k+1}\cdots p_{l}

\displaystyle=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}\cdot p_{k}p_{k+1}\cdots p_{l}=\pi(\mathfrak{p})\pi(\mathfrak{q})=\pi(\mathfrak{p})\circ\pi(\mathfrak{q}).

To show that
{\approx}\subseteq\ker(\pi)
, we must show that
\Omega\subseteq\ker(\pi)
, i.e. that
\pi(\mathfrak{s})=\pi(\mathfrak{t})
for all
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Omega
. This is clear if
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
has the form
(

Ω

1)
. For
(

Ω

2)
, recall that
p=q\theta_{p}=pqp
when
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
.
∎

Definition 6.27
.

Since
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}
, it follows from Lemma
6.26
that we have a well-defined
v
-functor

\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S):\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\varepsilon[\mathfrak{p}]=\pi(\mathfrak{p})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}$.}

That is,
\varepsilon[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
whenever
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}
.

Lemma 6.28
.

The functor
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S):\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
is an evaluation map.

Proof.

Conditions
(E1)
and
(E2)
hold because
\varepsilon
is a
v
-functor. As explained in Remark
6.18
, we can prove
(E6)
in place of
(E3)
, and we note that
(E6)
says

\displaystyle\varepsilon({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\mathfrak{p}])

\displaystyle={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon[\mathfrak{p}]

for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
and
q\leq{\bf d}[\mathfrak{p}]={\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
.

Since
\varepsilon({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\mathfrak{p}])=\varepsilon[{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}]=\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})
and
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\varepsilon[\mathfrak{p}]={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\pi(\mathfrak{p})
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\pi(\mathfrak{p})

for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
and
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})
.

(6.29)

We prove this by induction on
k
, the length of the path
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
. When
k=1
, we have

\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})=\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}(p_{1}))=\pi(q)=q=qp_{1}=q\pi(\mathfrak{p})={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\pi(\mathfrak{p}),

where we used the fact that
q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{1}\ \Rightarrow\ q=qp_{1}
. Now suppose
k\geq 2
, and let
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
be as in (
5.3
). Let
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1})\in\mathscr{P}
, noting that
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k-1})
. Then

\displaystyle{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\pi(\mathfrak{p})=q\cdot p_{1}\cdots p_{k}

\displaystyle=qp_{1}\cdots p_{k}\cdot(qp_{1}\cdots p_{k})^{*}\cdot qp_{1}\cdots p_{k}

\displaystyle=qp_{1}\cdots p_{k}\cdot p_{k}\cdots p_{1}q\cdot qp_{1}\cdots p_{k}

\displaystyle=q\cdot(p_{1}\cdots p_{k-1})\cdot(p_{k}\cdots p_{1}\cdot q\cdot p_{1}\cdots p_{k})

\displaystyle={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\pi(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\cdot q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}

\displaystyle=\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\cdot q_{k}

by induction and (
5.4
)

\displaystyle=q_{1}\cdots q_{k-1}\cdot q_{k}=\pi({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}).

This completes the proof of (
6.29
), and hence of the lemma.
∎

Definition 6.30
.

It follows from Proposition
6.11
and Lemma
6.28
that
(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))
is a weak chained projection groupoid for any regular
*
-semigroup
S
(cf. Definitions
3.19
,
4.3
and
6.27
).

Proposition 6.31
.

The assignment
S\mapsto(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))
is the object part of a functor
{{\bf RSS}\to{\bf WCPG}}
.

Proof.

It remains to check that any morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
in
{\bf RSS}
is a chained projection functor from
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))
to
(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})=(P(S^{\prime}),\mathcal{G}(S^{\prime}),\varepsilon(S^{\prime}))
. Since
\phi
is a projection groupoid morphism (cf. Proposition
6.11
), it remains to check that it respects the evaluation maps (cf. Definition
6.20
). For this, fix a chain
{\mathfrak{c}=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\in\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)}
. Writing
\Phi=\mathscr{C}(\phi):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})
as in Proposition
5.18
, we have

(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))\phi=(p_{1}\cdots p_{k})\phi=(p_{1}\phi)\cdots(p_{k}\phi)=\varepsilon^{\prime}[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]=\varepsilon^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}\Phi).\qed

We will ultimately see that the functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf WCPG}
from Proposition
6.31
is injective (on objects), although it is still not surjective. Rather than proving injectivity directly, we will first identify the image of the functor, and then construct an inverse functor in the opposite direction. The image will consist of the
chained projection groupoids
defined in Section
6.4
, and the inverse functor will be constructed in Section
7
.

Before we do this, we can already check that the functor from Proposition
6.31
allows us to distinguish between Rees 0-matrix semigroups.

Remark 6.32
.

Consider again the Rees 0-matrix regular
*
-semigroup
S=\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
, as in Example
3.39
. We saw previously that the projection algebra of
S
is
P_{0}=P\cup\{0\}
, with
\theta
operations as in (
3.44
). As in (
6.21
), the evaluation map
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S):\mathscr{C}(P_{0})\to S
is completely determined by the elements
\varepsilon[p,q]
, for
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
. Of course we have
\varepsilon[0,0]=0
. Consulting (
3.45
), the other
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related pairs in
P_{0}
have the form
(p,q)
, for
p,q\in P
with
m_{pq}\not=0
, and for these we have

\varepsilon[p,q]=pq\equiv(p,1,p)(q,1,q)=(p,m_{pq},q).

(6.33)

In this way, the evaluation map
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S)
specifies the entries of the sandwich matrix
M
, and hence the entire structure of
S
, so that sandwich matrices and evaluation maps are two sides of the same coin.

6.4
Chained projection groupoids

We are almost ready to give the definition of chained projection groupoids, as the weak chained projection groupoids satisfying a certain coherence condition. A key ingredient is the concept of a
linked pair
of projections, the definition of which does not require the existence of an evaluation map.

Definition 6.34
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G})
be a projection groupoid (cf. Definition
6.10
), and consider a morphism
b\in\mathcal{G}
. A pair of projections
(e,f)\in P\times P
is said to be
b
-linked
if

f=e\Theta_{b}\theta_{f}\and e=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e}.

(6.35)

We will soon associate two morphisms
\lambda(e,b,f)
and
\rho(e,b,f)
to the
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
. But first we prove the next result, which will ensure these morphisms are well defined.

Lemma 6.36
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G})
be a projection groupoid, and suppose
(e,f)
is
b
-linked, where
{b\in\mathcal{G}(q,r)}
. Define further projections

e_{1}=e\theta_{q},\hskip 20.00003pte_{2}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}=e\Theta_{b}\and f_{2}=f\theta_{r}.

(6.37)

Then

(i)

e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
and
f\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
,

(ii)

e_{i}\leq q
and
f_{i}\leq r
for
i=1,2
,

(iii)

e\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e_{i}
and
f\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}f_{i}
for
i=1,2
,

(iv)

{}_{e_{i}}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

It is clear from Definition
6.34
that
(e,f)
is
b
-linked if and only if
(f,e)
is
b^{-1}
-linked. If we replace
b\leftrightarrow b^{-1}
and
(e,f)\leftrightarrow(f,e)
, then the projections defined in (
6.37
) are interchanged accordingly,
viz.

e_{1}\leftrightarrow f_{2}
and
e_{2}\leftrightarrow f_{1}
.
Because of this symmetry, for parts
(i)
–
(iii)
, it suffices to prove the claims concerning
e,e_{1},e_{2}
. (Alternatively, the arguments below can be easily adapted to prove the claims regarding
f,f_{1},f_{2}
.)

(ii)
. This follows from
\operatorname{im}(\Theta_{b^{-1}})=\operatorname{im}(\theta_{q})=q^{\downarrow}
.

(iii)
. From
e\leq e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
, it follows from Lemma
4.17
(ii)
that
e\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e\theta_{q}=e_{1}
. By Lemma
4.13
(i)
, we have
e=e_{2}\theta_{e}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e_{2}
. It remains to show that
e_{2}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e
, and for this we use
(G1b)
, (
6.35
) and (
6.37
):

e\theta_{e_{2}}=e\theta_{f\Theta_{b^{-1}}}=e\Theta_{b}\theta_{f}\Theta_{b^{-1}}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}=e_{2}.

(iv)
. We must show that
f_{i}=e_{i}\vartheta_{b}
(
i=1,2
). Keeping
q={\bf d}(b)
and
r={\bf d}(b^{-1})
in mind, we have

e_{1}\vartheta_{b}=e\theta_{q}\vartheta_{b}=e\Theta_{b}=f_{1}\and e_{2}\vartheta_{b}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}\vartheta_{b}=f\theta_{r}=f_{2},

where we used Lemma
6.4
(iii)
in the second calculation.

(i)
. Combining parts
(ii)
and
(iii)
, we have
e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e_{1}\leq q
, and Lemma
4.17
(i)
then gives
{e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q}
.
∎

Definition 6.38
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
be a weak chained projection groupoid (cf. Definition
6.20
). Let
(e,f)
be a
b
-linked pair, where
b\in\mathcal{G}(q,r)
, and let
e_{1},e_{2},f_{1},f_{2}\in P
be as in (
6.37
). By Lemma
6.36
, we have two well-defined morphisms

\displaystyle\lambda(e,b,f)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]

and

\displaystyle\rho(e,b,f)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ{}_{e_{2}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{1}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f].

(6.39)

These morphisms are shown in Figure
14
.

e
e_{1}
e_{2}
f
f_{1}
f_{2}
q
r
b
{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{1}}
{}_{e_{2}}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}
\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]
\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]
\varepsilon[f_{1},f]
\varepsilon[f_{2},f]

Figure 14:
The projections and morphisms associated to a
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
, for
b\in\mathcal{G}(q,r)
; see Definitions
6.34
,
6.38
and
6.40
, and Lemma
6.36
. Dotted and dashed lines indicate
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\leq
relationships, respectively. Axiom
(G2)
says that the hexagon at the bottom of the diagram commutes.

The above-mentioned coherence property states that the two terms in (
6.39
) must be equal:

Definition 6.40
.

A
chained projection groupoid
is a weak chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
(cf. Definition
6.20
) satisfying the following coherence condition:

(G2)

For every
b\in\mathcal{G}
, and for every
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
, we have
\lambda(e,b,f)=\rho(e,b,f)
, where these morphisms are as in (
6.39
).

We denote by
{\bf CPG}
the full subcategory of
{\bf WCPG}
consisting of all chained projection groupoids (and all chained projection functors between them).

Remark 6.41
.

One might wonder if the concept of linked pairs could be avoided when defining chained projection groupoids. Specifically, one might wonder if
(G2)
is equivalent to:

(G2)
′

For any morphism
b\in\mathcal{G}(q,r)
, and for any projections
e,e_{1},e_{2},f,f_{1},f_{2}\in P
satisfying conditions
(i)
–
(iv)
from Lemma
6.36
, we have
\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ{}_{e_{2}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f]
.

While
(G2)′
of course implies
(G2)
, the converse does not hold, as we will see in Remark
6.43
.

For a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we denote by
{\bf G}(S)=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))
the weak chained projection groupoid from Definition
6.30
. Proposition
6.31
says that the assignment
S\mapsto{\bf G}(S)
is the object part of a functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf WCPG}
. The next result says that
{\bf G}
maps into the coherent subcategory
{\bf CPG}
.

Theorem 6.42
.

{\bf G}
is a functor
{{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}}
.

Proof.

Given Proposition
6.31
, it remains to check that
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)={\bf G}(S)
satisfies
(G2)
for any regular
*
-semigroup
S
. To do so, let
(e,f)
be a
b
-linked pair, where
{b\in\mathcal{G}(=S)}
. Write

q={\bf d}(b)=bb^{*}\and r={\bf r}(b)=b^{*}b,

and let the
e_{i},f_{i}
be as in (
6.37
). Keeping (
6.14
) in mind, we have

e_{1}=qeq,\hskip 20.00003pte_{2}=bfb^{*},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}=b^{*}eb\and f_{2}=rfr.

We then calculate
ee_{1}=eqeq=eq
and
bf_{1}=bb^{*}eb=qeb
. Since
e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
by Lemma
6.36
(i)
, we also have
e=q\theta_{e}=eqe
. Putting this all together, we have

\lambda(e,b,f)=\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]=ee_{1}\cdot e_{1}b\cdot f_{1}f=ee_{1}\cdot bf_{1}\cdot f=eq\cdot qeb\cdot f=eqe\cdot bf=ebf.

A similar calculation gives
\rho(e,b,f)=ebf
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 6.43
.

In the above proof, verification of
(G2)
boils down to checking that

ee_{1}bf_{1}f=ee_{2}bf_{2}f,

where
b\in\mathcal{G}(q,r)
,
(e,f)
is
b
-linked, and the
e_{i},f_{i}
are as in (
6.37
). We are now in a position to see that condition
(G2)
cannot be replaced by the stronger
(G2)′
discussed in Remark
6.41
. Indeed, consider the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{4}
, and the elements defined by
