}}=ee_{2}bf_{2}f.

7
From chained projection groupoids to regular
*
-semigroups

In Section
6
we introduced the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids, and constructed a functor
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
from the category of regular
*
-semigroups. Our goal in this section is to go backwards, and construct a functor
{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
. The bulk of the work is carried out in Section
7.1
, where we show how to build a regular
*
-semigroup
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
from a chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
; see Definition
7.1
and Theorem
7.13
. After showing that this does indeed yield a functor in Section
7.2
, we establish some basic properties of the semigroups
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
in Section
7.3
. These will be used in Section
8
to show that
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
are mutually inverse isomorphisms between the categories
{\bf RSS}
and
{\bf CPG}
.

7.1
The regular
*
-semigroup associated to a chained projection groupoid

Our aim in this section is to construct a regular
*
-semigroup
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
from a chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. The underlying set of
S
will simply be
\mathcal{G}
, and the involution of
S
will simply be inversion in
\mathcal{G}
:

a^{*}=a^{-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a\in\mathcal{G}$.}

The definition of the product in
S
, which we will denote by
\bullet
, is more involved. Its inspiration is drawn from the properties of regular
*
-semigroups discussed in Remark
3.29
. Coherency (cf. Definition
6.40
) is not required for the definition of
\bullet
, or indeed for the first series of results in this section.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
be a weak chained projection groupoid (cf. Definition
6.20
), and consider an arbitrary pair of morphisms
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
. Let
p={\bf r}(a)
and
q={\bf d}(b)
, and define the projections

p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\and q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}.

By Lemma
4.16
, we have
p^{\prime}\leq p
,
q^{\prime}\leq q
and
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q^{\prime}
. In particular, the morphisms
a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}
,
\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]
and
{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b
exist, and we define

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b.

(7.2)

This is illustrated in Figure
15
. We define
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
to be the
(2,1)
-algebra with underlying set
\mathcal{G}
, and with binary operation
\bullet
and unary operation
{}^{*}={}^{-1}
.

p
q
p^{\prime}
q^{\prime}
a
b
a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}
{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b
\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]
a\bullet b

Figure 15:
Construction of the product
a\bullet b
, as in Definition
7.1
.

Theorem
7.13
below says that
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a regular
*
-semigroup when
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
belongs to
{\bf CPG}
, and not just to
{\bf WCPG}
. To prove this, we must establish the identities:

(a\bullet b)\bullet c=a\bullet(b\bullet c),\hskip 20.00003pt(a^{*})^{*}=a=(a\bullet a^{*})\bullet a\and(a\bullet b)^{*}=b^{*}\bullet a^{*}.

Associativity of
\bullet
is quite difficult to demonstrate, and is finally achieved in Lemma
7.11
below. The identities involving
∗
are comparatively easy, however, and we verify these shortly in Lemma
7.4
. For its proof, we need Lemma
7.3
, which shows that the (total) product
\bullet
extends the (partial) composition
\circ
, in the sense that these operations agree whenever the latter is defined.

For the rest of this section we fix a weak chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
; we only assume coherency in Lemma
7.11
, at the very last stage of the proof of associativity.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
are such that
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
a\bullet b=a\circ b
.

Proof.

In the notation of Definition
7.1
, we have
p=q
, so also
p^{\prime}=q^{\prime}=p
. But then

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p}\circ\varepsilon[p,p]\circ{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b=a\circ p\circ b=a\circ b.\qed

Lemma 7.4
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
, we have

(a^{*})^{*}=a=(a\bullet a^{*})\bullet a\and(a\bullet b)^{*}=b^{*}\bullet a^{*}.

Proof.

Of course
(a^{*})^{*}=(a^{-1})^{-1}=a
. It follows from Lemma
7.3
that
a\bullet a^{*}=a\circ a^{-1}={\bf d}(a)
, and so
(a\bullet a^{*})\bullet a={\bf d}(a)\bullet a={\bf d}(a)\circ a=a
.

Next, let
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
be as in Definition
7.1
. Since
q={\bf r}(b^{*})
and
p={\bf d}(a^{*})
, we have

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b\and b^{*}\bullet a^{*}=b^{*}{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},p^{\prime}]\circ{}_{p^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*},

and so

(a\bullet b)^{*}=({}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b)^{*}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]^{*}\circ(a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}})^{*}=b^{*}{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},p^{\prime}]\circ{}_{p^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a^{*}=b^{*}\bullet a^{*}.\qed

The symmetry/duality afforded by the identity
(a\bullet b)^{*}=b^{*}\bullet a^{*}
will allow for some simplification in some of the proofs to follow. This is the case with the proof of the next result, which identifies the domain and range of a product
a\bullet b
.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
we have

{\bf d}(a\bullet b)={\bf d}(b)\Theta_{a^{-1}}\and{\bf r}(a\bullet b)={\bf r}(a)\Theta_{b}.

Proof.

Let
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
be as in Definition
7.1
, so that
a\bullet b
is as in (
7.2
). Then

{\bf r}(a\bullet b)={\bf r}({}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b)=q^{\prime}\vartheta_{b}=p\theta_{q}\vartheta_{b}={\bf r}(a)\theta_{{\bf d}(b)}\vartheta_{b}={\bf r}(a)\Theta_{b}.

The identity involving domains may be proved in similar fashion (using (
2.11
)). Alternatively, it follows from the range identity and duality:

{\bf d}(a\bullet b)={\bf r}((a\bullet b)^{*})={\bf r}(b^{*}\bullet a^{*})={\bf r}(b^{*})\Theta_{a^{*}}={\bf d}(b)\Theta_{a^{-1}}.\qed

Our next result establishes an important identity, and can be thought of as a generalisation of Lemma
6.5
(in light of Lemma
7.3
) in the special case of (weak) chained projection groupoids.

Lemma 7.6
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
we have
\Theta_{a\bullet b}=\Theta_{a}\Theta_{b}
.

Proof.

Let
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
be as in Definition
7.1
. Then by the projection algebra axioms we have

\theta_{p^{\prime}}\theta_{q^{\prime}}=\theta_{q\theta_{p}}\theta_{p\theta_{q}}=_{4}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=_{5}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}.

We then calculate

\displaystyle\Theta_{a\bullet b}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}}\circ\Theta_{\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]}\circ\Theta_{{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b}

by (
7.2
) and Lemma
6.5

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\theta_{p^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{p^{\prime}}\theta_{q^{\prime}}\circ\theta_{q^{\prime}}\Theta_{b}

by
(G1c)
and Lemmas
6.4
(iv)
and
6.22
(ii)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\circ\theta_{p^{\prime}}\theta_{q^{\prime}}\circ\Theta_{b}

by
(P2)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\circ\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\circ\Theta_{b}

by the above observation

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\theta_{{\bf r}(a)}\circ\theta_{{\bf d}(b)}\Theta_{b}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{a}\Theta_{b}

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:G2}\ref{G22}.}\qed

We are almost ready to show that
\bullet
is associative when
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is coherent, i.e. that
(a\bullet b)\bullet c
and
a\bullet(b\bullet c)
are equal, for all
a,b,c\in\mathcal{G}
. Before we do so, we note that Lemmas
7.5
and
7.6
can be used to show that these two terms have equal domains, and equal ranges, still without assuming coherency. For example,

{\bf r}((a\bullet b)\bullet c)={\bf r}(a\bullet b)\Theta_{c}={\bf r}(a)\Theta_{b}\Theta_{c}={\bf r}(a)\Theta_{b\bullet c}={\bf r}(a\bullet(b\bullet c)).

(7.7)

A similar calculation gives

{\bf d}((a\bullet b)\bullet c)={\bf d}(a\bullet(b\bullet c))={\bf d}(c)\Theta_{b^{-1}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}.

(7.8)

As a stepping stone to associativity, the next result gives expansions of
(a\bullet b)\bullet c
and
a\bullet(b\bullet c)
, expressing each term as a composition of five morphisms.

Lemma 7.9
.

If
a,b,c\in\mathcal{G}
, and if
p={\bf r}(a)
,
q={\bf d}(b)
,
r={\bf r}(b)
and
s={\bf d}(c)
, then

(i)

(a\bullet b)\bullet c=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]\circ{}_{f}{\downharpoonleft}c
,
where

e=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p},\hskip 20.00003pte_{1}=e\theta_{q},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}=e\Theta_{b}\and f=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s},

(ii)

a\bullet(b\bullet c)=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f]\circ{}_{f}{\downharpoonleft}c
,
where

e=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p},\hskip 20.00003pte_{2}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{2}=f\theta_{r}\and f=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}.

The above factorisations are illustrated in Figure
16
.

Proof.

(i)
. Put
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}
and
q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}
, so that

a\bullet b=a^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}\circ b^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pta^{\prime}=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptb^{\prime}={}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b.

Keeping Lemma
7.5
in mind, we now define

t={\bf r}(a\bullet b)=p\Theta_{b}.

Then

(a\bullet b)\bullet c=(a\bullet b){\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[t^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\circ{}_{s^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}c\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{$t^{\prime}=s\theta_{t}$ and $s^{\prime}=t\theta_{s}$.}

We now focus on the term
(a\bullet b){\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}
. By (
2.13
) we have

(a\bullet b){\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}=(a^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}\circ b^{\prime}){\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{u}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v}\circ b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptv=t^{\prime}\vartheta_{b^{\prime}}^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptu=t^{\prime}\vartheta_{b^{\prime}}^{-1}\vartheta_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{-1}.

Now,

•

a^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{u}=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}{\downharpoonright}_{u}=a{\downharpoonright}_{u}
,

•

\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]
,
{\bf d}(\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v})={\bf r}(a^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{u})=u
and
{\bf r}(\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v})=v
, so
\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v}=\varepsilon[u,v]
by Lemma
6.23
, and

•

b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}\leq b^{\prime}\leq b
and
{\bf d}(b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}})={\bf r}(\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v})=v
, so
b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}={}_{v}{\downharpoonleft}b
. (For later use, since
b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}
and
b{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}
are both below
b
and have codomain
t^{\prime}
, it also follows that
b{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}=b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}={}_{v}{\downharpoonleft}b
.)

Putting everything together, it follows that

\displaystyle(a\bullet b)\bullet c

\displaystyle=(a\bullet b){\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[t^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\circ{}_{s^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}c

\displaystyle=a^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{u}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{v}\circ b^{\prime}{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[t^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\circ{}_{s^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}c

\displaystyle=a{\downharpoonright}_{u}\circ\varepsilon[u,v]\circ{}_{v}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[t^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\circ{}_{s^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}c.

It therefore remains to check that

(a)

u=e
,

(b)

v=e_{1}
,

(c)

t^{\prime}=f_{1}
,

(d)

s^{\prime}=f
,

where
e,e_{1},f_{1},f
are as defined in the statement of the lemma.

(a)
. First note that
{\bf d}(a{\downharpoonright}_{u})={\bf d}((a\bullet b)\bullet c)=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}
by (
7.8
), so
a{\downharpoonright}_{u}={}_{s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}}{\downharpoonleft}a
. But then

u={\bf r}(a{\downharpoonright}_{u})={\bf r}({}_{s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}}{\downharpoonleft}a)=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\Theta_{a^{-1}}\vartheta_{a}=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}=e,

where we used Lemma
6.4
(iii)
in the second-last step.

(c)
. Here we use
(G1b)
to calculate
t^{\prime}=s\theta_{t}=s\theta_{p\Theta_{b}}=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{b}=e\Theta_{b}=f_{1}
.

(b)
. We noted above that
{}_{v}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}}
. Combining this with (
2.11
), the just-proved item
(c)
, and Lemma
6.4
(iii)
, it follows that

v={\bf d}({}_{v}{\downharpoonleft}b)={\bf d}(b{\downharpoonright}_{t^{\prime}})=t^{\prime}\vartheta_{b^{-1}}=f_{1}\vartheta_{b^{-1}}=(e\Theta_{b})\vartheta_{b^{-1}}=e\theta_{q}=e_{1}.

(d)
. Finally,
s^{\prime}=t\theta_{s}=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}=f
.

As noted above, this completes the proof of
(i)
.

(ii)
. This can be proved in similar fashion to part
(i)
, but in fact we can also deduce it from
(i)
. Indeed, we first use Lemma
7.4
to expand

(a\bullet(b\bullet c))^{-1}=(b\bullet c)^{-1}\bullet a^{-1}=(c^{-1}\bullet b^{-1})\bullet a^{-1}.

Note that
s={\bf r}(c^{-1})
,
r={\bf d}(b^{-1})
,
q={\bf r}(b^{-1})
and
p={\bf d}(a^{-1})
. It follows from part
(i)
that

(c^{-1}\bullet b^{-1})\bullet a^{-1}=c^{-1}{\downharpoonright}_{e^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[e^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime}]\circ{}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b^{-1}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1}^{\prime},f^{\prime}]\circ{}_{f^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a^{-1},

where

e^{\prime}=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s},\hskip 20.00003pte_{1}^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\theta_{r},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\Theta_{b^{-1}}\and f^{\prime}=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}.

But then

\displaystyle a\bullet(b\bullet c)

\displaystyle=((c^{-1}\bullet b^{-1})\bullet a^{-1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(c^{-1}{\downharpoonright}_{e^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[e^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime}]\circ{}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b^{-1}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1}^{\prime},f^{\prime}]\circ{}_{f^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a^{-1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=({}_{f^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a^{-1})^{-1}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1}^{\prime},f^{\prime}]^{-1}\circ({}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b^{-1})^{-1}\circ\varepsilon[e^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime}]^{-1}\circ(c^{-1}{\downharpoonright}_{e^{\prime}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=a{\downharpoonright}_{f^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[f^{\prime},f_{1}^{\prime}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{e_{1}^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[e_{1}^{\prime},e^{\prime}]\circ{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}c.

(7.10)

We note immediately that
f^{\prime}=e
and
e^{\prime}=f
(where
e,f
are as in the statement of the lemma). We also have

e_{1}^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\theta_{r}=f\theta_{r}=f_{2}\and f_{1}^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\Theta_{b^{-1}}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}=e_{2}.

Thus, continuing from (
7.10
), we have

a\bullet(b\bullet c)=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f]\circ{}_{f}{\downharpoonleft}c,

as required.
∎

e
e_{1}
e_{2}
f
f_{1}
f_{2}
p
q
r
s
a
b
c
{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{1}}
{}_{e_{2}}{\downharpoonleft}b=b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}
\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]
\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]
\varepsilon[f_{1},f]
\varepsilon[f_{2},f]
a{\downharpoonright}_{e}
{}_{f}{\downharpoonleft}c

Figure 16:
Top: three morphisms
a,b,c\in\mathcal{G}
. Bottom: the upper and lower paths represent
(a\bullet b)\bullet c
and
a\bullet(b\bullet c)
, respectively; cf. Lemma
7.9
. Dashed lines indicate
\leq
relationships.

Lemma 7.11
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a chained projection groupoid, then
(a\bullet b)\bullet c=a\bullet(b\bullet c)
for any
a,b,c\in\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.9
, it suffices to show (in the notation of that lemma) that

\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f].

(7.12)

Comparing the definitions of the
e_{i},f_{i}
from Lemma
7.9
(in terms of
e,f,q,r,b
) with (
6.37
), an application of
(G2)
will give us (
7.12
) if we can show that
(e,f)
is
b
-linked (cf. Definitions
6.34
and
6.38
). In other words, the proof of the lemma will be complete if we can show that

f=e\Theta_{b}\theta_{f}\and e=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e}.

For the first, we have

\displaystyle e\Theta_{b}\theta_{f}

\displaystyle=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{b}\theta_{p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}}

by definition

\displaystyle=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}\theta_{p\Theta_{b}}\theta_{s}

by
(P4)

\displaystyle=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}

by
(G1b)
and Lemma
6.16

\displaystyle=s\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}

by Lemma
6.16

\displaystyle=p\Theta_{b}\theta_{s}=f

by Lemma
6.16
.

The proof that
e=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e}
is virtually identical.
∎

Lemmas
7.4
and
7.11
give the following:

Theorem 7.13
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a chained projection groupoid, then
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a regular
*
-semigroup. ∎

7.2
A functor

We have just seen how to construct a regular
*
-semigroup
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
from a chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. As ever, this construction can be thought of as an object map from the category
{\bf CPG}
to the category
{\bf RSS}
. Since a morphism
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
in
{\bf CPG}
is a (special kind of) map
\phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and since the semigroups
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
and
S^{\prime}={\bf S}(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
have the same underlying sets as
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(respectively), we can think of
\phi
as a map
{{\bf S}(\phi)=\phi:S\to S^{\prime}}
. We now show that
{\bf S}
(interpreted this way) is a functor:

Theorem 7.14
.

{\bf S}
is a functor
{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
.

Proof.

It remains to show that any morphism
\phi:(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
in
{\bf CPG}
is a
*
-morphism
S\to S^{\prime}
, where
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
and
S^{\prime}={\bf S}(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
.

Since
\phi
is a groupoid functor, we have

(a^{*})\phi=(a^{-1})\phi=(a\phi)^{-1}=(a\phi)^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a\in S$.}

To make the rest of this proof notationally concise, we will write
\overline{a}=a\phi
for
a\in S(=\mathcal{G})
. It remains to show that
\phi
is a semigroup homomorphism, i.e. that
\overline{a\bullet b}=\overline{a}\bullet\overline{b}
for all
a,b\in S
. (Here we write
\bullet
for the product on both
S
and
S^{\prime}
.) For this, write as usual

p={\bf r}(a),\hskip 20.00003ptq={\bf d}(b),\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\and q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q},

so that

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b.

Since
\phi
is a functor, we have

{\bf r}(\overline{a})={\bf r}(\overline{a\circ p})={\bf r}(\overline{a}\circ\overline{p})={\bf r}(\overline{p})=\overline{p}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf d}(\overline{b})=\overline{q}.

Thus,

\overline{a}\bullet\overline{b}=\overline{a}{\downharpoonright}_{\overline{p}^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}[\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{q}^{\prime}]\circ{}_{\overline{q}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\overline{b}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{p}^{\prime}=\overline{q}\theta^{\prime}_{\overline{p}}\and\overline{q}^{\prime}=\overline{p}\theta^{\prime}_{\overline{q}}.

Since
\phi
is a projection groupoid morphism, its object map
v\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
is a projection algebra morphism, and so

\overline{p^{\prime}}=\overline{q\theta_{p}}=\overline{q}\theta^{\prime}_{\overline{p}}=\overline{p}^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{q^{\prime}}=\overline{q}^{\prime}.

Since
\phi
respects the evaluation maps, we have

\overline{\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]}=(\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}])\phi=\varepsilon^{\prime}([p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\Phi)=\varepsilon^{\prime}[\overline{p^{\prime}},\overline{q^{\prime}}]=\varepsilon^{\prime}[\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{q}^{\prime}].

Since
\phi
is an ordered functor, we have

\overline{a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}}=\overline{a}{\downharpoonright}_{\overline{p^{\prime}}}=\overline{a}{\downharpoonright}_{\overline{p}^{\prime}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b}={}_{\overline{q}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\overline{b}.

Putting everything together, we have

\overline{a\bullet b}=\overline{a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b}=\overline{a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}}\circ\overline{\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]}\circ\overline{{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b}=\overline{a}{\downharpoonright}_{\overline{p}^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}[\overline{p}^{\prime},\overline{q}^{\prime}]\circ{}_{\overline{q}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\overline{b}=\overline{a}\bullet\overline{b}.\qed

7.3
Products of projections

For the duration of this section we fix a chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. The next two results concern
\bullet
products involving projections in the regular
*
-semigroup
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. These will be of use later, but we also apply them here to describe the projections, idempotents and idempotent-generated subsemigroup of
S
in Proposition
7.17
.

Lemma 7.15
.

If
a\in\mathcal{G}(p,q)
, and if
t\in P
, then

(i)

a\bullet t=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
, where
q^{\prime}=t\theta_{q}
and
t^{\prime}=q\theta_{t}
,

(ii)

t\bullet a=\varepsilon[t^{\prime},p^{\prime}]\circ{}_{p^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}a
, where
p^{\prime}=t\theta_{p}
and
t^{\prime}=p\theta_{t}
,

(iii)

a\bullet t=a\circ\varepsilon[q,t]
if
q\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}t
,

(iv)

t\bullet a=\varepsilon[t,p]\circ a
if
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}t
,

(v)

a\bullet t=a{\downharpoonright}_{t}
if
t\leq q
,

(vi)

t\bullet a={}_{t}{\downharpoonleft}a
if
t\leq p
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to prove
(i)
,
(iii)
and
(v)
.

(i)
. We have
a\bullet t=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\circ{}_{t^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}t=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\circ t^{\prime}=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
.

(iii)
. This follows from part
(i)
, as
q^{\prime}=q
and
t^{\prime}=t
if
q\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}t
.

(v)
. If
t\leq q
, then we also have
t\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
by Lemma
4.13
(ii)
, so that
t=t\theta_{q}=q^{\prime}
and
t=q\theta_{t}=t^{\prime}
. It then follows from part
(i)
that

a\bullet t=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},t^{\prime}]=a{\downharpoonright}_{t}\circ\varepsilon[t,t]=a{\downharpoonright}_{t}\circ t=a{\downharpoonright}_{t}.\qed

Lemma 7.16
.

If
p,q\in P
, then

(i)

p\bullet q=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]
, where
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}
and
q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}
,

(ii)

p\bullet q=\varepsilon[p,q]
if
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
.

(iii)

p\bullet q\bullet p=q\theta_{p}
.

Proof.

(i)
. By Lemma
7.15
(i)
we have
p\bullet q=p{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]=p^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]
.

(ii)
. This follows from part
(i)
, as
p=p^{\prime}
and
q=q^{\prime}
when
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
.

(iii)
. Here we have

\displaystyle p\bullet q\bullet p

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\bullet p

by part
(i)
, where
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}
and
q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime}]

by Lemma
7.15
(i)
, where
q^{\prime\prime}=p\theta_{q^{\prime}}
and
p^{\prime\prime}=q^{\prime}\theta_{p}
.

Using the projection algebra axioms, it is easy to show that in fact
p^{\prime\prime}=p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}=q^{\prime}
. Thus, continuing from above, we have

p\bullet q\bullet p=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime\prime}]=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},p^{\prime}]=\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},p^{\prime}]=p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}.\qed

Recall that the sets of idempotents and projections of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
are denoted

E(S)=\{e\in S:e^{2}=e\}\and P(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}.

Although these sets are not equal in general, Lemma
3.3
(ii)
says that they generate the same subsemigroup of
S
, i.e.
\langle E(S)\rangle=\langle P(S)\rangle
. We write
\mathbb{E}(S)
for this idempotent-generated subsemigroup. The results proved above allow us to give the following descriptions of
P(S)
,
E(S)
and
\mathbb{E}(S)
in the case that
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
arises from a chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
be a chained projection groupoid, and let
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. Then

(i)

P(S)=P
,

(ii)

E(S)=\{\varepsilon[p,q]:(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\}
,

(iii)

\mathbb{E}(S)=\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon)=\{\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}):\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}(P)\}
, and consequently
S
is idempotent-generated if and only if
\varepsilon
is surjective.

Proof.

(i)
. By Lemmas
3.3
(i)
and
7.3
we have

P(S)=\{a\bullet a^{*}:a\in S\}=\{a\circ a^{-1}:a\in\mathcal{G}\}=\{{\bf d}(a):a\in\mathcal{G}\}=v\mathcal{G}=P.

(ii)
. By Lemma
3.3
(ii)
, and part
(i)
, we have
E(S)=\{p\bullet q:p,q\in P\}
. The claim then follows from Lemma
7.16
.

(iii)
. If
\mathfrak{c}=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\in\mathscr{C}(P)
, then

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[p_{1},p_{2}]\circ\varepsilon[p_{2},p_{3}]\circ\cdots\circ\varepsilon[p_{k-1},p_{k}]

by (
6.21
)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[p_{1},p_{2}]\bullet\varepsilon[p_{2},p_{3}]\bullet\cdots\bullet\varepsilon[p_{k-1},p_{k}]

by Lemma
7.3

\displaystyle=(p_{1}\bullet p_{2})\bullet(p_{2}\bullet p_{3})\bullet\cdots\bullet(p_{k-1}\bullet p_{k})

by Lemma
7.16
(ii)

\displaystyle=p_{1}\bullet p_{2}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{k}\in\mathbb{E}(S).

(7.18)

Conversely, fix some
a\in\mathbb{E}(S)
, so that
a=p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{k}
for some
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P
. We show that
a\in\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon)
by induction on
k
. The
k=1
case being trivial, we assume that
k\geq 2
, and let
b=p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{k-1}
. By induction we have
b=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})
for some
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathscr{C}(P)
, and we write
r={\bf r}(b)={\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})
. Then with
r^{\prime}=p_{k}\theta_{r}
and
p_{k}^{\prime}=r\theta_{p_{k}}
, it follows from Lemma
7.15
(i)
and properties of
\varepsilon
from Definition
6.17
that

a=b\bullet p_{k}=b{\downharpoonright}_{r^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[r^{\prime},p_{k}^{\prime}]=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}){\downharpoonright}_{r^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[r^{\prime},p_{k}^{\prime}]=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{r^{\prime}})\circ\varepsilon[r^{\prime},p_{k}^{\prime}]=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{r^{\prime}}\circ[r^{\prime},p_{k}^{\prime}])\in\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon).\qed

Remark 7.19
.

We will see in Section
8
(see Proposition
8.4
) that every regular
*
-semigroup has the form
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
for some chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
. Combining this with Proposition
7.17
(iii)
, we obtain a proof of Proposition
3.16
, stated in Section
3.1
.

8
The category isomorphism

We are now ready to bring all of the above ideas together. By Theorems
6.42
and
7.14
, we have two functors

{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}\and{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS},

and we now wish to show that these are mutual inverses, thereby proving the following, which is the main result of the paper:

Theorem 8.1
.

The category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups (with
*
-semigroup homomorphisms) is isomorphic to the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids (with chained projection functors).

Proof.

The functors
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
both act identically on morphisms, which in each category are certain structure-preserving mappings on objects (regular
*
-semigroups or chained projection groupoids). It follows that
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
are mutually inverse at the level of morphisms. For objects, it remains to check that

{\bf G}({\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon))=(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\and{\bf S}({\bf G}(S))=S

for any chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, and any regular
*
-semigroup
S
. This is achieved in Propositions
8.2
and
8.4
below.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a chained projection groupoid, then
{\bf G}({\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon))=(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

Write

S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\and(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})={\bf G}(S)=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S)).

We must show that:

(i)

P^{\prime}=P
, meaning that the two projection algebras have the same underlying sets, and the same
\theta
operations,

(ii)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}
, meaning that the two groupoids have the same underlying (object and morphism) sets, and all the same operations and relations (domain/range maps, composition, inversion and order), and

(iii)

\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon
, meaning simply that the two maps are identical.

(i)
. This follows quickly from Proposition
7.17
(i)
and Lemma
7.16
(iii)
.

(ii)
. By construction, the underlying sets of
S
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}(S)
are simply
\mathcal{G}
, and the unary operations of
S
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are simply inversion in
\mathcal{G}
. The projection set of
S
is
P
(by Proposition
7.17
(i)
), and the object set of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}(S)
is
P(S)=P
(by construction).

Recall that the product
\bullet
in
S
is given by Definition
7.1
. The groupoid
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is constructed from
S
, as outlined in Definition
3.19
. To avoid ambiguity, we will denote the composition on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by
\circ^{\prime}
in order to distinguish it from the original composition
\circ
in
\mathcal{G}
. For the same reason, we also denote the domain and range maps in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by
{\bf d}^{\prime}
and
{\bf r}^{\prime}
. So for
a,b\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(=\mathcal{G})
, we have

{\bf d}^{\prime}(a)=a\bullet a^{-1},\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf r}^{\prime}(a)=a^{-1}\bullet a\and a\circ^{\prime}b=a\bullet b\text{ when ${\bf r}^{\prime}(a)={\bf d}^{\prime}(b)$.}

By Lemma
7.3
, and since
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(a^{-1})
, it follows that

{\bf d}^{\prime}(a)=a\bullet a^{-1}=a\circ a^{-1}={\bf d}(a)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf r}^{\prime}(a)={\bf r}(a).

(8.3)

It also follows that

{\bf r}^{\prime}(a)={\bf d}^{\prime}(b)\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ {\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and then}\hskip 20.00003pta\circ^{\prime}b=a\bullet b=a\circ b.

Thus, the compositions in the categories
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
coincide.

We now move on to the orders on
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. We use the symbols
{\downharpoonleft}
and
{\downharpoonright}
to denote restrictions in
\mathcal{G}
as usual, and
{\upharpoonleft}
and
{\upharpoonright}
for restrictions in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(note the placement of the arrow heads). Let
a\in\mathcal{G}
and write
q={\bf d}(a)
. By (
8.3
),
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
is defined in
\mathcal{G}
precisely when
{}_{p}{\upharpoonleft}a
is defined in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, i.e. when
p\leq q
. And for such
p
, it follows from Definition
3.19
and Lemma
7.15
(vi)
that

{}_{p}{\upharpoonleft}a=p\bullet a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a.

But then for
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
, and writing
\leq
and
\leq^{\prime}
for the orders in
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we have

a\leq b\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b\text{ for some $p\leq{\bf d}(b)$}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a={}_{p}{\upharpoonleft}b\text{ for some $p\leq{\bf d}^{\prime}(b)$}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ a\leq^{\prime}b.

(iii)
. If
\mathfrak{c}=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\in\mathscr{C}
, then Definition
6.27
and (
7.18
) give
\varepsilon^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c})=p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{k}=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})
.
∎

Proposition 8.4
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, then
{\bf S}({\bf G}(S))=S
.

Proof.

Write

(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)={\bf G}(S)=(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))\and S^{\prime}={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon).

Again, by construction the underlying sets of
S
and
S^{\prime}
coincide, and so do the involutions. It therefore remains to show that the products in
S
and
S^{\prime}
coincide as well. We denote the product in
S
by juxtaposition, as usual. The product in
S^{\prime}
is
\bullet
, which is constructed from
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
as in Definition
7.1
, while
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is in turn constructed from
S
as in Definition
6.30
.

So let
a,b\in S
; we must show that
a\bullet b=ab
. Write
p={\bf r}(a)
and
q={\bf d}(b)
, so that

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}.

Following Definition
3.19
, and using Lemma
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=a^{*}a,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=pqp,

\displaystyle a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=ap^{\prime}=apqp,

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=bb^{*},

\displaystyle q^{\prime}

\displaystyle=qpq,

\displaystyle{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b

\displaystyle=q^{\prime}b=qpqb,

\displaystyle\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]=p^{\prime}q^{\prime}=pqpqpq=pq.

Combining all of the above, and keeping Definition
3.19
and Lemma
3.3
in mind, it follows that

a\bullet b=apqp\circ pq\circ qpqb=apqpqpqb=apqb=aa^{*}abb^{*}b=ab,

as required.
∎

As noted above, this completes the proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Remark 8.5
.

We can use Theorem
8.1
to deduce a
structure theorem
for regular
*
-semigroups, in the sense that it shows how the entire structure of such a semigroup can be entirely, and uniquely, determined by ‘simpler’ objects. Specifically, given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, one constructs:

•

the projection algebra
P=P(S)
, as in Definition
4.3
,

•

the ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
, as in Definition
3.19
, and

•

the evaluation map
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S)
, as in Definition
6.27
.

Proposition
8.4
tells us that
S
is completely determined by these three simpler objects, in the sense that
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
can be constructed from
P
,
\mathcal{G}
and
\varepsilon
in the manner described in Definition
7.1
. Examples
3.33
and
3.39
demonstrate the subtlety of the decomposition, showing how small changes to
P
,
\mathcal{G}
or
\varepsilon
can result in very different semigroups.

Remark 8.6
.

Theorem
8.1
shows that the categories of regular
*
-semigroups and chained projection groupoids are in a sense the same. It also follows from the definition of the functors
{\bf S}
and
{\bf G}
that the algebras of projections of regular
*
-semigroups are precisely the object sets of chained projection groupoids. It follows from Proposition
4.26
(or indeed from results of
[
72
,
74
,
33
,
37
]
) that this is precisely the class of all (abstract) projection algebras.

Remark 8.7
.

It is again worth considering an example. Let
S=\mathcal{M}^{0}(P,P,G,M)
be a Rees 0-matrix regular
*
-semigroup, as in Example
3.39
, and let
(P_{0},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)={\bf G}(S)
be the associated chained projection groupoid. The product
a\bullet b
of two non-zero elements
a=(p,g,q)
and
b=(r,h,s)
of
S
is given by

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{q^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[q^{\prime},r^{\prime}]\circ{}_{r^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptq^{\prime}=r\theta_{q}\and r^{\prime}=q\theta_{r}.

By (
3.44
), we have

q^{\prime}=\begin{cases}q&\text{if $m_{qr}\not=0$}\\
0&\text{if $m_{qr}=0$}\end{cases}\and r^{\prime}=\begin{cases}r&\text{if $m_{qr}\not=0$}\\
0&\text{if $m_{qr}=0$.}\end{cases}

When
m_{qr}=0
, we have
a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{0}\circ\varepsilon[0,0]\circ{}_{0}{\downharpoonleft}b=0
. Otherwise, we use (
6.33
) to calculate

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{q}\circ\varepsilon[q,r]\circ{}_{r}{\downharpoonleft}b=(p,g,q)\circ(q,m_{qr},r)\circ(r,g,s)=(p,gm_{qr}h,s).

Thus, in both cases we have
a\bullet b=ab
, as expected.

9
Inverse semigroups and inductive groupoids

We have noted on a number of occasions that any inverse semigroup is a regular
*
-semigroup (with
a^{*}=a^{-1}
). In this final section, we look at how the theory developed above simplifies in the case of inverse semigroups. In particular, we will see that our results allow us to deduce the celebrated Ehresmann–Schein–Nambooripad (ESN) Theorem, stated below as Theorem
9.1
, and discussed in more detail in Section
1
.

In what follows, we write
{\bf IS}
for the category of inverse semigroups. Morphisms in
{\bf IS}
are simply the semigroup homomorphisms. (Any semigroup homomorphism between inverse semigroups automatically respects the involutions.) We also write
{\bf IG}
for the category of
inductive groupoids
, i.e. the ordered groupoids whose object set is a semilattice (under the order inherited from the containing groupoid). Morphisms in
{\bf IG}
are the
inductive functors
, i.e. the ordered groupoid functors whose object maps are semilattice morphisms. The ESN Theorem is as follows:

Theorem 9.1
.

The category
{\bf IS}
of inverse semigroups (with semigroup homomorphisms) is isomorphic to the category
{\bf IG}
of inductive groupoids (with inductive functors).

We prove this by
first identifying (in Section
9.1
) the image of the category
{\bf IS}
under the isomorphism
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
, and then
showing (in Section
9.2
) that this image is isomorphic to
{\bf IG}
.

9.1
The chained projection groupoid associated to an inverse semigroup

In Theorem
8.1
we showed that the functors

{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}\and{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}

are mutually inverse isomorphisms between

•

the category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups, with
*
-semigroup homomorphisms, and

•

the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids, with chained projection functors.

It follows that for any subcategory
\mathcal{C}
of
{\bf RSS}
, the functor
{\bf G}
restricts to an isomorphism from
\mathcal{C}
onto its image
{\bf G}(\mathcal{C})
in
{\bf CPG}
:

{\bf RSS}
\mathcal{C}
{\bf CPG}
{\bf G}(\mathcal{C})
{\bf G}
{\bf S}

In particular, the category
{\bf IS}
of inverse semigroups is isomorphic to its image
{\bf G}({\bf IS})
. The next result identifies this image.

Proposition 9.2
.

If
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
is a chained projection groupoid, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

q\theta_{p}=p\theta_{q}
for all
p,q\in P
,

(ii)

{\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}}={\leq}
,

(iii)

{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=\Delta_{P}
,

(iv)

\mathscr{C}(P)=P
(in which case
\varepsilon
is the inclusion
\varepsilon=\iota:P\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
),

(v)

\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon)=P
,

(vi)

(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)={\bf G}(S)
for some inverse semigroup
S
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. This is clear, upon inspecting (
4.4
) and (
4.11
).

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. We have
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}={\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}}\cap{\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}}={\leq}\cap{\geq}=\Delta_{P}
.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iv)
. The only
P
-paths have the form
(p,p,\ldots,p)\approx(p)\equiv p
.

(iv)
\ \Rightarrow\
(v)
. This is clear.

(v)
\ \Rightarrow\
(vi)
. By Proposition
8.2
we have
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)={\bf G}(S)
, for
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
as in Definition
7.1
. By Proposition
7.17
, and by assumption, we have
{E(S)\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\varepsilon)=P=P(S)}
. It then follows that
E(S)=P(S)
, so
S
is inverse by Lemma
3.9
.

(vi)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. This follows from Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

At the outset, one might have expected the following condition to be included in the proposition:

•

\mathcal{G}
is inductive (i.e.,
(P,{\leq})
is a semilattice).

However, as in Remark
3.10
, this condition is necessary, but not sufficient, for
S
to be inverse; see also Examples
3.33
and
3.39
.

Definition 9.4
.

We call a chained projection groupoid
trivial
if it satisfies any (and hence all) of the conditions of Proposition
9.2
. We denote by
{\bf TCPG}
the full subcategory of
{\bf CPG}
consisting of all trivial chained projection groupoids (and all chained projection functors between them).

9.2
The Ehresmann–Schein–Nambooripad Theorem

Theorem
8.1
and Proposition
9.2
show that the categories
{\bf IS}
(of inverse semigroups) and
{\bf TCPG}
(of trivial chained projection groupoids) are isomorphic. Thus, we can complete the proof of the ESN Theorem by showing that the category
{\bf IG}
of inductive groupoids is also isomorphic to
{\bf TCPG}
. We do this by constructing a pair of mutually inverse functors

{\bf I}:{\bf TCPG}\to{\bf IG}\and{\bf T}:{\bf IG}\to{\bf TCPG}.

The first will simply be a forgetful functor:

•

On objects,
{\bf I}(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)=\mathcal{G}
.

•

On morphisms,
{\bf I}(\phi)=\phi
.

Proposition 9.5
.

{\bf I}
is a functor
{\bf TCPG}\to{\bf IG}
.

Proof.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)
be a trivial chained projection groupoid. Comparing Proposition
9.2
(i)
with Lemma
4.7
, it is clear that
P=v\mathcal{G}
is a
\wedge
-semilattice, so
\mathcal{G}
is inductive. It remains to check that any chained projection functor
\phi:(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
in
{\bf TCPG}
is an inductive functor
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. By definition,
\phi
is an ordered groupoid functor
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and
v\phi
is a projection algebra morphism
P\to P^{\prime}
. Again keeping Proposition
9.2
(i)
and Lemma
4.7
in mind, it follows that

(p\wedge q)\phi=(q\theta_{p})\phi=(q\phi)\theta_{p\phi}=p\phi\wedge q\phi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $p,q\in P$.}\qed

The definition of the functor
{\bf T}:{\bf IG}\to{\bf TCPG}
is relatively straightforward as well, although there are a few details to verify before we can give it.

Lemma 9.6
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is an inductive groupoid, with object semilattice
v\mathcal{G}=P
, then

(i)

P
is a projection algebra with respect to the maps

\theta_{p}:P\to P\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003ptq\theta_{p}=p\wedge q\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p,q\in P$,}

(ii)

(P,\mathcal{G})
is a projection groupoid.

Proof.

(i)
. It is a routine matter to check that axioms
(P1)
–
(P5)
hold.

(ii)
. It is easy to see that
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a
weak
projection groupoid; indeed, this follows quickly from the construction of the projection algebra operations, and the fact that
p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=p\wedge q
in a
\wedge
-semilattice. It remains to verify
(G1)
, and for this we check
(G1d)
. To do so, fix some
a\in\mathcal{G}
, and write
p={\bf d}(a)
and
q={\bf r}(a)
. We must show that
\vartheta_{a}:p^{\downarrow}\to q^{\downarrow}
is a projection algebra morphism, i.e. that

\displaystyle(s\theta_{t})\vartheta_{a}

\displaystyle=(s\vartheta_{a})\theta_{t\vartheta_{a}}

for all
s,t\leq p
.

By the definition of the
\theta
maps, this amounts to showing that

\displaystyle(s\wedge t)\vartheta_{a}

\displaystyle=s\vartheta_{a}\wedge t\vartheta_{a}

for all
s,t\leq p
,

i.e. that
\vartheta_{a}
is a semilattice morphism. But this follows quickly from basic order-theoretic facts:

•

p^{\downarrow}
and
q^{\downarrow}
are both
\wedge
-semilattices, as order ideals of the
\wedge
-semilattice
P
, and

•

\vartheta_{a}:p^{\downarrow}\to q^{\downarrow}
and its inverse
\vartheta_{a}^{-1}=\vartheta_{a^{-1}}:q^{\downarrow}\to p^{\downarrow}
are both order-preserving, by Lemma
2.12
.

It follows that
\vartheta_{a}
preserves meets.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is an inductive groupoid, with object semilattice
v\mathcal{G}=P
, then

(i)

\mathscr{C}(P)=P
, where the projection algebra structure on
P
is as in Lemma
9.6
,

(ii)

(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)
is a (trivial) chained projection groupoid, where
\iota:P\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
is the inclusion.

Proof.

(i)
. For any
p,q\in P
we have
q\theta_{p}=p\wedge q=q\wedge p=p\theta_{q}
. As in the proof of Proposition
9.2
, this implies
{\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}}={\leq}
, and then
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}=\Delta_{P}
, from which the claim follows.

(ii)
. By Lemma
9.6
(ii)
, and since
\iota:P\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
is clearly an ordered
v
-functor, it remains to check that
(G2)
holds, and this is essentially trivial. Indeed, consider some
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
, where
b\in\mathcal{G}
, and let
e_{1},e_{2},f_{1},f_{2}\in P
be as in (
6.37
). Since
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=\Delta_{P}
, it follows from Lemma
6.36
(iii)
that in fact
e_{1}=e_{2}=e
and
f_{1}=f_{2}=f
. But then

\lambda(e,b,f)=\iota[e,e]\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\iota[f,f]=e\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ f={}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\rho(e,b,f)={}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b.\qed

We can now define the functor
{\bf T}:{\bf IG}\to{\bf TCPG}
:

•

On objects,
{\bf T}(\mathcal{G})=(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)
is the trivial chained projection groupoid from Lemma
9.7
.

•

On morphisms,
{\bf T}(\phi)=\phi
.

Proposition 9.8
.

{\bf T}
is a functor
{\bf IG}\to{\bf TCPG}
.

Proof.

It remains to check that any inductive functor
\phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a chained projection functor
(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})
, where
(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)={\bf T}(\mathcal{G})
and
(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})={\bf T}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
.
Following Definition
6.20
, we need to show that:

(i)

\phi
is a projection groupoid morphism, and

(ii)

\phi
respects the evaluation maps.

(i)
. Since
\phi
is an ordered groupoid functor by assumption, we just need to check that
v\phi
is a projection algebra morphism
{P\to P^{\prime}}
.
This follows from the fact that
v\phi
is a semilattice morphism (as
\phi
is inductive), and keeping the rule
q\theta_{p}=p\wedge q
in mind.

(ii)
. Since
\mathscr{C}(P)=P
and
\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})=P^{\prime}
, the functor
\Phi=\mathscr{C}(v\phi)
at the top of the diagram in Definition
6.20
is just the object map
\Phi=v\phi
. The diagram then becomes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

and this obviously commutes.
∎

Now that we have defined the two functors
{\bf I}
and
{\bf T}
, it is completely routine to check that they are mutually inverse. As noted above, this completes the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Remark 9.9
.

Restricting the isomorphisms
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
and
{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
as appropriate, we obtain isomorphisms

{\bf G}^{\prime}:{\bf IS}\to{\bf TCPG}\and{\bf S}^{\prime}:{\bf TCPG}\to{\bf IS},

as shown in Figure
17
. Composing these with
{\bf I}
and
{\bf T}
, we therefore obtain isomorphisms

\mathbb{G}={\bf I}\circ{\bf G}^{\prime}:{\bf IS}\to{\bf IG}\and\mathbb{S}={\bf S}^{\prime}\circ{\bf T}:{\bf IG}\to{\bf IS}.

The functors
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{S}
both act identically on morphisms. At the object level, we have

\mathbb{G}(S)={\bf I}(P(S),\mathcal{G}(S),\varepsilon(S))=\mathcal{G}(S)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for an inverse semigroup $S$,}

so that
\mathbb{G}
is simply the restriction to
{\bf IS}\subseteq{\bf RSS}
of the functor
\mathcal{G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf OG}
(cf. Definition
3.19
and Proposition
3.27
).
On the other hand,
\mathbb{S}
maps an inductive groupoid
\mathcal{G}
to the inverse semigroup

S=\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{G})={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\iota),

where
(P,\mathcal{G},\iota)={\bf T}(\mathcal{G})
is the trivial chained projection groupoid from Lemma
9.7
(ii)
. Since
S
is inverse, the product
\bullet
given in Definition
7.1
takes on a simpler form here. Indeed, let
a,b\in S(\equiv\mathcal{G})
, write
p={\bf r}(a)
and
q={\bf d}(b)
, and also let
e=p\wedge q
. Examining Proposition
9.2
(i)
and Lemma
4.7
, we note that
e=p^{\prime}=q^{\prime}
, where these projections are as in Definition
7.1
. It then follows that

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ\iota[e,e]\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ e\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b.

In this way, we see that the functors
\mathbb{G}={\bf I}\circ{\bf G}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{S}={\bf S}^{\prime}\circ{\bf T}
are precisely those used in the canonical proof of the ESN Theorem; see
[
88
, Chapter 4]
.

{\bf RSS}
{\bf IS}
{\bf CPG}
{\bf TCPG}
{\bf IG}
{\bf G}^{\prime}
{\bf S}^{\prime}
{\bf I}
{\bf T}

Figure 17:
The categories and isomorphisms discussed in Remark
9.9
.

Remark 9.10
.

Alternatively, one could also obtain a direct (and quite transparent) proof of the ESN Theorem by specialising our proof of Theorem
8.1
to the inverse case. We will not give the full outline of this, but will comment briefly on the most involved step, which is to show that the product
\bullet
defined on the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is associative. As in the previous remark, this product reduces (in the inductive/inverse case) to

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{e}\circ{}_{e}{\downharpoonleft}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a,b\in\mathcal{G}$, where $e={\bf r}(a)\wedge{\bf d}(b)$.}

It follows that for
a,b,c\in\mathcal{G}
, we have

(a\bullet b)\bullet c=a{\downharpoonright}_{f}\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{g}\circ c{\downharpoonright}_{h}\and a\bullet(b\bullet c)=a{\downharpoonright}_{f^{\prime}}\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{g^{\prime}}\circ c{\downharpoonright}_{h^{\prime}}

for some
f,g,h,f^{\prime},g^{\prime},h^{\prime}\in P=v\mathcal{G}
. We can then show that
(a\bullet b)\bullet c
and
a\bullet(b\bullet c)
are equal by showing that they have equal domains and equal ranges. This follows from (
7.7
) and (
7.8
), which were proved quite early in Section
7.1
.

Remark 9.11
.

Our proof of the ESN Theorem involved showing that two categories of categorical structures (
{\bf IG}
and
{\bf TCPG}
) were isomorphic. This bears some resemblance to the approach of
[
7
]
, which gives a direct proof of the equivalence of Nambooripad’s inductive groupoids
[
114
]
and cross-connections
[
116
]
.

10
Conclusion and discussion of future work

The main result of this paper, Theorem
8.1
, states that the category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups (with
*
-semigroup homomorphisms) is isomorphic to the category
{\bf CPG}
of chained projection groupoids (with chained projection functors). At the object level, the functor
{\bf G}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf CPG}
maps a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to
{\bf G}(S)=(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, where

•

P=P(S)
is the projection algebra of
S
, a classical structure that goes back to Imaoka
[
72
]
,

•

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(S)
is the ordered groupoid associated to
S
, constructed in entirely the same way as inductive groupoids are from inverse semigroups
[
88
]
, and

•

\varepsilon=\varepsilon(S)
is a functor from the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}(P)
to
\mathcal{G}
, which provides the key link between the structures of
P
and
\mathcal{G}
.

As explained in Remark
8.5
, the existence of the inverse functor
{\bf S}={\bf G}^{-1}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
means that the structure of
S
is entirely determined by that of these smaller and simpler parts, in the sense that
S={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
can be reconstructed directly from them.
This has a number of important consequences, and leads to a number of interesting applications that will be the subject of future articles
[
33
,
37
]
, and which we will briefly discuss here.

10.1
Fundamental regular
*
-semigroups

One of the important themes in early papers in the area was the classification of
fundamental
regular
*
-semigroups, i.e. those with no non-trivial idempotent-separating congruences. See for example the papers of Imaoka and Yamada
[
155
,
70
,
72
]
, and the more recent work of Jones
[
74
]
. Some of these papers drew inspiration from Munn’s classical construction of fundamental
inverse
semigroups
[
113
]
, which were built from order-isomorphisms between principal ideals of a semilattice. The need for a regular
*
-analogue of a semilattice eventually led to the definition of (what we call) projection algebras in
[
72
]
.

In the sequel
[
33
]
, we will use our groupoid approach to provide what we believe is a more transparent analysis of fundamental regular
*
-semigroups. For one thing, we can use our
\vartheta
and
\Theta
maps to describe the maximum idempotent-separating congruence
\mu_{S}
of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
,
viz.
:

\mu_{S}=\{(a,b)\in S\times S:\vartheta_{a}=\vartheta_{b}\}=\{(a,b)\in S\times S:\Theta_{a}=\Theta_{b}\text{ and }\Theta_{a^{*}}=\Theta_{b^{*}}\}.

We can also construct a maximum fundamental regular
*
-semigroup
\mathcal{M}_{P}
with a given projection algebra
P
by first building an appropriate chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{M},\varepsilon)
, and then defining
\mathcal{M}_{P}={\bf S}(P,\mathcal{M},\varepsilon)
. As with some previous studies (see especially
[
74
, Section 4]
), our construction involves projection algebra isomorphisms
p^{\downarrow}\to q^{\downarrow}
between down-sets in
P
. The operations in
\mathcal{M}
are simply function composition, inversion and restriction. The fundamental semigroups constructed in the literature
[
155
,
70
,
72
,
74
]
all have somewhat-complicated products, but these are all easily understood as special instances of the
\bullet
product from Definition
7.1
. The morphisms
\varepsilon[p,q]
featuring in such
\bullet
products have the form
\varepsilon[p,q]=\theta_{q}|_{p^{\downarrow}}
.

Given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, with
P=P(S)
, each
\vartheta_{a}
(
a\in S
) is a projection algebra isomorphism
{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}
(cf.
(G1d)
), so we have a well-defined map
\phi:S\to\mathcal{M}_{P}:a\mapsto\vartheta_{a}
. One of the main results of
[
33
]
is that
\phi
is a
*
-semigroup homomorphism with
\ker(\phi)=\mu_{S}
, and whose image
\operatorname{im}(\phi)\cong S/\mu_{S}
is the fundamental image of
S
.

Specialising to diagram monoids (cf. Section
3.4
), we will show in
[
33
]
that when
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{n})
is the projection algebra of a finite partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the maximum fundamental regular
*
-semigroup
\mathcal{M}_{P}
is (isomophic to)
\mathcal{P}_{n}
itself. This is achieved by continuing the combinatorial analysis of the projection algebra
P(\mathcal{P}_{n})
initiated in previous works such as
[
35
]
.

10.2
Free (idempotent- and projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroups

In Section
4.2
we showed that the construction
S\mapsto P(S)
of the projection algebra of a regular
*
-semigroup is the object part of a functor
P:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
. It is natural to think of
P
as a
forgetful functor
. Indeed, identifying
{\bf RSS}
with
{\bf CPG}
(via the isomorphism from Theorem
8.1
), we can think of
P
as a functor
{\bf CPG}\to{\bf PA}
, under which
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\mapsto P
.
One of the main results of the sequel
[
37
]
is the following theorem:

•

The forgetful functor
P:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
has a
left adjoint
.

At the object level, the adjoint
\mathcal{F}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
maps a projection algebra
P
to what we will call the
chain semigroup

\mathscr{C}_{P}
. This semigroup is constructed as
\mathscr{C}_{P}={\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\varepsilon)
, where
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a certain quotient of the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
, and
\varepsilon
is the canonical quotient map. (The definition of
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is very natural, but too involved to give here. Suffice it to say that it involves the concept of
linked pairs
in
P
, which are regular
*
-analogues of Nambooripad’s
singular squares
in biordered sets
[
114
]
.) There are two main consequences of the above theorem.

First, since the image of
\mathcal{F}
turns out to be a full subcategory of
{\bf RSS}
, we can identify
{\bf PA}
with a
coreflective
subcategory of
{\bf RSS}
. This has an analogue in inverse semigroups, where it can be seen that the category of semilattices is coreflective in
{\bf IS}
. We are unaware of a statement of this fact in the literature (but we note that
[
88
, Theorem 2.4.2]
says that the category of groups is
reflective
in
{\bf IS}
). This lends extra support to the claim that projection algebras are the appropriate regular
*
-analogue of semilattices.

The second main consequence of the above theorem is that the semigroups
\mathscr{C}_{P}
are therefore the
P
-free objects
in the category
{\bf RSS}
.
6
6

6

This notion of free-ness is different from the
varietal
sense; free regular
*
-semigroups with respect to the forgetful functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf Set}
are studied in
[
123
]
. We note, however, that the left adjoint
{\bf Set}\to{\bf RSS}
to
this
forgetful functor does not map onto a full subcategory of
{\bf RSS}
, so that
{\bf Set}
is not (isomorphic to) a coreflective subcategory of
{\bf RSS}
. (This is analogous to other varietal free constructions. For example, there exist morphisms
X^{+}\to Y^{+}
between free semigroups that do not arise from set maps
X\to Y
.)

This conveys a lot of deep information, which we will not fully unpack here (see
[
37
]
for details), but one of the main points is the following theorem:

•

If
P
is a projection algebra and
S
a regular
*
-semigroup, then for any projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P(S)
there is a unique
*
-semigroup homomorphism
\Phi:\mathscr{C}_{P}\to S
such that the following diagram commutes (where both ‘
\iota
’s denote inclusion maps):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(S)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{P}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

As we will explain in
[
37
]
, the semigroups
\mathscr{C}_{P}
are the regular
*
-analogues of the free (idempotent-generated) semigroups
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
associated to a biordered set
E
, studied by Nambooripad, Easdown and others
[
114
,
117
,
29
,
55
,
56
,
26
,
25
]
. The semigroup
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
can be defined in terms of a presentation by generators and relations:

\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)=\langle X_{E}:x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\text{ for }(e,f)\in\mathbb{B}\rangle,

where here

X_{E}=\{x_{e}:e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E
, and

\mathbb{B}
is the set of all
basic pairs
in
E
, i.e. all pairs
(e,f)\in E\times E
for which
\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\not=\varnothing
.
One of the main results in
[
37
]
is a presentation for the chain semigroup
\mathscr{C}_{P}
:

\mathscr{C}_{P}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{PG}}(P)=\langle X_{P}:x_{p}^{2}=x_{p},\ (x_{p}x_{q})^{2}=x_{p}x_{q},\ x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{q\theta_{p}}\text{ for }p,q\in P\rangle,

where again

X_{P}=\{x_{p}:p\in P\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
P
.

Another result of
[
37
]
shows that a projection algebra
P
uniquely determines a biordered set
E=E(P)
, in the sense that any regular
*
-semigroup
S
with projection algebra
P(S)=P
also has biordered set
E(S)\cong E
. The chain semigroup
\mathscr{C}_{P}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{PG}}(P)
is then exhibited
as a natural quotient of
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
,
viz.
:

\mathscr{C}_{P}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}^{*}(E)=\langle X_{E}:x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\text{ for }(e,f)\in\mathbb{B}\cup(P\times P)\rangle.

A natural family of questions therefore arises:

•

Given an important regular
*
-semigroup
S
, with projection algebra
P=P(S)
and biordered set
E=E(S)
, how are the structures of
\mathscr{C}_{P}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{PG}}(P)\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}^{*}(E)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
related, and how are these related to
S
itself?

We will show in
[
37
]
that when
P=P(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
is the projection algebra of a (finite)
Temperley-Lieb monoid
,
\mathscr{C}_{P}
is (isomorphic to)
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
itself.

The situation for partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is rather more complicated, and will be treated in a separate paper
[
36
]
, where we will show that when
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{n})
and
E=E(\mathcal{P}_{n})
:

•

the maximal subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{PG}}(P)\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}^{*}(E)
corresponding to an idempotent of rank
r\leq n-2
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{r}
, the symmetric group of degree
r
, and

•

the maximal subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
corresponding to an idempotent of rank
r\leq n-2
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
, where
\mathbb{Z}
is the infinite cyclic group.

Higher rank subgroups are free (of calculable degrees). These results naturally complement existing studies of maximal subgroups in other families of monoids, such as transformation and linear monoids
[
14
,
56
,
25
,
157
,
21
]
. As we explain in
[
36
]
, the additional free factor in the subgroups
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{S}_{r}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{IG}}(E)
comes from the fact that the biordered set
E=E(\mathcal{P}_{n})
is isomorphic to that of a certain ‘twisted’ monoid mapping onto
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
The above results are obtained by utilising a general (group) presentation for a maximal subgroup of an arbitrary free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup, obtained in
[
37
]
via an application of the Reidemeister–Schreier rewriting machinery from
[
131
]
.

10.3
Open problems

Throughout the text we have mentioned a number of open problems. For convenience, we list these here, as well as some others:

•

To what extent is a projection algebra determined by its associated relations (
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
)?

•

Is every group (isomorphic to) a maximal subgroup of some free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroup (cf.
[
27
,
55
]
)?

•

Does the groupoid-approach developed here have consequences for the representation theory of regular
*
-semigroups (cf.
[
139
,
140
,
138
,
133
,
134
,
135
,
136
,
100
,
137
]
)?

•

Can the theory developed above be applied to other categories of ‘projection-based’ semigroups (cf.
[
74
,
151
]
)?

•

Is there a parallel theory for (non-projection-based) generalisations of regular
*
-semigroups, e.g. for involution semigroups satisfying
aa^{*}aa^{*}=aa^{*}
in place of
aa^{*}a=a
?

•

Does the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
lead to an efficient algorithm for enumerating finite regular
*
-semigroups (cf.
[
98
]
)?

•

Can one develop a symmetrical theory of cross-connections for regular *-semigroups (cf.
[
7
,
6
,
116
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
]
)?
